height=7.89447pt,width=0.4pt}\kern-0.2pt\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-5.1007pt\kern-5.1007pt\hbox{$\textstyle{x_{1}}$}\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-0.2pt\raise 6.30554pt\hbox{\vrule height=11.49448pt,width=0.4pt}\kern-0.2pt\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-5.1007pt\kern-5.1007pt\hbox{$\textstyle{x_{2}}$}\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-0.2pt\raise 6.30554pt\hbox{\vrule height=11.49448pt,width=0.4pt}\kern-0.2pt\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-5.1007pt\kern-5.1007pt\hbox{$\textstyle{x_{3}}$}\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-0.2pt\raise 6.30554pt\hbox{\vrule height=7.89447pt,width=0.4pt}\kern-0.2pt\vbox{\halign{#\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\kern 3.2pt\cr\vphantom{\leaders{\hrule}{\hfill}\kern 0.0pt}\crcr\cr$\hfil\textstyle{\vbox to14.0pt{}\hskip 5.1007pt}\hfil$\crcr}}\kern-5.1007pt\kern-5.1007pt\hbox{$\textstyle{x_{4}}$}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{1}x_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{3}x_{4}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{1}x_{3}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{2}x_{4}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{1}x_{4}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{2}x_{3}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{12}^{-1}\,A_{34}^{-1}+A_{13}^{-1}\,A_{24}^{-1}+A_{14}^{-1}\,A_{23}^{-1}.

(103)

To illustrate the appearance of a combinatorial factor, we may imagine
the example that all
x_{1}=x_{2}=x_{3}=x_{4}=x
in the previous
example are identical. We see from (
103
)
by setting all indices to the same value that we get the same term
three times in this case, namely

\displaystyle\langle x^{4}\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 3\,\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}^{2}.

4
Appendix: Self-adjoint operators

We denote as
(x,y)
a scalar product. We may think of the Euclidean
scalar product
(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}y_{i}
as a concrete example.
The condition for symmetry of
A
can more accurately be stated as
the operator
A
being self-adjoint. In general, the adjoint operator
is defined with regard to a scalar product
(\cdot,\cdot)
as

\displaystyle(x,A\,y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def. adjoint}}}{{=:}}(A^{\mathrm{T}}\,x,y)\,\quad\forall x,y.

An operator is self-adjoint, if
A^{\mathrm{T}}=A.

If a matrix
A
is self-adjoint with respect to the Euclidean scalar
product
(\cdot,\cdot)
, its diagonalizing matrix
U
has orthogonal
column vectors with respect to the same scalar product, because from
the general form of a basis change into the eigenbasis
\mathrm{diag}(\{\lambda_{i}\})=U^{-1}\,A\,U

follows that
(U^{-1\mathrm{T}},A\,U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def. of adjoint}}}{{=}}(A^{\mathrm{T}}\,U^{-1\mathrm{T}},U)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{symm. of }(\cdot,\cdot)}}{{=}}(U,A^{\mathrm{T}}\,U^{-1\mathrm{T}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle A\text{ self. adj.}}}{{=}}(U,A\,U^{-1\mathrm{T}})
.
So the column vectors of
U^{-1T}
need to be parallel to the eigenvectors
of
A
, which are the column vectors of
U
, because eigenvectors
are unique up to normalization. If we assume them normalized we hence
have
U^{-1\mathrm{T}}=U
or
U^{-1}=U^{\mathrm{T}}
. It follows that
(Uv,Uw)=(v,U^{\mathrm{T}}U\,w)=(v,w)
,
the condition for the matrix
U
to be unitary with respect to
(\cdot,\cdot)
,
meaning its transformation conserves the scalar product.

5
Appendix: Normalization of a Gaussian

The equivalence between positivity and all eigenvalues being positive
follows from diagonalizing
A
by an orthogonal transform
U

\displaystyle\mathrm{diag}(\{\lambda_{i}\})

\displaystyle=U^{\mathrm{T}}\,A\,U,

where the columns of
U
are the eigenvectors of
A
(see
Section
4

for details). The determinant of the orthogonal transform, due to

U^{-1}=U^{\mathrm{T}}
is
|\det(U)|=1
, because
1=\det(\mathbf{1})=\det(U^{\mathrm{T}}U)=\det(U)^{2}
.
The orthogonal transform therefore does not affect the integration
measure. In the coordinate system of eigenvectors
v
we can then
rewrite the normalization integral as

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Pi_{i}dx_{i}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}x^{\mathrm{T}}Ax\Big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle x=U\,v}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Pi_{k}dv_{k}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}v^{\mathrm{T}}U^{\mathrm{T}}AUv\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\Pi_{k}dv_{k}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}v_{i}^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Pi_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2\pi}{\lambda_{k}}}=(2\pi)^{\frac{N}{2}}\det(A)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

where we used in the last step that the determinant of a matrix equals
the product of its eigenvalues.

6
Keypoints

In summary:

Gaussian distribution and Wick’s theorem

•

Gaussian distributions are characterized by only two cumulants: Mean
and variance (mean vector and covariance matrix for non-scalar Gaussian
variables). The CGF is a polynomial of degree 2 in the source terms

j
.

•

The moments of a centered Gaussian can be obtained from the variance
using Wick’s theorem, which considers all possible pairings.

7
Exercises

a) Cumulants

Calculate the moment generating function and the cumulant generating
function for

1.

the Gaussian distribution
p(x)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}\sigma}\,e^{-\frac{(x-\mu)^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}}
;
determine all cumulants of the distribution; (2 points)

2.

the binary distribution
p(x)=(1-m)\,\delta(x)+m\,\delta(x-1)
with
mean
m\in[0,1]
; determine the first three cumulants expressed in

m
, verify that the first two correspond to the mean and the variance;
(2 points). Convince yourself that the link between moments and cumulants
holds on the example of the third moment, by once computing it directly
and once from the first three cumulants.

b) Joint, marginal and conditional probability distribution

Consider the joint Gaussian distribution of two random variables
x,y\in\mathbb{R}

p(x_{1},x_{2})=\frac{1}{2\pi\sqrt{\det\left(\Sigma\right)}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}-\mu_{1}\\
x_{2}-\mu_{2}\end{array}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\Sigma^{-1}\left(\begin{array}[]{c}x_{1}-\mu_{1}\\
x_{2}-\mu_{2}\end{array}\right)\right)

with
\Sigma=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\
\Sigma_{12}&\Sigma_{22}\end{array}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 2}
. State the cumulant generating function and show that it decomposes
into a sum for the hypothetical scenario of
\Sigma_{12}=0
(
x_{1}

and
x_{2}
are independent)

W_{x_{1},x_{2}}(\tilde{j}_{1},\tilde{j}_{2})=W_{x_{1}}(\tilde{j}_{1})+W_{x_{2}}(\tilde{j}_{2}).

Now let us keep
\Sigma_{12}\neq 0
in the following so that the last
decomposition does not hold. Compute the marginal distribution
p(x_{1})=\int dx_{2}\,p(x_{1},x_{2})
.
We now want to compute the conditional probability
p(x_{1}|x_{2})=p(x_{1},x_{2})/p(x_{2})
.
In this formula, the denominator
p(x_{2})
only serves as the correct
normalization; we will first ignore it and fix the normalization in
the end. Use that we can write the probability distribution in its
Fourier representation

p(x_{1},x_{2})=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{1}}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{2}}{2\pi i}\,\exp\left(-\tilde{j}_{1}x_{1}-\tilde{j}_{2}x_{2}+W_{x_{1},x_{2}}(\tilde{j}_{1},\tilde{j}_{2})\right).

To compute the conditional probability distribution most easily, isolate
the
x_{1}
dependency by integrating out
\tilde{j}_{2}
and rewrite

\displaystyle p(x_{1}|x_{2})\propto p(x_{1},x_{2})

\displaystyle=\underbrace{\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{1}}{2\pi i}\,\exp\left(-\tilde{j}_{1}x_{1}+W_{x_{1}}(\tilde{j}_{1})\right)}_{\text{independent of $\tilde{j}_{2}$}}\times\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{2}}{2\pi i}\,\dots,

\displaystyle=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{1}}{2\pi i}\,\exp\left(-\tilde{j}_{1}x_{1}+W_{x_{1}|x_{2}}(\tilde{j}_{1})\right)

with a new cumulant generating function
W_{x_{1}|x_{2}}(\tilde{j}_{1})
.
The Gaussian identity
\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{j}_{2}}{i}\,\exp\left(a\tilde{j}_{2}+\frac{b}{2}\tilde{j}_{2}^{2}\right)\propto\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}a^{2}/b\right)

may be useful in your calculations. Read off the cumulants
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(x_{1}|x_{2})^{k}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

from the cumulant generating function. How does this distribution
change if we had a larger uncertainty
\Sigma\to c\,\Sigma
with

c\in\mathbb{R}
?

c) Pair of coupled spins

Let us assume we have a pair of coupled Ising spins
s_{1,2}\in\{-1,1\}
.
Compute the partition function

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}(j_{1},j_{2},J)

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}=-1}^{1}\,\exp(Js_{1}s_{2}+j_{1}s_{1}+j_{2}s_{2}),

Show that it holds that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\mathcal{Z}}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{Z}}{\partial j_{1}\partial j_{2}}

and that for
W=\ln\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial W}{\partial J}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial^{2}W}{\partial j_{1}\partial j_{2}}+\frac{\partial W}{\partial j_{1}}\,\frac{\partial W}{\partial j_{2}}.

Determine the first and second cumulants of the system for
j_{1}=j_{2}=h
.
Show that the second cumulant
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}s_{1}s_{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}
vanishes
in the case the that coupling
J=0
vanishes between the spins.

Show that we may also obtain the same result by considering a pair
of spins
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
within a Gaussian fluctuating field

h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,J)
, so

\displaystyle\mathcal{Z}

\displaystyle=\sum_{s_{1},s_{2}=-1}^{1}\,\left\langle\exp\big((s_{1}+s_{2})\,h+j_{1}s_{1}+j_{2}s_{2}\big)\right\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,J)}.

(104)

Chapter 3
Linear regression as Bayesian inference

The fundamental idea of machine learning, adapting parameters to
model and input-output relationship, goes back to ordinary linear
regression. Instead of dealing with a multitude of different non-linear
activation functions, network layers and parameters, we here first
focus on the problem of a linear setup, where the trainable parameters
consist of a single weight vector. Here we will reformulate the idea
of parameter selection from the point of view of Bayesian inference,
which will provide the starting point to analyze properties of shallow,
deep, and recurrent networks and will give an intuition on how to
relate classical approaches such as Maximum likelihood parameter estimations
to Bayesian statistics.

1
Basics of supervised learning and generalization

In machine learning we typically consider different settings where
learning takes place. Those settings usually depend on the task at
hand; all of which require input data sets, that we denote with
X
.
Single examples of this input data, e.g. a single image or measurement
point, are denoted in this manuscript using lowercase letters and
Greek indices
x_{\alpha}
.

In an
unsupervised setting
, we work solely with the input
data
X
and without any additional information. A classical example
of this task setting is
clustering
, where we want to identify
different groups in the set of input data
X
. This is prominently
employed in natural language processing, where you might want to identify
different thematic clusters in texts. Another example might be the
analysis of clusters of participants in social networks. Classical
cluster algorithms are:
k
-means clustering, the principle component
analysis (PCA) or the Tf-idf score for natural language processing.

Here we will not go deeper into these aspects of learning but we rather
focus on learning in a
supervised setting
. In addition to
input data we here have a corresponding set of labels or, more generally,
desired outputs
Y
. The labels can be a set of finite and discrete
values or continuous numbers. This supervised setting is closer to
what the reader might have already encountered so far in their studies:
The problem of regression, where you want to obtain a model of the
input-output relation
y(x)
of a system, is a classical example
of supervised learning. You provide both the input samples
x_{\alpha}

and the observed outputs
y_{\alpha}
for some observations and want
to gain some knowledge of the relation between the two. During the
course of this lecture we focus on linear regression, Bayesian inference
and neural networks; all of which deal with learning in supervised
settings.

From a statistical viewpoint, the problem of supervised learning can
be formulated as follows:

•

A core assumption is that the inputs
x
and the outputs
y
are
following a joint distribution
p(x,y)
; typically this distribution
is not known, but rather one has a set of
P
tuples
\mathcal{D}:=\{(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}

drawn from this distribution. Note that this distribution does not
need to be stochastic but can also be deterministic and purely given
through empirical data.

•

The task is to learn this joint distribution from the given set of

P
tuples.

•

Most of the time this is done with help of a
hypothesis class

\phi_{\Theta}(x)
; this is a set of functions of the input
x

that depend on a set of parameters
\Theta
; in this lecture the
set of functions will be neuronal networks, which, for each provided
input
x
produce an output
\phi_{\Theta}(x)
that depends on the
parameters
\Theta
, which are here typically the connections between
the neurons (weights) and other parameters, such as biases.

•

The intermediate goal of learning is to adapt the parameters
\Theta

such that the output
\phi_{\Theta}(x_{\alpha})
matches the desired
output
y_{\alpha}
for all
\alpha
; defining what “matches”
means translates to defining a measure of distance between the obtained
output and the desired one, often called a
loss function

\mathcal{L}(y;\phi_{\Theta}(X))
. The result of this step often is a single
value
\hat{\Theta}
for all parameters (e.g., for standard training
of networks) or a distribution of such parameters in the case of Bayesian
inference (see below).

•

The ultimate goal of learning is to use the function
\phi_{\Theta}(x_{\alpha})

to make
predictions
y_{\ast}=\phi_{\Theta}(x_{\ast})
for
the value
y_{\ast}
that corresponds to a hitherto unseen input

x_{\ast}
; such a task is denoted as generalization. In-distribution
generalization assumes that also the test input
x_{\ast}
and the
test output
y_{\ast}
follow the
same distribution

p(x_{\ast},y_{\ast})

that has been used to generate the training data.

Even though both the unsupervised and supervised setting differ in
their structure, they encounter similar problems that one needs to
address. A primary concern which appears in both settings is the
issue of
overfitting
. The intuition behind overfitting is
that the machine learning model takes the data at face-value and may
not be able to generalize the results to unknown data-points. This
is concerning, as we want the models to be flexible and somewhat indicative
of the underlying process. But how can we spot and mitigate this problem?

To spot overfitting we usually split the data that we have at our
hand
\mathcal{D}:=\{(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
into a training-set

\mathcal{P_{\mathrm{tr}}}
and a test-set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{test}}
.
We now train our model exclusively on the training set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{tr}}

and check the validity of the model on the test set
\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{test}}
.
It is important, that the sets are distinct and do not share any instances,

\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{tr}}\cap\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{test}}=\emptyset
.
If the performance of the model on the training set is good, whereas
it is bad on the test set, we know that the model overfit the training
set and did not learn the task properly and is not able to generalize.
To measure generalization one for example studies
\langle\mathcal{L}\rangle_{\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{test}}}=\sum_{(x,y)\in\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{test}}}\,\mathcal{L}(y;\phi_{\hat{\Theta}}(x))
.
There are different ways to mitigate this problem. One popular way
in neural networks is to introduce regularizers such as
L2
-regularizers
(which correspond to weight decay) or drop-out, where some of the
trainable network parameters are fixed for a training step at random
points during the training process.

In this course we further take assumptions made in statistical learning
theory: In order to make theoretical predictions within our frameworks
we make the assumption that all data points
\mathcal{P}
are
independently
and identically distributed (i.i.d.)
according to
p(x,y)
. This
assumption is reasonable: Imagine that the task is to classify images
into cats and dogs. If, for some reason, there are also images of
toads present in your data-set this would correspond to a draw from
a different probability distribution, hence violating the “identically”
in i.i.d. Likewise, for the case of independently distributed data-points
we simply require that there are no spurious correlations in the measurement
process between different samples; a particular example violating
this assumption are repeated data samples. Both assumptions are hence
reasonable and simply correspond to working with a cleaned and well
curated data-set. If you consider practical machine learning applications
a significant portion of time is indeed allocated to curate a clean
data-set before starting the training of a machine learning model.

2
Linear regression

Consider the problem in a supervised learning setup with
P
tuples

\mathcal{D}:=\{(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
, where
x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

is a data point and
y_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
is the target, sometimes
also referred as a label. We wish to train a linear model of the data
of the form

\displaystyle\phi_{w}(x_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha},

(1)

where
w\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
. If there are fewer data points than variables,

P<d
, the problem is underdetermined. If there are more,
P>d
,
the problem is overdetermined. In both cases, one may formulate instead
an optimization problem, known as
linear regression
,

which seeks to minimize the
squared error loss (SE loss)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}(w|\mathcal{D})

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\big(y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big)^{2},

(2)

which measures the sum of quadratic deviations. We may wish to minimize
this function to obtain the value
w^{\ast}
which best fits the
data, so

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle:=\arg\min_{w}\,\mathcal{L}(w|\mathcal{D}),

which leads to the stationarity condition
0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}-\frac{\partial}{\partial w_{i}}\,\mathcal{L}(w|\mathcal{D})=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\big(y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big)\,x_{\alpha i}
.
Defining the matrix
X\in\mathbb{R}^{P\times d}
as
X=\{x_{\alpha i}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P,1\leq i\leq d}

one may write this in the form
0=y^{\mathrm{T}}X-X^{\mathrm{T}}X\,w^{\ast}
which
is a linear equation for
w^{\ast}
with the solution

\displaystyle\hat{w}

\displaystyle=\big(X^{\mathrm{T}}X\big)^{-1}\,X^{\mathrm{T}}y.

(3)

The square matrix
X^{\mathrm{T}}X\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
may not be invertible;
this happens in particular if the number of data points
P<d
, because
then the rank of the matrix is at most
P
. This problem will be
cured by what is known as regularization, adding a small diagonal
matrix
X^{\mathrm{T}}X+\kappa\mathbb{I}.
Formally this may be seen as modifying
the loss function (
2
) to read
\mathcal{L}_{\kappa}(w|\mathcal{D}):=\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\big(y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\kappa\,w^{\mathrm{T}}w

which can be considered a term that favors solutions with small L2-norm

\|w\|=w^{\mathrm{T}}w
. The limit
\kappa\searrow 0
yields the Moore-Penrose
pseudoinverse.

The
linear predictor
for a new test point
x^{\ast}
is
then obtained as

\displaystyle y^{\ast}

\displaystyle=w^{\ast\mathrm{T}}x^{\ast}

(4)

\displaystyle=\big[\big(X^{\mathrm{T}}X\big)^{-1}\,X^{\mathrm{T}}y\big]^{\mathrm{T}}x^{\ast}

\displaystyle=y^{\mathrm{T}}X\,\big(X^{\mathrm{T}}X\,\big)^{-1}\,x^{\ast}.

This predictor is linear in
x^{\ast}
, due to the linearity of the
model, but also linear in
y
. This calculation simply utilizes that
the output is a linear function of the network parameter, which allows
one to utilize the same equations for setups where the non-linear
transformations
\phi
is applied to the data as
y=w^{\top}\phi(x)
.

3
Bayesian formulation

We will now perform a reformulation of the problem of linear regression
in the framework of Bayesian inference. The idea of Bayesian inference
consists of two steps. First, one proposes a
family of models

which is given by a probability distribution over models, called
the
prior
. Then one uses Bayes theorem (
8
)
for conditional distributions to compute the distribution of models
that one obtains when conditioning on the presented training data.
This approach is general and can be applied to any hypothesis class

\phi_{\Theta}(x)
; it is easiest illustrated on the example of a
linear model. To this end, consider the family of linear models, parameterized
by
w
given by (
1
).

Now choose a prior distribution for the linear weights

\displaystyle w_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g\,d^{-1})\,,

(5)

so all
w_{i}
follow the same distribution and are independent of
one another. As we will see, both linear regression and Bayesian
inference share some similarities but let us focus on the key differences:
The aim of (linear) regression is to obtain a single value for each
of the parameters; in the example of linear regression the single
value
w^{\ast}
. One finds this value by demanding that the input-output
relation of the training dataset
\mathcal{P}
is fullfilled as accurately
as possible. In short: Linear regression provides a point estimate
for all parameters.

Opposed to this, the Bayesian approach follows a different, probabilistic,
reasoning: The set of network outputs on the training data
\{f_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}

produced by the linear model (
1
) with
f_{\alpha}:=\phi_{w}(x_{\alpha})

may formally be written as a conditional probability distribution
(cf.
Section
2
)

\displaystyle p(f|X,w)

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[f_{\alpha}-\phi_{w}(x_{\alpha})\big].

(6)

Here the product over all data points
\alpha
appears, because we
want to enforce the same input-output relation (
1
)
for each data point
\alpha
, so that output
f_{\alpha}
corresponds
to input
x_{\alpha}
using the very same value
w
for the weights
for all these points. The prior distribution of parameters (
5
),
by Bayes’ law (
8
), induces a joint distribution for
the set of outputs
\{y_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
and the weights
via the chain rule of probabilities

\displaystyle p(y,w|X)

\displaystyle=p(y|X,w)\,p(w).

(7)

The idea of Bayesian inference is to determine the
posterior
distribution

for the weights
on the left hand side below
, which is the distribution of the
w
given one fixes the outputs

y
to the known values of the training set, namely by Bayes’ law
(
8
)

\displaystyle p(w|y,X)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(y,w|X)}{p(y|X)}.

(8)

Here
p(y|X)
on the right hand side is the marginalization of (
7
)
over
w
, namely
p(y|X)=\int dw\,p(y,w|X)
such that the last expression
is properly normalized. Intuitively this means that we only allow
those parameters
w
which correctly produce the output
y
, because

p(y|X,w)
vanishes for all other parameters. One may then use the
conditional distribution (
6
) and
the posterior for the weights (
8
) to obtain
the
predictive distribution
for the output corresponding
to a new (so far unseen) data point
x_{\ast}
, namely

\displaystyle p(y_{\ast}|y,X,x_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\int dw\,p(y_{\ast}|x_{\ast},w)\,p(w|y,X).

(9)

One hence obtains the
posterior distribution for the output

y_{\ast}
. To illustrate the logic of Bayesian inference, we here
went the following way:

1.

prior for weights
w
(
5
)

2.

condition on training outputs
y
to obtain posterior for weights
(
8
)

3.

use posterior for weights to obtain posterior for the output (
9
)
for a new input to make a prediction

We will now investigate a shortcut that omits the intermediate step
of computing the weight posterior. This shortcut can be appreciated
by inserting (
7
) and (
8
)
into (
9
) with the result

\displaystyle p(y_{\ast}|y,X,x_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{\int dw\,p(y|X,w)\,p(y_{\ast}|x_{\ast},w)\,p(w)}{p(y|X)}

(10)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(y,y_{\ast}|X,x_{\ast})}{p(y|X)},

where in the second step we used that the numerator is the joint distribution
of the training outputs
y_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
and the test output

y_{\ast}
under the prior distribution for the weights
w
; the
numerator can hence be considered as the
prior distribution
for the outputs
. This prior distribution is here directly conditioned
onto the training data, which is given by Bayes formula for a conditional
probability distribution. We have hence circumvented the intermedate
step of computing the posterior for the weights and instead directly
obtained the posterior for the test output
y_{\ast}
. The denominator
in (
10
), called the
model
evidence
because it quantifies how likely the training data
y

came from the model, does not depend on
y_{\ast}
, so it does not
change the shape of the distribution for
y_{\ast}
; it only affects
its normalization. The interesting structure of the posterior for

y_{\ast}
must hence be contained in the numerator of (
10
),
corresponding to the joint distribution (
11
).

Considering the test input
x_{\ast}
and the test output
y_{\ast}

as the sample
\alpha=P+1
, the numerator in (
10
)
may be written with (
6
) as

\displaystyle p(y,y_{\ast}|X,x_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\,\delta\big[y_{\alpha}-\phi_{w}(x_{\alpha})\big]\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})}

(11)

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\,\delta\big[y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big]\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})}.

Due to the linearity of the output
\phi_{w}
in
w
, for the linear
model this is a joint Gaussian distribution on the set of
\{y_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P+1}
.
Note that the
X
are given and fixed here and the randomness is
entirely caused by the random weights
w_{i}
. In the following we
will write
\bar{y}:=(y,y_{\ast})
and
\bar{X}=(X,x_{\ast})
for
the outputs and inputs jointly for all training points and the one
test point.

We know by the linear appearance of
w_{i}
that the distribution

p(\bar{y}|\bar{X})
is Gaussian. We here introduce a helpful technique
we will use throughout to see this explicitly which in particular
allows us to conveniently compute the conditional (
10
).
To this end one uses the Fourier transform of the Dirac distribution

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}[\delta](\omega)

\displaystyle=\int\delta(y)\,e^{-i\omega y}\,dy=1

\displaystyle\delta(y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,e^{i\omega y}\,1\,d\omega

\displaystyle=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{y}}{2\pi i}\,e^{\tilde{y}y}

to express the Dirac distribution with
\delta(y)=\delta(-y)
in
(
11
) as

\displaystyle p(\bar{y}|\bar{X})

\displaystyle=\Big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{y}_{\alpha}}{2\pi i}\,\exp\big(\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,\big[w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}\big]\big)\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})}

(12)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,y_{\alpha}\big)\,\Big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big)\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})},

where

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{y}_{\alpha}}{2\pi i}.

(13)

The latter expectation value takes the form of the moment-generating
function for the independently drawn Gaussian weights
w_{i}
with
a source
j_{i}=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}x_{\alpha i}

\displaystyle\Big\langle\exp\big(\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big)\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\,\Big\langle\exp\big(w_{i}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}x_{\alpha i}\big)\Big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,gd^{-1})}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{d}\,\exp\Big(\frac{g}{2d}\,\Big[\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P+1}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}x_{\alpha i}\Big]^{2}\Big)

\displaystyle=\exp\Big(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P+1}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\frac{g}{d}\,\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{\alpha i}x_{\beta i}\Big),

where we used that the cumulant-generating function of the Gaussian

w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g/d)
by (
4
) is
W_{w_{i}}(j_{i})=\frac{g}{2d}j_{i}^{2}
.
Inserted back into (
12
) one has

\displaystyle p(\bar{y}|\bar{X})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+W_{y}(\tilde{y})\big),

(14)

where

\displaystyle W_{y}(\tilde{y})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}C^{(xx)}\tilde{y},

(15)

\displaystyle C^{(xx)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g}{d}\,\bar{X}\bar{X}^{\mathrm{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(P+1)\times(P+1)}.

The covariance matrix
C^{(xx)}
is obviously symmetric, as it has
to be (see
Section
1
). Note that the
data here only enters in the form of an inner product across indices

i
, which is also called a
dot-product kernel
. In linear
regression, we had the matrix
X^{\mathrm{T}}X
, so an inner product over
the sample indices
\alpha
instead. In the case of non-linear regression

y=w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(x)
, instead,
\phi(X)^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(X)
would appear.

(
14
) is the Fourier representation of the distribution
for
y
(cf. (
20
)) and (
15
)
is a quadratic polynomial, so it describes a zero mean Gaussian distribution
with covariance given by
C^{(xx)}
, so

\displaystyle\{y_{\alpha}\}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)}).

(16)

To obtain the form of the posterior for the test output (
10
)
it is sufficient to consider the numerator explicitly, because the
denominator only supplies the correct normalization, which we may
fix post hoc. This conditional distribution is obtained most easily
from the Fourier representation (
14
) by inserting
the set of training outputs
y_{\circ}:=y
(using the subscript
\circ

here to denote all sample indices
\circ=\{\alpha\in\mathbb{d}:1\leq\alpha\leq P\}
)

\displaystyle p(y_{\circ},y_{\ast}|X,x_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}(\tilde{y}_{\circ},\tilde{y}_{\ast})\,\exp\big(-\tilde{y}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}}y_{\circ}-\tilde{y}_{\ast}y_{\ast}+\frac{1}{2}\,(\tilde{y}_{\circ},\tilde{y}_{\ast})^{\mathrm{T}}C^{(xx)}(\tilde{y}_{\circ},\tilde{y}_{\ast})\big)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}_{\ast}\,\exp\big(-\tilde{y}_{\ast}y_{\ast}+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}_{\ast}C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}\tilde{y}_{\ast}\big)

\displaystyle\phantom{=}\times\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}_{\circ}\,\exp\big(\big[\tilde{y}_{\ast}C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}-y_{\circ}\big]^{\mathrm{T}}\tilde{y}_{\circ}+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}_{\circ}^{\mathrm{T}}C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}\tilde{y}_{\circ}\big),

where we split the matrix
C^{(xx)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}&C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}\\
C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}&C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}\end{array}\right]
into four blocks and used the symmetry
C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}=C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}
.
Performing the integration over
\tilde{y}_{\circ}
only affects the latter
line, using the Gaussian identity (
3
) to obtain

\displaystyle p(y_{\circ},y_{\ast}|X,x_{\ast})

\displaystyle\propto\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}_{\ast}\,\exp\big(-\tilde{y}_{\ast}y_{\ast}+W_{\ast}(\tilde{y}_{\ast})\big),

(17)

\displaystyle W_{\ast}(\tilde{y}_{\ast})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}_{\ast}C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}\tilde{y}_{\ast}-\frac{1}{2}\,\big[\tilde{y}_{\ast}C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}-y_{\circ}\big]^{\mathrm{T}}\,[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}\,\big[C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}\tilde{y}_{\ast}-y_{\circ}\big]

\displaystyle=\tilde{y}_{\ast}C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}y_{\circ}+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}_{\ast}\big[C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}-C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}\big]\,\tilde{y}_{\ast}+\mathrm{const.},

where we dropped terms independent of
\tilde{y}
, because they only affect
the normalization. The form (
17
)
is the Fourier representation of a Gaussian distribution in
y_{\ast}

with a mean given by the linear coefficient of
\tilde{y}
and a covariance
given by the quadratic coefficient, so that one reads off

\displaystyle y_{\ast}

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}\Big(C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}y_{\circ},

(18)

\displaystyle\phantom{\sim\mathcal{N}\Big(}C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}-C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}\Big).

The meaning of this distribution is the distribution for the output

y_{\ast}
if one conditions the distribution of models on the presented
training data. The mean value can be considered the mean output, the
variance represents a form of uncertainty which is still present despite
the presented training data. The knowledge of the training data and
its labels leads to a mean-predictor
\langle y_{\ast}\rangle
which
is in general non-zero, in contrast to the prior in (
16
).
The variance consists of the initial uncertainty of the prior and
a term which reduces this initial uncertainty due to the conditioning.
The change of the distribution of the weights and the predicted labels
by conditioning on training data is displayed in
Figure
1
.

Figure 1:
Linear regression in Bayesian framework.
Comparison between
prior and posterior distributions for the linear model. Here the output

p(y|X,w)
of the linear regression is assumed to be stochastic with
a Gaussian regularization noise, namely instead of (
6
)
we here use
p(y|X,w)=\mathcal{N}(y|w^{\mathrm{T}}x,\kappa\,\mathbb{I})

which corresponds to adding Gaussian noise
\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
,
i.e.
y_{\alpha}\to y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}
; this is often done
as a means of regularization: it forces the outputs to be close to
the training points, but allows for some wiggle room.
a)

Prior and posterior of labels
y_{\ast}
shown as mean and standard
deviation from (
18
). The posterior is obtained
by conditioning on the training labels
y_{\circ}
.
b)
Prior
and posterior distributions of the slope of the linear model.
c)

Same as
a)
but for zero noise (
\kappa=0
).
d)

Same as
b)
but for zero noise (
\kappa=0
). (Adapted from
Bachelor thesis by Bastian Epping, 2020.)

1
Bias-variance decomposition

The Gaussian distribution of the output for the test point allows
us to measure how well the test point matches a potentially given
ground truth
y_{\ast}^{0}
. If we use the squared error loss, we
may be interested in the mean of this loss under the distribution
of outputs (
18
), whose two cumulants we
here denote as
\mu
and
\Sigma
, so
y_{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}(y_{\ast},y_{\ast}^{0})\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle(y_{\ast}-y_{\ast}^{0})^{2}\rangle_{y_{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\Sigma)}

(19)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle(\mu+\delta y_{\ast}-y_{\ast}^{0})^{2}\rangle_{\delta y_{\ast}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mu-y_{\ast}^{0})^{2}+(\mu-y_{\ast}^{0})\,\underbrace{\langle\delta y_{\ast}\rangle}_{=0}+\frac{1}{2}\underbrace{\langle\delta y_{\ast}^{2}\rangle}_{\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}(\mu-y_{\ast}^{0})^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma,

which is called a
bias-variance decomposition
. The bias describes
the average output of the ensemble of posterior networks, the variance
the variability of these outputs around this average. Both terms enter
the expected loss; the mean output enters in relation to the desired
output,
y_{\ast}^{0}
.

2
Connection to linear regression

To make connection to the result from linear regression, (
4
),
we may compare it to the mean of the predictor in (
18
)
written as (writing again
y\equiv y_{\circ}
here)

\displaystyle\langle y_{\ast}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}y

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:cum_gen_prior})}}{{=}}x_{\ast}^{\mathrm{T}}\,X^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big(XX^{\mathrm{T}}\big)^{-1}\,y,

while the predictor for linear regression is

\displaystyle y_{\ast}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:posterior_Gaussi})}}{{=}}y^{\mathrm{T}}X\,\big(X^{\mathrm{T}}X\big)^{-1}x_{\ast}

\displaystyle=x_{\ast}^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big(X^{\mathrm{T}}X\big)^{-1}X^{\mathrm{T}}\,y.

The two expressions are in fact identical because by the associativity
of matrix multiplication

\displaystyle(X^{\mathrm{T}}\,X)\,X^{\mathrm{T}}

\displaystyle=X^{\mathrm{T}}\,(X\,X^{\mathrm{T}}),

from which follows by multiplying with
(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}
from left
and by
(XX^{\mathrm{T}})^{-1}
from right

\displaystyle X^{\mathrm{T}}\,(XX^{\mathrm{T}})^{-1}

\displaystyle=(X^{\mathrm{T}}X)^{-1}\,X^{\mathrm{T}},

showing that the two expressions for the linear predictor and the
mean of the predictive distribution agree. So linear regression yields
the mean predictor of Bayesian inference, while the latter, in addition,
yields the variance that can be used to quantify the uncertainty of
the prediction.

4
Keypoints

In summary

Linear regression as Bayesian inference

•

Linear regression is a type of supervised learning.

•

Bayesian inference in a linear regression setup produces Gaussian
processes with dot-product kernels.

•

The results of linear regression for inferred labels matches the mean
prediction of the Gaussian process in Bayesian approach. Linear regression
provides a point estimate for model parameters whereas Bayesian inference
produces a posterior distribution of parameters.

•

Supervised learning in a Bayesian setting corresponds to computing
the probability of inferred network outputs at the inference points
conditioned on the training data.

•

One can reframe the problem of supervised learning in Bayesian setting
in a field theoretic language by enforcing the regression model using
Dirac-constraints and by introducing conjugate fields
\tilde{y}
.

•

Regularization mitigates ill-conditioned matrices and can be introduced
by observation noise or by constraining the weight norm using L2 regularizers
(in the exercises)

5
Exercises

Exercise a) Addition of independent variables (2p)

Show that the cumulant-generating function of a sum
z=x+y
of two
independently distributed multivariate variables
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and

y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
decomposes into a sum itself:

\displaystyle W_{z}(j)

\displaystyle=W_{x}(j)+W_{y}(j).

Apply this result to a pair of Gaussian variables
x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{x},\Sigma_{x})

and
y\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{y},\Sigma_{y})
to show that the sum is again a
Gaussian with distribution

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{x}+\mu_{y},\Sigma_{x}+\Sigma_{y}).

Exercise b) Useful derivatives (4p)

1.

Show

\frac{\partial\ln\left(\det\left(C\right)\right)}{\partial C_{ij}}=C_{ij}^{-1},

(20)

for
\det\left(C\right)>0
. Hint: Express
\det\left(C\right)
through
a Gaussian integral using the normalization condition of the Gaussian

\displaystyle 1

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}^{D}\det\left(C^{-1}\right)^{1/2}}\int dx\,\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}x^{\mathrm{T}}Cx\right).

(2p)

2.

Compute the derivative

\frac{\partial C^{-1}}{\partial C_{ij}}

(21)

Hint: take the derivative of
\mathbb{I}=CC^{-1}
(2p)

Exercise c) Linear regression and Bayesian inference (9p)

As we have seen in the main text there are inherent similarities
but also important differences between Bayesian inference and linear
regression, both of which we will explore a bit further in this exercise.
First we want to understand what happens to the inferred label mean
and variance

\displaystyle\langle y_{*}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}y_{\circ}

(22)

\displaystyle\langle\langle y_{*}^{2}\rangle\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ast\ast}^{(xx)}-C_{\ast\circ}^{(xx)}[C_{\circ\circ}^{(xx)}]^{-1}C_{\circ\ast}^{(xx)}

(23)

if the test point
*
is part of the training data-set
\circ
?
Does the result match your intuition? (2p)We now want to understand
what happens, when we make the labels stochastic; alternatively, this
can be seen as adding random noise on-top of our network output. Consider
the following construction for labels
y_{\alpha}
according to a
simple linear function

\displaystyle f_{\alpha}

\displaystyle:=w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha},

(24)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=f_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha},

(25)

\displaystyle\xi_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }\alpha}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa),

(26)

where we assume that both the scalar inputs
x_{\alpha}
and the
scalar noise
\xi_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
are both i.i.d. and we
assume a prior distribution on
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g\,d^{-1})
.
The data is given by pairs of inputs
x
and labels
z
as
\mathcal{D}:=\{(x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
.
Show that we can write the distribution
p(y,f|X)
as

\displaystyle p(y,f|X)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(y|f,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{f}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha}\,\tilde{f}_{\alpha}\big[f_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}\big]\,\big)\big\rangle_{w},

(27)

where

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{f}

\displaystyle=\prod_{\alpha}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{1}{2\pi i}d\tilde{f}_{\alpha}\,,

(28)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\xi|\mu,\kappa)

\displaystyle:=\prod_{\alpha=1}^{D}\,\mathcal{N}(\xi_{\alpha}|\mu_{\alpha},\kappa)\,,

(29)

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\xi_{\alpha}|\mu_{\alpha},\kappa)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\kappa}}\,e^{-\frac{(\xi_{\alpha}-\mu_{\alpha})^{2}}{2\kappa}}.

(30)

(2p). Start by enforcing the relations
f_{\alpha}=w^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\alpha}

and
y_{\alpha}=f_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}
using Dirac-delta constraints
and average over the noise
\xi
in addition to the weights
w
.
Following the arguments in the main text (1p) show, that the distribution
of
y
, given by
p(y|X)=\int df\,p(y,f|X)
, is a Gaussian with
covariance (kernel)

C_{\alpha\beta}^{(zz)}=\frac{g}{d}\,x_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\beta}+\kappa\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}.

(31)

Why could the additional term on the diagonal of (
31
)
become relevant in numerical implementation? Hint: Consider the formula
for the inferred label (0.5p). Consider now setting with regularization

\kappa>0
: What are the mean and the variance of the inferred label
if the test point
*
is now of the training data-set
\circ
?

We want to connect this result to linear regression: To this end marginalize
the distribution (
27
) as

\displaystyle p(y|X)

\displaystyle=\int df\,p(y,f|X).

Perform the integral over
y
and write the result as
p(y|X)=\int dw\,\exp\big(S(w)\big)
.
Then compute the
w
which maximizes the exponent
S
(2p) and compare
to the result (

). What happens, if instead
of (
24
) we take a model of
the structure

y_{\alpha}=w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(x_{\alpha})+\xi_{\alpha}\,.

which maximizes the

Chapter 4
The law of large numbers – large deviation principle

In this section we visit an important principle that helps us to
describe systems with large numbers of degrees of freedom – the law
of large numbers and its formal underpinning, large deviation theory.
We will see that the former describes small deviations of a random
quantity away from its typical value, while the latter also captures
deviations far away from the expected value. We will subsequently
use these results to quantify properties of networks with large numbers
of neurons.

1
Gärtner-Ellis theorem

We here follow
[
32
, Appendic C]
. Consider a sequence
of random variables
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\sim p
, which are jointly
distributed with a certain distribution
p
. Assume we are interested
in a random variable
S_{N}(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})=S_{N}(x)
. For example,
we may want to known the sample mean
S_{N}(x)=N^{-1}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}
.
In particular, we would like to know the distribution of
S_{N}

in the limit of large
N
. This distribution is, with
Section
2
,

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta\big[s-S_{N}(x)\big]\big\rangle_{x\sim p}.

(1)

We will now employ the Laplace representation of the Dirac distribution

\displaystyle\delta(x)

\displaystyle=\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,e^{jx},

(2)

where
a\in\mathbb{R}
is arbitrary. The integration contour is called a

Bromwich contour
. The integration runs parallel to the imaginary
axis. One can show that this representation is correct by considering
how the Dirac distribution acts on a test function
f

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\,dx\,f(x)\,\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,e^{jx}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dx\,e^{ax}\,f(x)\,\underbrace{\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,e^{jx}}_{\delta(x)}=f(0),

where we used the Fourier representation of the Dirac distribution

\delta(x)=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,e^{jx}=1

and note that the result is independent of
a
, the position where
the Bromwich contour intersects the real axis.

Using the representation (
2
) in (
1
)
one has

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle=\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,\big\langle\exp\big(j\,(S_{N}(x)-s)\big)\big\rangle_{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,\exp\big(-j\,s+W_{N}(j)),

(3)

where
W_{N}(j)=\ln\,\big\langle\exp\big(j\,S_{N}(x)\big)\big\rangle_{x}

is the cumulant-generating function of
S_{N}
. In the case that
the
x_{i}
were independent, one would obtain
W_{N}\propto N

due to the
N
independent variables (see exercises). In the general
case this motivates the definition of the
scaled cumulant-generating
function
as

\displaystyle\lambda_{N}(k)

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}W_{N}(N\,k),

where the factor
N
in the argument can be thought of in the example
of the sample mean
S_{N}(x)=N^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}
to compensate
the prefactor
N^{-1}
: In this example, we would have
W_{N}(j)=N\,W_{1}(j/N)
,
where
W_{1}(j)
is the cumulant-generating function of a single

x_{i}
(see also example below); so in this case
\lambda_{N}(k)=W_{1}(k)
,
which is independent of
N
.

In the general case that the limit
N\to\infty
of this function

\lambda_{N}
exists, one defines

\displaystyle\lambda(k)

\displaystyle:=\lim_{N\to\infty}N^{-1}W_{N}(N\,k).

In this case, we may replace
W_{N}(j)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\to\infty}}{{\simeq}}N\,\lambda(j/N)

to get for the distribution (
3
)

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\gg 1}}{{\simeq}}\int_{a-i\infty}^{a+i\infty}\,\frac{dj}{2\pi i}\,\exp\big(-j\,s+N\,\lambda(j/N)\big)

(4)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{subst. }N\,k=j}}{{=}}\int_{a/N-i\infty}^{a/N+i\infty}\,\frac{d(Nk)}{2\pi i}\,\exp\big(-N\,\big[k\,s-\lambda(k)\big]\,\big).

Since the parameter
a
was arbitrary in the Laplace representation
(
2
) we may choose it conveniently. We here choose
it such that the Bromwich contour passes through the saddle point
of the integrand; this is the real-valued point
a/N=k^{\ast}
, where

\frac{\partial}{\partial k}\big[k\,s-\lambda(k)\big]=0
. Due to
the imaginary unit in the integration variable, all other points along
the Bromwich contour produce oscillatory contributions that cancel
each other, so that the dominant contribution comes from this saddle
point
k^{\ast}(s)\in\mathbb{R}
, so one approximates

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\gg 1}}{{\simeq}}\exp\big(-N\,\big[k^{\ast}\,s-\lambda(k^{\ast})\big]\,\big)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\to\infty}\,-\frac{1}{N}\,\ln\,p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k}\big[k\,s-\lambda(k)\big]=:\gamma(s),

(5)

where we could write
\sup
here, because we know that
\lambda^{\prime\prime}

is the variance, which must be
\geq 0
, so that
\lambda
has positive
curvature, so
-\lambda
has negative, so the stationary point must
be a local maximum. This is the Gärtner-Ellis theorem. The function

\gamma(s)
is called the
rate function
and we have

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\gg 1}}{{\simeq}}e^{-N\,\gamma(s)}

up to proportionality.

2
Example

Consider a set of
N
random numbers
(x_{1},\ldots,x_{N})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}p
.
Assume that we are interested in another random variable, the
sample
mean
or
empirical average

\displaystyle S_{N}(N)

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}.

(6)

Let us check that the scaled cumulant generating function exists.
The cumulant-generating function for
S_{N}
is

\displaystyle W_{N}(j)

\displaystyle:=\ln\big\langle e^{j\,S_{N}(x)}\big\rangle_{x}=\ln\,\langle e^{j\,N^{-1}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{i}}\rangle_{x\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}p}

(7)

\displaystyle=\ln\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\langle e^{j\,N^{-1}\,x_{i}}\rangle_{x_{i}\sim p}

\displaystyle=N\,W_{1}(j/N),

where
W_{1}(k):=\ln\,\langle e^{j\,x}\rangle_{x\sim p}
is the cumulant-generating
function for a single variable
x_{i}
. So the scaled cumulant-generating
function is

\displaystyle\lambda_{N}(k)

\displaystyle:=N^{-1}\,W_{N}(N\,k)

\displaystyle=W_{1}(k).

(8)

The limit
N\to\infty
here exists trivially
\lambda(k)=\lim_{N\to\infty}\,\lambda_{N}(k)=W_{1}(k)
,
as the expression is independent of
N
. So the rate function is

\displaystyle\gamma(s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k}\,sk-W_{1}(k).

(9)

This result shows that the large deviation approach is more general
than the law of large numbers: It is not restricted to the summation,
as we have seen in the general derivation and it also holds for arbitrary
values of
s
, so also for
s
that are far away from the expected
mean value. Also we note that the function
\gamma
is in general
not a quadratic polynomial; only in that case one obtains the law
of large numbers, when
e^{-N\gamma(s)}
reduces to a Gaussian distribution.

The law of large numbers can be obtained from here by expanding
W_{1}(k)=\mu\,k+\frac{1}{2}\Sigma\,k^{2}+\mathcal{O}(k^{3})
,
which is valid for small
k
. In the rate function (
9
)
small values of
k
correspond to values of
s
close the mean value,
because a supremum at
k^{\ast}=0
is obtained precisely when
0=s-W_{1}^{\prime}(0)

and
W_{1}^{\prime}(0)=\mu
, the mean value of
x_{i}
and hence
of
S_{N}
. Using this quadratic expansion one gets

\displaystyle\gamma(s)

\displaystyle=\sup_{k}\,(s-\mu)\,k-\frac{1}{2}\Sigma\,k^{2}+\mathcal{O}(k^{3}),

whose supremum is at
0=(s-\mu)-\Sigma\,k^{\ast}
, which, inserted
back, yields

\displaystyle\gamma(s)

\displaystyle=\frac{(s-\mu)^{2}}{\Sigma}-\frac{1}{2}\frac{\Sigma\,(s-\mu)^{2}}{\Sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(s-\mu)^{2}}{\Sigma},

so for
s\simeq\mu
we get the law of large numbers

\displaystyle p_{N}(s)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-N\gamma(s)}\simeq e^{-\frac{1}{2}\,\frac{(s-\mu)^{2}}{\Sigma/N}}.

3
Legendre transform and equation of state

The operation in (
5
) is called a
Legendre-Fenchel
transform
. It may in general be described for any cumulant-generating
function
W(j)=\ln\langle e^{j^{\mathrm{T}}x}\rangle_{x}
as

\displaystyle\Gamma(x^{\ast})

\displaystyle:=\sup_{j}\,j^{\mathrm{T}}x^{\ast}-W(j).

Evaluating the supremum condition (assuming the r.h.s. be differentiable
in
j
) one gets the condition

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}x^{\ast}-\underbrace{W^{(1)}(j)}_{\langle x(j)\rangle},

which shows that
j
is chosen such that
x^{\ast}
becomes the
mean value of
x
. This condition always has a solution because one
can show that
W(j)
is a convex function.

So the Legendre transform can be considered the tool to define an
ensemble with a fixed mean value given by
x^{\ast}
. The large deviation
principle that we have seen before simply states that, because the
empirical average
S=N^{-1}\sum_{i}x_{i}
concentrates around a typical
value, it is sufficient to know this value to (approximately) know
the number of (microscopic) states of the
x_{i}
that contribute
and thus provide the probability for
p(s)
.

The Legendre transform has the general property

\displaystyle\frac{d\Gamma}{dx^{\ast}}(x^{\ast})=

\displaystyle j+\frac{\partial j^{\mathrm{T}}}{\partial x^{\ast}}x^{\ast}-\underbrace{\frac{\partial W^{\mathrm{T}}}{\partial j}}_{x^{\ast\mathrm{T}}}\frac{\partial j}{\partial x^{\ast}}

(10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle j.

The latter equation is also called
equation of state
, as
its solution for
x^{\ast}
allows us to determine the mean value
for a given source
j
. In statistical physics this mean value is
typically an order parameter, an observable that characterizes the
state of the system. One therefore has the reciprocity between the
pair of functions
W
and
\Gamma

\displaystyle W^{(1)}(j)

\displaystyle=x^{\ast},

\displaystyle\Gamma^{(1)}(x^{\ast})

\displaystyle=j.

4
Keypoints

We summarize the key points on large deviations

The law of large numbers - The large deviation principle

•

The Gärtner Ellis theorem allows us to estimate probabilities using
the rate function
\gamma
:
p(s)\propto\exp(-N\gamma(s))
, if we
have a scaled CGF and large
N
.

•

The rate function is the Legendre-Fenchel transform of the scaled
cumulant generating function.

•

The large deviation principle is more general than the central limit
theorem, because it is also valid for deviations far away from from
the mean.

•

Source and mean in the rate function are related via the equation
of state, which is a general property of Legendre-Fenchel transforms;
first derivatives
\Gamma^{\prime}
and
W^{\prime}
form a pair
of inverse functions of one another.

Exercises

a) Sums of random variables: Central limit theorem vs large deviation
theory

x_{i}

x_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\begin{cases}1&\text{with probability }p\\

. The

.

,

Optional:: Probability bounds and Large deviation theory (9p)

We want to use the results from the main text for some applications
in probability theory. Especially in computer science so called “probability
bounds” for random variables
x
are used quite often. The use-case
for the bounds is to produce upper/lower limits on the probabilities
such as
P(x>a)
with limited information about the distribution
of
x
. The most basic relation is the so called Markov bound on
positive random numbers
x
which only utilizes the mean of the distribution

\mathbb{E}(x)
to create a bound on the probability
p(x\geq a)

p(x\geq a)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(x)}{a}\quad\mathrm{for\quad}x\geq 0\quad.

(11)

Show that the Markov bound is valid for any
a\geq 0
(1p). Hint:
Start from the definition of the expectation value
\mathbb{E}(x)

and follow successive inequalities to reach the result. As it turns
out, the Markov bound is a reasonable first guess but only a rough
heuristic. One can however refine the statement using more information
about the probability distribution of
x
. One such way is to utilize
the Chebychev bound (1p):

p(|x-\mathbb{E}(x)|\geq a)\leq\frac{\mathrm{Var}(x)}{a^{2}}\quad\mathrm{with\quad}a\geq 0.

(12)

Use the Markov bound to prove this statement. Hint: Use an auxiliary
variable
y:=(x-\mathbb{E}(x))^{2}
. As you can see, the Chebychev
bound uses knowledge of both the mean
\mathbb{E}(x)
and the variance

\mathrm{Var}(x)
. In a similar spirit we can now ask whether there
is a way to include even more knowledge about the distribution of

x
into the bounds? One way to do this is the Chernoff bound. This
bound relates the cumulant generating function
W(s)
of the variable

x
to the probabibility
p(x\geq a)
in the following way:

p(x\geq a)\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}(\exp(sx))}{\exp(sa)}=\frac{\exp(W(s))}{\exp(sa)}\quad\mathrm{for\quad}s,a\geq 0\quad.

(13)

Use the Markov bound to show this inequality. Hint: Use the fact that

\exp(x)
is a convex function (2p). As (
13
) is
a bound for arbitrary
s
, we want to find the
s
which provides
the best possible bound on
p(x\geq a)
. Relate this to the rate
function in the main text and show that the minimal Chernoff bound
in terms of the rate function
I(a)
reads (1p)

p(x\geq a)\leq\exp(-I(a))\quad\mathrm{for\quad}x,a\geq 0\quad.

(14)

Let us investigate the validity of the results by using the example
of
n
i.i.d. coin tosses, where the coin yields the value
+1

(heads) with probability
p
and
0
(tails) with probability
(1-p)
.
What are the Markov, Chebychev and Chernoff bounds on the probability
to observe at least
\frac{3}{4}n
times heads when
p=0.6
. Comment
on the scaling of the three bounds with
n
and the consequences
to estimate the probability of large deviations from the mean. (3p+1p)
. Bonus: Assume you know the mean
\kappa_{1}
and the variance
\kappa_{2}

of the random variables
x_{i}
and you want to obtain the bound
on

p(y\geq\delta n)\quad\mathrm{with\quad}y=\sum_{i}x_{i}\,\quad\mathrm{and}\quad\delta\in[0,1].

What is the best guess that you can make using any of the above bounds?

Chapter 5
Neural network Gaussian processes

We will now apply the idea of Bayesian inference to networks that
include hidden layers. We begin with the single hidden layer network
and subsequently extend the theory to networks with multiple hidden
layers known as deep networks or multi-layer perceptrons. We begin
with the limit where we take the layer width to infinity and will
see that this limit yields to Gaussian processes, very similar to
linear regression. This limit will serve as the starting point to
study networks of finite width.

1
Single hidden layer network

We here consider a network with a single hidden layer whose activations
are called
h\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

\displaystyle h

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\,x,

(1)

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y+\xi,

where
\phi
is a point-wise applied activation function and
\xi

is a Gaussian readout noise
\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
.
We will see that this noise acts as a regulator. As in
Section
2

x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is the data and
y\in\mathbb{R}
is the scalar output and
we consider
P
tuples of training data
\mathcal{D}=\{(x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
.
We again consider the matrix
\{\mathbb{R}^{P\times d}\ni X\}_{\alpha i}=x_{\alpha i}
.
The readout
w\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and the matrix
V\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d}
are
the trainable weights. The noise is introduced to implement regularization,
as we have seen it in the case of linear regression of Bayesian inference,
so we will condition on
z
being fixed to the desired output, but
will be interested also in the distribution of the readout
y
of
the hidden layer.

2
Intuitive approach to neural network Gaussian processes

Before embarking on a more formal approach to derive the behavior
of networks in the limit of infinite width, we here present a heuristic
derivation. Let us consider the single hidden layer example (
1
).
For simplicity we here set the regularization noise
\xi=0
; an extension
to
\xi\neq 0
is straight forward.

Due to the sum appearing in
y_{\alpha}=\sum_{j=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})

and the independence of the
w_{i}
, we expect the distribution of
the
\{y_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
to be jointly Gaussian. We
also note that due to
\langle w_{i}\rangle=0
, the mean
\langle y\rangle=0

vanishes. It is therefore sufficient to characterize the statistics
by its second moment
\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle
, where
the average is with regard to the weights
w
and
V
. We start
from

\displaystyle\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\left\langle\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}w_{i}w_{j}\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta j})\right\rangle_{W,V}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\left\langle w_{i}w_{j}\right\rangle_{w}\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta j})\right\rangle_{V}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta i})\right\rangle_{V},

where we have split the averages, as
W
and
V
are independent
and we utilized that the
w_{i}
are pairwise independent
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g/N)

to write
\langle w_{i}w_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\,g_{w}/N
, eliminating
one of the sums. As
h_{\alpha i}
is the only dependence on
V
,
we can replace

\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta i})\right\rangle_{V}=\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta i})\right\rangle_{h_{\alpha i},h_{\beta i}}.

The distribution of the
h_{\alpha i}=\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{ij}\,x_{\alpha j}

is Gaussian, because the
x_{\alpha j}
are fixed and
h
depends
linearly on the Gaussian
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g/d)
;
due to the vanishing mean of
V
, also the mean of
h
vanishes,
so it is sufficient to compute the second moment

\displaystyle\big\langle h_{\alpha i}h_{\alpha j}\big\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,l=1}^{d}\,\underbrace{\big\langle V_{ik}\,V_{jl}\big\rangle}_{\delta_{ij}\,\delta_{kl}\,g_{V}/d}\,x_{\alpha k}x_{\beta l}=\delta_{ij}\,\frac{g_{V}}{d}\sum_{k=1}^{d}x_{\alpha k}x_{\beta k}=:\delta_{ij}C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)},

so
h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})

in particular, they are independent across
i
. Hence we obtain

\displaystyle\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle_{W,V}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha i})\phi(h_{\beta i})\right\rangle_{h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\left\langle\phi(h_{\alpha})\phi(h_{\beta})\right\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})},

where we used in the last step that all expectation values in the
sum yield the same result.

This Gaussian distribution for
y
is the prior distribution of the
network outputs. Conditioning on the training data, we may therefore
use the same expressions for the mean and covariance of the predictive
distribution as derived for linear regression (
18
),
only replacing
C^{(xx)}
(linear regression) by
g_{w}C^{(\phi\phi)}

(NNGP).

The derivation above can be made precise by employing the central
limit theorem. However, it prevents us from studying finite
N
corrections
and the interplay between the amount of training data
P
and
N
.
From the derivation above it is also clear that the procedure may
be iterated across multiple layers: One proceeds by induction to show,
layer by layer, that the central limit theorem assures a Gaussian
distribution.

3
Network field theory

To obtain the network prior, for an i.i.d. prior
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/d)
,
it is easy to see that also the
h_{\alpha i}=\sum_{j}V_{ij}\,x_{\alpha j}

are Gaussian random variables that are independent across
i
but
correlated across different
\alpha
following the distribution

\displaystyle h_{\alpha i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)}),

(2)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{P\times P}\ni C^{(xx)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{V}}{d}\,X\,X^{\mathrm{T}}

by the same arguments as in
Section
3
.
The probability distribution of the readout
y
is only a function
of
C^{(xx)}
and given with the Gaussian distribution
\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)=\frac{1}{(2\pi\kappa)^{\frac{P}{2}}}\,e^{-\frac{\|z-y\|^{2}}{2\kappa}}

a Gaussian distribution in
z
with mean
y

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\big\langle\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\,\big[y_{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})\big]\big\rangle_{w_{i},h_{\alpha i}}

(3)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\big[-y_{\alpha}+w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})\big]\,\big)\big\rangle_{w_{i},h_{\alpha i}},

where
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})

and
h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})

and
\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}
is given by (
13
) and we
use Einstein’s summation convention, summing over repeated indices

\alpha
and
i
on the right from the second line on. The normal
distribution
\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)=p(z|y)=p_{\xi}(\xi=z-y)
arises as the
conditional of
z
given
y
, which is the probability to have the
right realization of the readout noise
\xi_{\alpha}=z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}
.
Now take the expectation over
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
,
which yields

\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\phi_{\alpha i}\big)\big\rangle_{W}

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\frac{g_{W}}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi{}_{\alpha i}\,\phi_{\beta i}\big),

(4)

where we wrote for short
\phi_{\alpha i}=\phi(h_{\alpha i})
.

The latter term shows by the appearance of the
\sum_{i=1}^{N}
that
all neurons in the hidden layer contribute in a similar manner, so
the neuron identity has been lost here. We note that the terms

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi{}_{\alpha i}\,\phi_{\beta i}=:\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}^{\mathrm{T}},

(5)

have a form as in (
6
) in
Chapter
4
:
We have a sum over
i
and the distribution of
\phi_{\alpha i}\phi_{\beta i}

is i.i.d. over
i
. We hence expect that for large
N
a large deviation
principle holds for each of the
P^{2}
terms
1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq P
.
To exploit this principle, it is convenient to introduce
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}\quad\forall 1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq P

as what are called
auxiliary fields
by enforcing the definition
(
5
) in (
3
) as

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{D}C\,\ldots\prod_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\delta\big[-C_{\alpha\beta}+\frac{g_{w}}{N}\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}^{\mathrm{T}}\big]

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}C\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}\,\ldots\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\,-\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}C_{\alpha\beta}+\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\frac{g_{w}}{N}\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}^{\mathrm{T}}\big),

where
\int\mathcal{D}C=\prod_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}C_{\alpha\beta}

and
\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}=\prod_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\,\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\,\frac{d\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}}{2\pi i}

and
\ldots
may be any function of the matrix
C
, namely

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\int\mathcal{D}C

(6)

\displaystyle\phantom{=\mathcal{N}(y,\kappa;z)\,}\times\exp\Big(\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}C\tilde{y}\Big)

\displaystyle\phantom{=\mathcal{N}(y,\kappa;z)\,}\times\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}\,\exp\big(-\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C+W\,\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)}\big)\Big),

where we defined the cumulant-generating function

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi_{\alpha i}\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,\phi_{\beta i}\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

(7)

\displaystyle=\ln\,\prod_{i=1}^{N}\,\Big\langle\exp\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi_{\alpha i}\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,\phi_{\beta i}\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi_{\alpha}\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,\phi_{\beta}\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

The independence across the
N
neuron indices of the hidden layer
here shows up as the prefactor
N
, analogous to the simple example
in
Section
2
where we considered a sum of independent
variables.

The last line in (
6
) is the Fourier-representation
of the probability distribution for
C
, so we may write it as

\displaystyle p(C)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}\,\exp\big(-\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C+W\,\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)}\big)\Big).

(8)

The second line in (
6
), for
C
given
and fixed, describes a joint Gaussian distribution of
\{y_{\alpha}\}

with covariance
C
, so that in total we get

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,C)\,p(C),

(9)

which is a weighted sum of Gaussian distributions for
y
with different
variances
C
and the explicit form of the Gaussian including the
normalization condition
1/\big[(2\pi)^{N/2}\det(C)^{\frac{1}{2}}\big]

from (
2
).

4
Dominant behavior at large width

The form of the cumulant-generating function (
7
)
has the form of a scaled cumulant-generating function. So we expect
a large deviation principle to apply in the limit
N\to\infty
. To
be more precise, we consider the case where the number of training
samples
P
is kept finite, but the width of the layers
N\to\infty
.
Since
W
in (
7
) has the scaling form, which allows
us to define the scaled cumulant-generating function
\lambda_{N}(k):=N^{-1}\,W(N\,k|C^{(xx)})
,
which is independent of
N
and hence its limit
\lambda(k):=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lambda_{N}(k)

exists trivially. So from the Gärtner-Ellis theorem in
Chapter
4

we know that we may approximate the distribution
p(C)
defined by
(
8
) with help of the rate function

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tilde{C}}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C-W\,\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)}\big)

(10)

as

\displaystyle\ln\,p(C)

\displaystyle\simeq-\Gamma(C)

(11)

so that one obtains from (
6
)

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,C)\,e^{-\Gamma(C)}.

(12)

Writing all terms in the exponent one has

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}y^{\mathrm{T}}C^{-1}y-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(C)-\Gamma(C)\big).

(13)

The matrix
C\in\mathbb{R}^{P\times P}
has a fixed dimension
P<\infty
.
Assuming its eigenvalues
\lambda_{1\leq\mu\leq P}
, the bilinear form

-\frac{1}{2}y^{\mathrm{T}}C^{-1}y=-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\lambda_{\mu}(v_{\mu}^{\mathrm{T}}y)^{2}=\mathcal{O}(P)
,
because for the orthogonal eigenvectors
v_{\mu}^{\mathrm{T}}v_{\nu}=\delta_{\mu\nu}

of
C
and for
y_{\alpha}=\mathcal{O}(1)
it is
\|y\|^{2}=\mathcal{O}(P)
,
so one has
\mathcal{O}(P)=\|y\|^{2}=\sum_{\mu=1}^{P}\,(v_{\mu}^{\mathrm{T}}y)^{2}
.
The determinant is
\ln\det(C)=\prod_{\mu=1}^{P}\ln(\lambda_{\mu})=\mathcal{O}(P)
.
The rate function (
10
), however, is
\Gamma\propto N
,
because, with the scaled cumulant-generating function
\lambda(k)

we may write
W(\tilde{C})=N\,\lambda(\tilde{C}/N)
. So the rate function

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{C}}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C-N\,\lambda(\tilde{C}/N)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle k=\tilde{C}/N}}{{=}}N\,\sup_{k}\,\mathrm{tr}\,k^{\mathrm{T}}C-\lambda(k)=N\,\gamma(C)=\mathcal{O}(N)

is a product of a function
\gamma
that is independent of
N
and
a prefactor
N
.

Taking a saddle point approximation of the integral
\int\mathcal{D}C
, in
the exponent (
13
) and in the limit
N\to\infty
, the
rate function
\Gamma
dominates its stationary point for
C^{\ast}
,
namely

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\Gamma^{\prime}(C)=\tilde{C},

(14)

where the last equal sign follows from the equation of state (
10
)
so the explicit supremum condition in (
10
) reads

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}C-W^{\prime}(0|C^{(xx)}),

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\,\phi_{\beta}\rangle.

(15)

We do not have to check the second derivative for the stationary point
because
\Gamma
is convex. We may therefore determine the network
prior, so the distribution of
y
and
z
, as

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,C^{\ast}).

(16)

We thus obtain what is known as the
neural network Gaussian
process
(NNGP), because the outputs
\{y_{\alpha}\}
follow a joint
Gaussian distribution.

We recapitulate the important steps which led to this result, because
in the following we will repeat the very same steps for different
architectures:

•

enforce the equations defining the network input-output mapping with
help of Dirac distributions

•

compute the expectation value over the prior of the weights to obtain
an expression for the joint distribution of
z
and
y

•

quantities appear which are summed over all neurons in the hidden
layer (in this case the matrix
C_{\alpha\beta}
); introduce these
as auxiliary variables, because we expect these to show a concentration
phenomenon, namely they will be dominated by their mean and fluctuations
will be small in the large
N
limit

•

write the network outputs
y
and
z
as a conditional distribution,
conditioned in
C
, multiplied by
p(C)

•

approximate
p(C)
for large
N
by the Gärtner-Ellis theorem

•

compute the integral over
C
in saddle point approximation; in the
NNGP limit
N\to\infty
,
P=\mathrm{const}.
this amounts to neglecting
the data term in favor of the term
\Gamma(C)
from the prior

5
Mean and covariance of the NNGP predictor

Since we have obtained a Gaussian process (
16
), as
in the case of the Bayesian treatment of linear regression, when conditioning
on the training data by fixing the
z
, the mean and covariance for
a test point is the same as in linear regression (
18
)

\displaystyle y_{\ast}\sim

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}\Big(C_{\ast\circ}[C_{\circ\circ}+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}z_{\circ},

(17)

\displaystyle\phantom{\mathcal{N}(}C_{\ast\ast}-C_{\ast\circ}[C_{\circ\circ}+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}C_{\circ\ast}\Big).

The covariance matrices arising here result from the
y
and
z

following a joint Gaussian distribution, with
z_{\alpha}=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}
,
so that

\displaystyle\langle y_{\ast}z_{\circ}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle y_{\ast}(y_{\circ}+\xi_{\circ})\rangle=\langle y_{\ast}y_{\circ}\rangle=C_{\ast\circ},

\displaystyle\langle y_{\ast}y_{\ast}\rangle

\displaystyle=C_{\ast\ast},

\displaystyle\langle z_{\circ}z_{\circ}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle(y_{\circ}+\xi_{\circ})\,(y_{\circ}+\xi_{\circ})\rangle=C_{\circ\circ}+\kappa\,\mathbb{I},

where we used that the readout noise
\xi_{\alpha}
is independent
of
y_{\alpha}
and independent across different
\alpha
.

6
Multi-layer perceptrons – deep networks

Figure 1:
Sketch of a deep network with input
x
,
L+1
hidden layers
h^{(0)},\ldots,h^{(L)}

and a scalar output
y
.

The treatment shown for a network with a single hidden layer can be
extended straight forwardly to deep networks (DNN), which have many
intermediate hidden layers. To keep the notation simple we here treat
the case of identical width in each layer; the extension to different
widths is easy and can be looked up in
[
65
]
. Likewise,
the treatment of bias terms, additive contributions to the pre-activations
is treated there and will be considered in the exercises.

Consider a DNN with
L
inner layers as specified in
[
65
]

as

\displaystyle h^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\,x,

(18)

\displaystyle h^{(a)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W^{(a)}\phi(h^{(a-1)})\quad 1\leq a\leq L,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h^{(L)}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y+\xi,

where
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
h\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
and
y\in\mathbb{R}
. We again assume
Gaussian priors
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/d)

and
W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
. Due to the
Gaussian i.i.d. prior on
V
one again has that

\displaystyle h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. in }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}),

where
C^{(xx)}
is given as before by (
2
).

The distribution of outputs
z
and readouts
y
, given the inputs

X
, is computed completely analogously as in the single hidden case
by enforcing (
18
) for each layer as

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y;\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}h^{(1\leq a\leq L)}\,\big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\big[y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(L)})\big]\big\rangle_{w}

(19)

\displaystyle\times\prod_{a=1}^{L}\,\big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[h_{\alpha}^{(a)}-W^{(a)}\,\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(a-1)})\big]\big\rangle_{W^{(a)},h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. in }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)})}.

We here used that the weights are drawn i.i.d. across layers, so that
the expectation values factorize over layers.

Each factor

\displaystyle p(h^{(a)}|h^{(a-1)})=

\displaystyle\big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[h_{\alpha}^{(a)}-W^{(a)}\,\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(a-1)})\big]\big\rangle_{W^{(a)}}

has the meaning of a conditional distribution, so that one may interpret
(
19
) as a chain of conditional probabilities, marginalized
(integrated) over all hidden layers

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y;\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}h^{(0\leq a\leq L)}\,p(y|h^{(L)})\,p(h^{(L)}|h^{(L-1)})\cdots p(h^{(1)}|h^{(0)})\,p(h^{(0)}|C^{(xx)}).

Resolving the Dirac distributions with their Fourier representation
as in (
3
), we compute the expectations
over all weights. Consider some pair of intermediate layers, coupled
by the matrix
W^{(a)}

\displaystyle p(h^{(a)}|h^{(a-1)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{h}\,\big\langle\exp\big(-\sum_{\alpha,i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}+\sum_{\alpha,i,j}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}W_{ij}^{(a)}\phi_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\big)\big\rangle_{W_{ij}^{(a)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. in }ij}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)}.

(20)

We hence need

\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha ij}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}W_{ij}^{(a)}\phi_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\big)\big\rangle_{W_{ij}^{(a)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. in }ij}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)}

(21)

\displaystyle=\prod_{ij}\,\Big\{\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}w\,\phi_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\big)\big\rangle_{w\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)}\Big\},
