\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha,i,j}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}W_{ij}^{(a)}\phi_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\big)\big\rangle_{W_{ij}^{(a)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. in }ij}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)}

where we used the independence of
W_{ij}
over both indices to factorize
the expectation value into
N^{2}
expectations over a univariate
Gaussian
w\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
. Taking this Gaussian integral one
again notices that this computation corresponds to the one obtaining
the moment-generating function of the univariate Gaussian variable

w
, so

\displaystyle=\prod_{i,j}\,\Big\{\,\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}\tilde{h}_{\beta i}\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\phi_{\beta j}^{(a-1)}\big)\Big\}

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\,\sum_{i}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}\tilde{h}_{\beta i}\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{j}\phi{}_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\,\phi_{\beta j}^{(a-1)}\big).

The appearance of the sum over
i
shows that the statistics of
h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}

is independent across
i
. The appearance of the sum
j
shows that
the problem becomes symmetric in the index of the sendind neuron indices.
To deal with the term coupling four variables
\tilde{h}\tilde{h}\phi\phi
, we
introduce auxiliary fields, analogous to (
5
)

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}:=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi{}_{\alpha j}^{(a-1)}\,\phi_{\beta j}^{(a-1)}=:\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi_{\alpha}^{(a-1)}\cdot\phi_{\beta}^{(a-1)}\,\quad 1\leq a\leq L,\;1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq P,

(22)

where we use
\cdot
to denote the summation over the
N
neuron
indices. This allows us to write (
20
) as

\displaystyle p(h^{(a)}|C^{(a)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{h}\,\exp\big(-\sum_{\alpha i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}+\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha}C^{(a)}\tilde{h}_{\beta}\big)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(\{h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}\}|0,\delta_{ij}C_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}).

So conditioned on the value of the auxiliary field
C^{(a)}
, the

h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}
are Gaussian, which are independent across neuron
indices
i
, but correlated across sample indices
\alpha
with

C_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}
.

The definition of the field (
22
)is enforced
by conjugate fields
\tilde{C}_{1\leq\alpha\beta\leq P}^{(1\leq a\leq L)}

to obtain

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}

\displaystyle\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}C^{(L+1)}\tilde{y}\big)\,p(C|C^{(xx)})

,

(23)

where the distribution of the
C
is of the form of a chain of conditional
distribution is given by

\displaystyle p(C^{(0\leq a\leq L+1)}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\prod_{a=1}^{L+1}\,P(C^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})\Big|_{C^{(0)}=C^{(xx)}}

(24)

\displaystyle P(C^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}^{(a)}\,\exp\Big(-\mathrm{tr}\tilde{C}^{(a)}C^{(a)}+W(\tilde{C}^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})\Big)

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\Big\langle\exp\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\phi^{(a-1)\mathrm{T}}\tilde{C}\phi^{(a-1)}\big)\Big\rangle_{h^{(a-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(a-1)})},

(25)

where the independence of the
h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}
in the index
i

yields the factor
N
in the cumulant-generation function
W
, in
the same way as in (
7
).

7
Behavior of deep networks at large width

At large network width, due to the independence across neuron indices
in each layer we have obtained the scaling form of the cumulant-generating
function in (
25
), so the scaled cumulant-generating
function
\lambda_{N}(k):=N^{-1}W(N\,k|C^{(a-1)})=\ln\Big\langle\exp\big(g_{w}\phi^{(a-1)\mathrm{T}}\tilde{C}\phi^{(a-1)}\big)\Big\rangle_{h^{(a-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(a-1)})}

trivially possesses the limit
N\to\infty
(because it is independent
of
N
), so one may compute
\ln\,P(C^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})
on exponential
scales in large deviation theory to obtain

\displaystyle\ln\,P(C^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})

\displaystyle\simeq-\Gamma(C^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{C}^{(a)}}-\mathrm{tr}\tilde{C}^{(a)}C^{(a)}+W(\tilde{C}^{(a)}|C^{(a-1)}).

Performing the same approximation as in (
4
)
one then obtains the NNGP result for the deep network from the above
supremum condition

\displaystyle\bar{C}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=W^{(1)}(0|\bar{C}^{(a-1)})

(26)

\displaystyle=g_{w}\langle\phi^{(a-1)}\phi^{(a-1)}\rangle_{h^{(a-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{C}^{(a-1)})}\quad\forall 1\leq a\leq L+1

with the initial condition
C^{(0)}=C^{(xx)}

[
21
]
. The
mean of the predictor is then given by (
17
),
only replacing
\bar{C}=C^{(L+1)}
. The iteration of these kernels
is shown in an example in
Figure
2
.
The accuracy of predicting the performance of a trained neuronal network
compared to the Gaussian process, using for the test point the prediction
(
17
) is shown in
[
66
]
for different
activation functions (ReLU
\phi(x)=H(x)\,x
,
\phi(x)=\tanh(x)
)
and for different data sets (MNIST: handwritten digits
0-9
; CIFAR
10: classification of
10
image categories). As the width of the
fully connected networks increases, the prediction of the Gaussian
process for the observed performance by tendency becomes better, as
expected from the limit
N\to\infty
.

Figure 2:
Neural Network Gaussian Process (NNGP) for erf-activation
function.
Display of the diagonal
C_{\alpha\alpha}
and off-diagonal

C_{\alpha\beta}
elements of NNGP kernel
a)
Dependence
of output variance
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(a)}
for different depths.

b)
Fixpoint values of variance for different hidden gain
values
g
.
c)
Input weight variance initialization determined
such that one obtains a fixed point
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(a)}
for
different bias values.
d)
Output covariance
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}

as a function of input covariance
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}
with
g_{v}=0.1
.

e)
Output covariance
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(a)}
as a function
of input covariance
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}
with
g_{v}
set so
that
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(a)}
is initialized at the fixpoint.
f)

Same setting as in e) for different values of the bias variance
g_{b}

and for
5
layers. All results are produced for
\phi=\mathrm{erf}

and with a regularization variance of
\kappa=1
.

8
Keypoints

We can summarize this chapter with the following points

Neural Network Gaussian Processes

•

Network outputs of shallow and deep networks are distributed according
to a Gaussian process for infinitely wide networks at finite amounts
of training data, which is referred to as the Neural Network Gaussian
process (NNGP).

•

The form of the NNGP kernel depends on the architecture and hyper
parameter settings of the network.

•

In the NNGP limit, the preactivations
h
of each neuron within a
given layer are statistical independent across neuron index and identically
Gaussian distributed.

•

The NNGP kernel allows us to obtain the mean and the covariance of
the network output on test and training points.

•

The presented field theoretic description will in the following chapters
allow us to systematically go beyond the
N\rightarrow\infty
case.

9
Exercises

a) Adding a bias term

In this exercise, we will investigate how the addition of an external
drive to a single hidden-layer network changes the distribution of
its outputs. The external drive is implemented by adding bias term

a,b
to the fields
h_{\alpha}\to h_{\alpha}+a
and to the output

y_{\alpha}\to y_{\alpha}+b
. We are interested in how this affects
the statistics of the network output. To this end, consider the network

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=Vx_{\alpha}+a

(27)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi\left(h_{\alpha}\right)+b

(28)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}\quad\xi_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa),

(29)

with
h_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
y_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}
,
x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/d)
,
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
,

a_{j}\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}\left(0,g_{a}\right)
,

b\overset{\mathrm{i.i.d.}}{\sim}\mathcal{N}\left(0,g_{b}\right)
.
First, How does the addition of the bias term
a
change the kernel

C^{(xx)}
and consequently the distribution of the fields
h_{\alpha i}
?
To this end, first compute the cumulants of
h_{\alpha i}
and argue
which cumulants dominate the distribution in the large
M
limit.

Second, do a more formal approach utilizing the disorder average by
computing

First, how does the addition of the bias term
a
a change
the kernel
C^{(xx)}
and, consequently, the distribution of the
fields
h_{\alpha i}
? To investigate this, first compute the cumulants
of
h_{\alpha i}
and argue which cumulants dominate the distribution
in the large
M
limit.

Second, take a more formal approach by utilizing the average over
network parameters by computing

p(h|X)=\Big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[h_{\alpha}-Vx_{\alpha}-a\big]\Big\rangle_{V_{ij},a_{i}}.

Does the distribution match your expectations? Now, consider the additional
bias term
b
. Once again, first compute the cumulants of
y_{\alpha}
,
and then apply the disorder average by starting from

p(z,y|C^{(xx)})=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\big\langle\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\,\big[y_{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})-b\big]\big\rangle_{w_{i},h_{\alpha i},b}.

How could you have obtained these modifications more easily in the
special case of
g_{a}=g_{V}/N
and
g_{b}=g_{w}/M
?

b) NNGPs for different weight initializations

In conventional machine learning frameworks such as TensorFlow, Keras,
or PyTorch you’ll find standard weight initializations, where the
hidden weights, the input weights and the biases are drawn from Gaussian
distributions. But what would happen, if we draw the weights from
a different distribution? We will try to answer this question in the
NNGP limit of the following network

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}V_{ij}x_{j\alpha}

(30)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}w_{i}\phi\left(h_{i\alpha}\right)

(31)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}

(32)

First show that equation 6.3 in the main text is valid if the elements
of
V_{ij}
are i.i.d. distributed according to a Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa_{2}^{(v)}/N_{\mathrm{in}})
.
Comment on the correlation of the different dimensions of the hidden
units. Show that we can write

\displaystyle p(z_{\alpha})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,dh\,\mathcal{D}w\,\exp\big(\tilde{y}_{\alpha}y_{\alpha}-\tilde{y}_{\alpha}w_{j}\phi(h_{j\alpha})\big)\,p(h)\,,

(33)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}

\displaystyle\propto\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)});\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}=\frac{\kappa_{2}^{(v)}}{N_{in}}x_{\alpha}^{\top}x_{\beta}\,.

(34)

We now want to consider the case where the
w_{i}
are distributed
independently according to a uniform distribution with
w_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}[-g/\sqrt{N},g/\sqrt{N}]
.
Compute the cumulant generating function of
w_{i}

\displaystyle Z(j)

\displaystyle=\int dw\,\mathrm{exp}(jw_{i})\,p(w_{i})

(35)

\displaystyle W(j)

\displaystyle=\ln\,Z(j)

(36)

and comment on the scaling of the cumulants with
N
that you would
expect from the structure of
W(j)
. Compute the first four cumulants

\kappa_{l}=\left[\partial^{l}W(j)/\partial j^{l}\right]|_{j=0},l=1,\ldots,4
.
Hint: It is a bit easier to simply compute moments and then to deduce
the cumulants explicitly from the moments rather than computing them
from the CGF. Compute the average over
w_{i}
in
p(z_{\alpha})
,
using the relation
p(w_{i})=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{dj_{i}}{2hi}\exp(-j_{i}w_{i})\exp(W(j_{i}))
.
Show that the fourth cumulant
\kappa_{4}
only creates a subleading
quartic term in the action which vanishes if
N\rightarrow\infty
.
What is the consequence for the behavior of two networks, where network
1 has a Gaussian initialization
w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g^{2}/N)

and network 2 has a uniform weight initialization
w_{i}\sim\mathcal{U}(-g/\sqrt{N},g/\sqrt{N})
.

c) Ising spin task and kernel structure

The structure of the kernel provides insights into the structure of
a given dataset. In this exercise, we investigate an example of a
block-like structure in the kernel. This block structure may arise
due to the presence of two different data classes: the overlap between
samples within the same class is larger than the overlap between samples
from different classes. If the samples are sorted according to class
membership, the block structure becomes apparent (see
Figure
3
).

Consider the (artificial) data-set
\{x_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq D}

consisting of vectors
x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
of two classes with
class-labels
z_{\alpha}\in\{-1,1\}
.

For the first class (
z_{\alpha}=1
), the entries of the vectors
are identically and independently distributed (over data realizations

\alpha
and neurons
i
) as

x_{\alpha i}=\begin{cases}1&\text{with probability }p\\
-1&\text{with probability }1-p\end{cases}

and for the second class (
z_{\alpha}=-1
)

x_{\alpha i}=\begin{cases}-1&\text{with probability }p\\
1&\text{with probability }1-p\end{cases}

In other words, the two classes differ by their average spin magnetization.
Compute the mean
\mu_{\alpha\beta}=\langle K_{\alpha\beta}\rangle

and covariance
\Sigma_{(\alpha\beta)(\alpha\delta)}=\langle(K_{\alpha\beta}-\mu_{\alpha\beta})(K_{\alpha\delta}-\mu_{\alpha\delta})\rangle

of the overlap (kernel)

K_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{\alpha i}x_{\beta i}.

Convince yourself that the mean can only take on three different values
while the covariance
\Sigma_{(\alpha\beta)(\alpha\delta)}
can only
take on two different values for
\alpha,\beta,\delta
being three
different indices. What happens for the covariances
\Sigma_{(\alpha\alpha)(\gamma\delta)}
,

\Sigma_{(\alpha\beta)(\alpha\beta)}
or
\Sigma_{(\alpha\beta)(\gamma\delta)}
?
The overlap within vectors of the same/different class are also referred
to as intra/inter-class distances.

Figure 3:
Data samples and dot-product kernel for the Ising spin task

9
. a)
Two data samples
x_{\alpha}

for each class. Left two vectors:
z_{\alpha}=1
. Right two vectors:

z_{\alpha}=-1
.
p=0.7
.
b)
Two realizations of the corresponding
dot-product kernels
K_{\alpha\beta}\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times D}
showing
a block-like structure.
D=40
. The covariance
\Sigma_{(\alpha\beta)(\alpha\delta)}

captures the variability between specific entries (i.e. between
(\alpha\beta)

and
(\alpha\delta)
) across different realizations of the kernel.

d) Regular activity and vanishing prediction with homogeneous kernel

Assume a data set
\{(x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq D}

in which there are two classes of vectors,
x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

belonging to classes
z_{\alpha}\in\{-1,1\}
. As in the Ising spin
task above, we assume a bipartite classification task, in which the
kernel exhibits a block-like structure. In the limit of very many
nodes
N\to\infty
the fluctuations around the values characterizing
each block diminish. In this limit, we can assume that the overlaps
are given by a matrix with three distinct values

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}x_{\alpha i}x_{\beta i}

\displaystyle\simeq\,\begin{cases}1&\alpha=\beta\\
a&\alpha\neq\beta,\,z_{\alpha}=z_{\beta}\\
b&\alpha\neq\beta,\,z_{\alpha}\neq z_{\beta}\end{cases},

where
0\leq b\leq a\leq 1
. The amount of training data
D
is even
and the first
D/2
samples are of class
z_{\alpha}=1
, the second

D/2
samples are of class
z_{\beta}=-1
.

Compute the mean
\mu
of the predictive distribution in linear regression

z=w^{\mathrm{T}}x+\xi
with
\xi\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
and
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
.
Reminder: The predictive mean
\mu
is given by

\mu=C_{*\circ}\left(C_{\circ\circ}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\right)^{-1}z_{\circ}

with the kernel

C_{\alpha\beta}=\frac{g_{w}}{N}x_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{T}}x_{\beta},

such that

\displaystyle\left(C_{\circ\circ}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\right)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\simeq g_{w}\,\begin{cases}1+\kappa/g_{w}&\alpha=\beta\\
a&\alpha\neq\beta,\,z_{\alpha}=z_{\beta}\\
b&\alpha\neq\beta,\,z_{\alpha}\neq z_{\beta}\end{cases}.

The remaining matrix for the mean
\mu
is

\displaystyle\left(C_{*\circ}\right)_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\simeq g_{w}\,\begin{cases}a&z_{\alpha}=z_{\beta}\\
b&z_{\alpha}\neq z_{\beta}\end{cases}.

This matrix
C_{*\circ}
can only take on two possible values, since
no training point is included in the test set
*\neq\circ
.

Convince yourself that
\mu
can only assume two different values.
To this end consider the training labels to be
z_{\circ}=(-1,\ldots,-1,1,\ldots 1)^{\mathrm{T}}
.
Which
a
maximizes
\mu
? What happens if
a=b
?

Hint:
To invert a matrix of a form
A=\mathbb{I}+B
, where
B

is a two by two block matrix with identical entries on the two diagonal
blocks and also identical entries in the two off-diagonal blocks,
one may use that one can write
B=c\,vv^{\mathrm{T}}+d\,ww^{\mathrm{T}}
, with vectors

v=(1,\ldots,1)^{\mathrm{T}}
and
w=(-1,\ldots,-1,1,\ldots 1)^{\mathrm{T}}
. Then
use that
v
and
w
are mutually orthogonal eigenvectors of
B

and hence of
A
and all other eigenvalues must vanish for the rank
two matrix
B
. Decomposing any vector in such basis allows the computation
of the inverse of
A
.

Bonus: e) Simple convolutional layers

A prominent architecture in image processing is the so called convolutional
layer. Those kinds of layers are particularly useful when dealing
with image processing tasks. To get a better understanding of these
layers we consider the setup

h_{i}=\sum_{j}V_{ij}x_{j},\,\mathrm{with\>}h_{i},x_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},V_{ij}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}.

(37)

We now assume that the weights
V_{ij}
only depend on the difference
of the indices
i,j
and hence
V_{ij}=v_{i-j}
. Defining
k=i-j\in[-N,N]

we assume that

v_{k}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{v}^{2}/N)\quad\mathrm{for\quad}k\in[-N,N]\,.

(38)

Compute the covariance function
\langle h_{i}h_{k}\rangle_{V}
.
What are differences to an i.i.d. Gaussian initialization of
V_{ij}
.
Now assume that the inputs are distributed as

\langle x_{i}x_{j}\rangle_{x}=\exp\left[-\frac{1}{2\zeta^{2}}(i-j)^{2}\right]

(39)

What is the data averaged covariance function
\langle h_{i}h_{k}\rangle_{V,x}
?
What happens when you send
\zeta\rightarrow 0
?

Now we consider a so called pooling layer, which aggregates different
parts of the input and is also an essential building block in machine
learning architectures. We define it as

\displaystyle h_{i}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{j}K_{ij}x_{j}

(40)

\displaystyle\mathrm{choose\,}K\,\mathrm{so\,that:}h_{i}

\displaystyle=\alpha_{i}\sum_{j=(i-w)\mod N}^{(i+w)\mod N}x_{j},\quad\alpha_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{a}^{2}),

(41)

where we call
w
is the window size and we use periodic boundary
conditions (
j:=j\,\mod{\,}N
). Show how the structure of the matrix

K_{ij}
looks like for
w=1
. Compute the variance
\langle h_{i}^{\alpha}h_{i}^{\beta}\rangle_{K,x}

and covariance
\langle h_{i}^{\alpha}h_{k\neq i}^{\beta}\rangle_{K,x}

with the assumption that the input data is i.i.d. distributed
x_{i\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma_{x}^{2}
)
and for a general value of
w
.

Chapter 6
Recurrent networks

Recurrent networks are networks where the activity of the neurons
evolves over time, so that the activity at time
t
influences the
activity at the next time step
t+1
. These networks intrinsically
implement a fading memory, namely the imprinted input at time
t

will stay present for some time in the system. Such networks are therefore
useful for tasks that require the processing of temporal sequences
or the classification of temporal signals. In this section we will
develop the field theory of such networks, in complete analogy to
the treatment of deep networks. It will turn out that a particular
form of recurrent networks follows a tightly related mean-field theory
as the one found for deep networks. We here follow the presentation
of
[
67
]
.

1
Recurrent network

Figure 1:
a
Recurrent network with input
x
and a scalar output
y
,
where the activity evolves in discrete time steps
t=0,\ldots,T
.

b
Equivalent representation by “unrolling”
time into
T+1
hidden layers
h^{(0)},\ldots,h^{(T)}
: The recurrent
network may be thought of as a deep network, where the single layer
of neurons that is actually present is copied for each time step
t

and connected to the layer in the next time step
t+1
by the very
same connectivity
W
for all adjacent time steps. This “weight
sharing” over layers will be the cause of correlated
activity across layers.

The setting of a recurrent network (RNN) is very much analogous to
that of the multi-layer perceptron studied in
Section
6
.
The main difference is that there is only a single set of weights

W
involved, which is the same for all time steps. We illustrate
the setting in
Figure
1
, given in equations
as

\displaystyle h^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\,x,

(1)

\displaystyle h^{(t)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W\,\phi(h^{(t-1)})\quad 1\leq t\leq T,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h^{(T)}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y+\xi,

where the input is
x\in\mathbb{R}^{P}
, the single hidden layer is
h\in\mathbb{R}^{N}

and the scalar output
y\in\mathbb{R}
. Here the index
t
plays the role
of a discrete time and we consider the simplest setting that the input
is presented to the network at time
t=0
and the output is obtained
as a scalar readout of the network’s activity at time
t=T
.

2
Recurrent network field theory

We again assume Gaussian priors
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/P)

and
w_{i},W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
. Due
to the Gaussian i.i.d. prior on
V
one again has that

\displaystyle h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P. iP }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}),

where
C^{(xx)}
is given as before by (
2
).

The distribution of outputs
y
under the prior and given the inputs

X
is computed completely analogously as in the case of the deep
network by enforcing the equations of motion for each layer as

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}h^{(1\leq t\leq T)}\,\big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\big[y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(T)})\big]\big\rangle_{w}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle\prod_{t=1}^{T}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[h_{\alpha}^{(t)}-W\,\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(t-1)})\big]\big\rangle_{W,h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P. iP }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)})},

(2)

where the only difference to (
19
) is that the
connectivity matrix
W
is the same for all times and thus does not
carry a superscript
t
. As a consequence, we need to have the product
over
t
inside the expectation value, because the matrix
W
is
the same for all
t
and not independent across different
t
. This
also makes a difference when computing the expectation over these
weights one obtains for the expectation over
w_{i}
the same form
as (
4
) and for those over
W

\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(-\sum_{t=1}^{T}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(t)}W_{ij}\phi_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)}\big)\big\rangle_{W}

\displaystyle=\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{P}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\,\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(t)}\tilde{h}_{\beta i}^{(s)}\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi{}_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)}\,\phi_{\beta j}^{(s-1)}\big),

(3)

where the appearance of the sums over
i
and
j
again shows that
the problem becomes completely symmetric across neuron indices; in
particular, the statistics of
h_{\alpha i}^{(a)}
become independent
across different
i
. We here write
\phi{}_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)}\equiv\phi(h_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)})

for short. A difference to the DNN is, though, that we obtain two
sums, one
\sum_{t}
and
\sum_{s}
, because the
W_{ij}
are correlated
(the same) across all times.

We introduce auxiliary fields, analogous to (
5
),
but now carrying two time indices
t
and
s

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}:=

\displaystyle\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}\phi{}_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)}\,\phi_{\beta j}^{(s-1)}\quad\forall 1\leq\alpha,\beta\leq P\quad 1\leq t,s\leq T

(4)

which we enforce by conjugate fields
\tilde{C}_{1\leq\alpha\beta\leq P}^{(0<t,s\leq T)}

to obtain

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}

\displaystyle\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}C^{(T+1)}\tilde{y}\big)\,P(C|C^{(xx)})

,

(5)

where the distribution of the
C
is of the form

\displaystyle P(C|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{C}\,\exp\big(-\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\mathrm{tr}\tilde{C}^{(t,s)}C^{(t,s)}+W(\tilde{C}|C)\big)\Big|_{C^{(0,0)}=C^{(xx)}}

(6)

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C)

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\tilde{C}^{(t,s)}\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\phi^{(t-1)}\cdot\phi^{(s-1)}\big)\Big\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)},

where the factor
N
again comes from the
N
identical expectation
values over neurons. So given the values of
C
and
\tilde{C}
, the statistics
of the neurons is independent. While (
5
)
has precisely the same form as in the case of the deep network (
23
),
the distribution of the
C
(
6
), in contrast
to (
24
), does not factorize into products
of distributions across layers here. This difference will lead to
non-zero correlations of the activities across layers.

3
Dominant behavior of RNN at large width

At large network width
N\gg 1
and
P=\mathcal{O}(1)
, due to the independence
across neuron indices in each layer, one may compute
P(C|C^{(1)})

in large deviation theory to obtain

\displaystyle\ln\,P(C|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq-\Gamma(C|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{C}}-\mathrm{tr}\sum_{t,s=1}^{T}\tilde{C}^{(t,s)}C^{(t,s)}+W(\tilde{C}|C).

Performing the same approximation as in (
4
)
one then obtains the NNGP result for the deep network from the above
supremum condition

\displaystyle C^{\ast(t,s)}

\displaystyle=W^{(1)}(0|C^{\ast(t-1,s-1)})

(7)

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{(t-1)}\phi^{(s-1)}\rangle_{h^{(t-1)},h^{(s-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast(t-1,s-1)})}\quad\forall 1\leq t,s\leq T+1

with the initial condition
C^{(0,0)}=C^{(xx)}
. The mean and variance
of the predictor are then given by (
17
), only
replacing
C^{\ast}=C^{\ast(T+1,T+1)}
.

We note that the correlation
C^{\ast(T+1,T+1)}
by (
7
)
only depends on all preceding equal-time covariance matrices
C^{\ast(t,t)}
,
but not on the covariances between different time points
C^{\ast(t,s)}

for
t\neq s
. Also the iteration (
7
)
for
t=s
is identical to the iteration (
26
),
including the initial condition. We thus conclude that the NNGP result
for a deep network is identical to the one of a recurrent network,
if one only supplies the input to time step
0
and reads out the
readout from the activity at some final time point
T
which corresponds
to the depth of the deep network
L
.

Still, the iteration (
7
) predicts non-vanishing
values for the covariances between activations across layers in general.
This is because one may solve the iteration for
C^{\ast(2,1)}
,

C^{\ast(3,1)}
,
\ldots
. For the first, one obtains
C^{\ast(2,1)}=g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{(1)}\phi^{(0)}\rangle_{h^{(1)},h^{(0)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast(1,0)})}
.
So if the expectation value
\langle\phi^{(1)}\phi^{(0)}\rangle_{h^{(1)},h^{(0)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast(1,0)})}\neq 0
,
for example for a ReLU activation function, this generates covariances
between adjacent layers which propagate through the iterative equation
(
7
). For point-symmetric non-linearities

\phi
, however, the expectation value
\langle\phi^{(1)}\phi^{(0)}\rangle_{h^{(1)},h^{(0)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast(1,0)})}=0
,
because
h_{0}
and
h_{1}
will be uncorrelated, since
V
and

W
are uncorrelated. In this case, correlations across different
time steps vanish. By induction this holds for all correlations that
are not at equal time points. A comparison of these correlations between
DNN and RNN is shown in
Figure
2
.

Figure 2:
Mean-field theory for DNN and RNN with a single input. a)

Average variance in mean-field theory
\overline{C}{}^{(a,a)}
(mean
field theory; solid gray curve) and estimate
\frac{1}{P_{a}}\sum_{i}h_{i}^{(a)}h_{i}^{(a)}

from simulation, averaged over
100
realizations of networks, for
biases that are uncorrelated across time/layers (blue crosses RNN;
orange dots DNN).
b)
Cross-covariance
\overline{C}{}^{(a,b)}

as a function of the hidden layer index
a
for fixed
b\in\{10,30\}

and uncorrelated biases. RNN: Mean-field theory (solid dark blue and
dark magenta). Mean (blue / purple dots) and standard error of the
mean (light blue / light purple tube) of
\frac{1}{P_{a}}\sum_{i}h_{i}^{(a)}h_{i}^{(b)}

estimated from simulation of
100
network realizations. DNN: Mean
(orange dots) and standard error of the mean of
\frac{1}{P_{a}}\sum_{i}h_{i}^{(a)}h_{i}^{(b)}

estimated from simulation of
100
network realizations. Other parameters

g_{0}^{2}=g^{2}=1.6
,
\sigma^{2}=0.2
, finite layer width
P_{a}=2000
,

A=30
hidden layers, ReLU activation
\phi(x)=\max(0,x)
and Gaussian
inputs
x\overset{\text{i.i.P.}}{\sim}\mathcal{P}(1,1)
with

P_{\text{iP}}=10^{5}
.
c)
Same as a) but for biases that
are static across time/layers.
d)
Same as b) but for the
static bias case.

4
Chaos transition and depth scales

The propagation of the cross covariance over time described by the
iteration (
7
) has an interesting interpretation
in terms of the network dynamics
[
35
]
. A dynamical
system may show the property of
chaos
. This term refers to
the tendency that a pair of initial states of the same network depart
from one another over time, as illustrated in
Figure
3

(middle): two initial states of the system may either increase their
distance as the dynamics evolves, corresponding to chaotic dynamics
or they may converge to the same state, corresponding to regular dynamics.
The mathematical definition is given below in terms of the Lyapunov
exponent.

Figure 3:
Chaos in recurrent network.
Different initial conditions
(left, here illustrated in two-dimensional space) lead to different
trajectories in the state space of the networks (right). Middle: Illustration
of chaotic and of regular dynamics: For chaotic dynamics, a pair of
initially nearly trajectories separates as a function of time (red).
For regular dynamics, the pair of initially nearby trajectories converges
to the same trajectory after some time (green).

To quantitatively asses chaos, we may alternatively interpret the
correlation between two data points
\alpha
and
\beta
as the
expected Euclidean distance
P(t)
between a pair of networks, each
initialized in a different initial state

\displaystyle P(t)

\displaystyle:=\langle\|\phi_{\alpha}^{(t)}-\phi_{\beta}^{(t)}\|^{2}\rangle_{W}

(8)

\displaystyle=\langle\|\phi_{\alpha}^{(t)}\|^{2}\rangle-2\langle\phi_{\alpha}^{(t)}\cdot\phi_{\beta}^{(t)}\rangle+\langle\|\phi_{\beta}^{(t)}\|^{2}\rangle

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\gg 1}}{{=}}N\,g_{w}^{-1}\,\big(C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t,t)}-2\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,t)}+C_{\beta\beta}^{(t,t)}\big).

The three different quantities appearing here each evolve according
to the iteration (
7
). In the following,
we write
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t)}\equiv C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,t)}
for
short. A particular situation that may appear is that the diagonal
elements reach fixed points
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)}=C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}\quad\forall\,t
.
The fixed point condition is given by

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast})}.

(9)

One may ask on which time-scale such a fixed point is reached. To
this end one may linearize the iteration about the fixed point and
investigate the evolution of the discrepancy
\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)}

to the fixed point which obeys

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}+\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}+\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)})}.

To make progress, one may linearize the function
f(\delta C):=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast}+\delta C)}

\displaystyle f(\delta C)

\displaystyle:=f(0)+f^{\prime}(0)\,\delta C+\mathcal{O}(\delta C^{2})

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}+\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta C}\,g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}+\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)})}\big|_{\delta C=0}\,\delta C+\mathcal{O}(\delta C^{2}),

so that the dynamics of the deviation becomes a linear iteration

\displaystyle\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=f^{\prime}(0)\,\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)},

(10)

which has the discrete exponential function as a solution

\displaystyle\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}\,(f^{\prime})^{t}

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}\,\exp\big(t\,\ln f^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}^{(0)}\,e^{-t/\tau_{\alpha\alpha}}

so one obtains an effective time-scale

\displaystyle\tau_{\alpha\alpha}

\displaystyle=-\big[\ln f^{\prime}\big]^{-1}.

One may use Price’s theorem (see
Section
6
) to
write
f^{\prime}(0)=g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{\prime\prime}\phi\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}+g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}
.
For an error function as the activation
\phi(x)=\mathrm{erf}(x)
we get
with (
64
)
\langle\phi\phi\rangle=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\arcsin\big(\frac{\Sigma}{1+\Sigma}\big)

whose derivative by
\Sigma=C^{\ast}
yields
f^{\prime}
.

Since the NNGP for RNN and DNN is identical, this temporal scale corresponds
to a depth-scale for the DNN. It shows how quickly different values
of the initial covariance converge to the fixed point value
C^{\ast}
.

Analogously one may consider the off-diagonal elements of the covariances.
To this end, assume that the diagonal elements are at their fixed
point values
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}
given by (
9
).
The off-diagonal elements then as well obey a condition for a fixed
point value which has the same form

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h_{\alpha},h_{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}.

Since the form of the equation is the same as (
9
),
also one of the fixed point solutions is identical

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}.

This fixed point therefore corresponds to perfect correlation between
the two copies of the system and, by (
8
), to a vanishing
distance. There may be a second fixed point, though, which for an
odd activation function is

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{\ast}

\displaystyle=0,

because then
C_{\alpha\beta}^{\ast}=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h_{\alpha},h_{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}\big|_{C_{\alpha\beta}^{\ast}=0}=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}}\,\langle\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h_{\beta}}=0
.
To find out which of the two fixed points is approached, one again
may perform a stability analysis by considering the linearized dynamics.
Investigating the stability of the perfectly correlated fixed point,
for example, one needs to study small departures
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t)}=C_{\alpha\alpha}^{\ast}-\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t)}

which then obey, analogously to (
10
), the linear
iterative equation

\displaystyle\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=g^{\prime}(0)\,\delta C^{(t)},

\displaystyle g(\delta C)

\displaystyle:=g_{w}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h_{\alpha},h_{\beta}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast}-\delta C^{(t)})},

where
g^{\prime}(0)=\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta C_{\alpha\beta}}g(\delta C)\big|_{\delta C=0}
.
This leads to an exponential solution

\displaystyle\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t)}

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}\,(g^{\prime})^{t}

(11)

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}\,\exp\big(t\,\ln g^{\prime}\big)

\displaystyle=\delta C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}\,\exp\big(-t/\tau_{\alpha\beta}\big),

\displaystyle\tau_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=-\big[\ln g^{\prime}\big]^{-1},

where we defined a time scale
\tau_{\alpha\beta}
. For
g^{\prime}>1

the exponent in (
11
) is hence positive, so the
departure of the two copies of the system and hence their distance
grows; the system is chaotic, small initial differences are amplified.
The rate of exponential growth is also defined as the Lyapunov exponent,
which considers the growth of the distance
\sqrt{P(t)}
in the limit
of large times and for initially infinitesimal distance, namely

\displaystyle\lambda

\displaystyle:=\lim_{\delta C(0)\searrow 0}\,\lim_{t\to\infty}\,\frac{1}{2t}\,\ln\,\frac{\delta C(t)}{\delta C(0)}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\ln g^{\prime}.

If the fixed point with vanishing correlation
C_{\alpha\beta}=0

is stable and the only additional fixed point besides the trivial
one
C_{\alpha\beta}=C_{\alpha\alpha}
, then the system will approach
it over time; the states are completely uncorrelated ultimately, the
system is chaotic.

For
g^{\prime}<1
, the exponent is negative, so the initial discrepancy
declines, the system is regular; this means that if one waits long
enough, the states of any pair of systems will be identical. In particular
the initial condition and hence the presented data has no influence
on the state anymore and is thus lost. The phase diagram of a recurrent
network is shown in
Figure
4
and
Figure
5
.

A qualitative difference between the two time scales
\tau_{\alpha\alpha}

and
\tau_{\alpha\beta}
is seen in
Figure
6
:
The depth scale for the off-diagonal elements
\tau_{\alpha\beta}

diverges at the point where
g^{\prime}
exceeds unity. The depth
scale for the diagonal elements, in contrast, peaks, but stays finite;
this implies that
f^{\prime}
does not exceed unity. The difference
can be understood with help of Price’s theorem (see
Section
6
),
which allows us to write

\displaystyle f^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta C_{\alpha\alpha}}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\alpha}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast}+\delta C)}\Big|_{\delta C=0}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{\prime\prime}\phi\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}+g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}

\displaystyle g^{\prime}(0)

\displaystyle=-g_{w}\,\frac{\partial}{\partial\delta C_{\alpha\beta}}\,\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast}-\delta C)}\Big|_{\delta C=0}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\langle\phi^{\prime}\phi^{\prime}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}.

The additional term appearing for
f^{\prime}
is
\langle\phi^{\prime\prime}\phi\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{\ast})}<0
,
for the point-symmetric non-linearity (
\phi=\tanh
) considered in
the figure. Another way to see that
f^{\prime}(0)<1
is required
by the stability of the fixed point for the diagonal elements. If

f^{\prime}(0)>1
, (
10
) would show that
the fixed point is unstable. For the point-symmetric activation function
that saturates at
\pm 1
, such as
\tanh
or
\mathrm{erf}
, the fixed
point for the diagonal elements is always stable, because the second
moment
\langle\phi^{2}\rangle\leq 1
by the saturation of
\sup_{h}|\phi(h)|=1
,
so the variance
C_{\alpha\alpha}\leq g_{w}
, it cannot grow indefinitely.
This is different for non-bounded activation functions, such as ReLU,

\phi(h)=x\,H(x)
, for which the variance may diverge to infinity
with increasing network depth (DNN) or time (RNN).

Figure 4:
Lyapunov exponent
\lambda
in a recurrent network. a
depicts
the magnitude of
\lambda
while
b
depicts the sign of
\lambda
.
The red curve is the theoretically predicted transition line where
the exponent changes sign. Here
g_{w}/N
is the standard deviation
of the Gaussian i.i.d. weights
W_{ij}
and
g_{b}
is the variance
of the bias term. Activation function
\phi=\tanh
and network width

N=1000
used in simulation (right panel). (Adapted from Bachelor
thesis Bastian Epping, 2020.)

Figure 5:
Depth scales of information propagation in a deep network.
a
Phase diagram with regular and chaotic phase. Here
\sigma_{w}^{2}

is the variance of the weight prior,
\sigma_{b}^{2}
the variance
of the prior of the biases.
b
Approach of diagonal elements
of kernel
C_{\alpha\alpha}
towards fixed point
. c
Approach
of off-diagonal elements of kernel
C_{\alpha\beta}
to fixed point.
Activation function
\phi=\tanh
. (Adapted from
[
33
]
)

Figure 6:
Depth scales of information propagation in a deep network.
b
Depth scale
\xi_{q}=\tau_{\alpha\alpha}
for the diagonal elements
of the covariance matrix.
c
Depth scale
\xi_{c}=\tau_{\alpha\beta}

for the off-diagonal element. Both as a function of
\sigma_{w}=g
,
the variance of the prior for the weights and different variances
for the biases (different colors, darker colors for lower bias variances;
adapted from
[
33
]
)

5
Keypoints

In summary:

Recurrent networks

•

Network field theory can be extended to recurrent networks by unrolling
the net in time and keeping the same weight matrix between all resulting
“layers”.

•

Equal time correlations of the NNGP for RNNs matches those of deep
feed forward neural networks (FFNs); Unequal time correlations in
RNNs yield non-trivial results compared to deep FFNs.

•

The network gain can tune the recurrent neural networks in the NNGP
limit towards and away from the edge of chaos.

6
Appendix: Price’s theorem

Computing expectation derivatives of expectation values of the form

\displaystyle f(C):=

\displaystyle\langle\phi(x)\phi(y)\rangle_{x,y\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}

by entries of the
2\times 2
covariance matrix follow from Price’s
theorem, which we may derive here by writing the Gaussian expectation
value with help of the Fourier representation and its cumulant-generating
function
\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})C(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})
as with
C=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}C_{11}&C_{12}\\
C_{12}&C_{22}\end{array}\right)

\displaystyle f(C)

\displaystyle=\int Px\,\int Py\,\phi(x)\phi(y)\,\int P\tilde{x}\,\int P\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-x\tilde{x}-y\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})C(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\big).

Differentiating by
C_{12}
, one gets

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{12}}f(C)

\displaystyle=\int Px\,\int Py\,\phi(x)\phi(y)\,\int P\tilde{x}\,\int P\tilde{y}\,\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-x\tilde{x}-y\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})C(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\big)

\displaystyle=\int Px\,\int Py\,\phi(x)\phi(y)\,\int P\tilde{x}\,\int P\tilde{y}\,(-\frac{\partial}{\partial x})(-\frac{\partial}{\partial y})\,\exp\big(-x\tilde{x}-y\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})C(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.b.p.}}}{{=}}\int Px\,\int Py\,\big[\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\phi(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial y}\phi(y)\big]\,\int P\tilde{x}\,\int P\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-x\tilde{x}-y\tilde{y}+\frac{1}{2}(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})C(\tilde{x},\tilde{y})\big)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi^{\prime}(x)\phi^{\prime}(y)\rangle,

where we used integration by parts (i.b.p.) and used that the boundary
term vanishes for functions
\phi
that grow less than
e^{x^{2}}
.
Analogously follows

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C_{11}}f(C)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\langle\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)\phi(y)\rangle,

because differentiating by a diagonal element of
C
one gets a factor

\frac{1}{2}\tilde{x}^{2}
, which leads to
\frac{1}{2}\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}

acting on
\phi(x)
and similarly (by the chain rule) for

\displaystyle g(C)

\displaystyle=\langle\phi^{2}(x)\rangle_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial C}g(C)

\displaystyle=\langle(\phi^{\prime})^{2}+\phi^{\prime\prime}\phi\rangle_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C)}.

7
Exercises

a) Parameter average with and without weight sharing

Here we want to recap some steps in detail that were left out in the
lecture. In the lecture we utilized the parameter average to infer
the distribution of the fields and ultimately the distribution of
the output
z
in the limit of many neurons. The difference in the
calculations between deep neural networks (DNNs) and recurrent neural
networks (RNNs) lies in the former having different realizations of
the connectivity
W^{(l)}
for each layer, whereas the latter has
the same realization
W^{(l)}\equiv W
for each layer (Every layer
in an RNN corresponds to one timestep).

In both cases we are interested in the output statistics for the setup

\displaystyle h^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\,x,

\displaystyle h^{(l)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W\,\phi(h^{(l-1)})\quad 1\leq l\leq L,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h^{(L)}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y+\xi,

which are given by (see (
19
) and (
2
))

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}h^{(1\leq t\leq L)}\,\big\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\big[y_{\alpha}-w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(L)})\big]\big\rangle_{w}

\displaystyle\times\big\langle\prod_{l=1}^{L}\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\delta\big[h_{\alpha}^{(l)}-W^{(l)}\,\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(l-1)})\big]\big\rangle_{W_{ij}^{(l)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P. }}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/d),h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P. id }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)})}.

(12)

Evaluate the average over the connectivities in the second line of
(
12
) for DNNs and RNNs. Compare
the form of the expressions that show up in the exponent and the resulting
implications on the correlation between different layers
C^{(l,l^{\prime})}
.
Then introduce the auxiliary variables
C
as in the lecture and
bring the result to the form (
5
) and (
6
)
(for the RNN) and (
23
) and (
24
)
(for the DNN). You may follow the analogous steps as in
Section
3
.

b)
Skip connections in ResNets

Consider the network with the following architecture

\displaystyle h^{(0)}

\displaystyle=V\,x,

\displaystyle h^{(1)}

\displaystyle=h^{(0)}+W\,\phi(h^{(0)}),

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h^{(1)}),

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=y+\xi.

with Gaussian priors
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/d)

and
w_{i},W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
,
\xi_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{d}(0,\kappa)
.
The first field has a direct connection to the readout, hence it is
referred to as a skip connection. Networks implementing these skip
connections are called residual networks (or ResNets in shortform).
These skip connections implement an identity mapping and thus facilitate
signal propagation to deeper layers. Resnet
50
implements skip connections
and is one of the state of the art networks on CIFAR-10
[
68
]
.

Make use of the large width limit and compute the NNGP for this setup.

Hint:
The sum of a pair of independent, Gaussian variables
is also distributed according to a Gaussian.

Can you see why the networks are called ResNets?

c) NNGP in recurrent neural networks for inputs at every timepoint

Consider the Gaussian process of a recurrent neural network if a scalar
input is presented at each time step
x^{(t)}\in\mathbb{R}
within
the interval
t\in[0,T]

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}^{(t+1)}

\displaystyle=W\,\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(t)})+v\,x_{\alpha}^{(t)},

(13)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=u^{\top}\phi\left(h_{\alpha}^{(T)}\right),

(14)

with
\mathbb{R}^{N}\ni h_{\alpha}^{(0)}=(0,\ldots,0)
and
W_{ij}\sim\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{w}/N),\,v_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{v}),\,w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
.
We want to understand how the covariances of the intermediate pre-activations

h_{\alpha}^{(t)}
and the network output
y_{\alpha}
relate to
the covariances of the network input. For this purpose start from

\displaystyle p(y)

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{P}h^{(1\leq t\leq T)}\mathcal{P}\tilde{h}^{(1\leq t\leq T)}\langle\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\left[y_{\alpha}-w^{\top}\phi(h_{\alpha}^{(T)})\right]\rangle_{u}

\displaystyle\times\langle\prod_{t=1}^{T}\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\prod_{i,j=1}^{\acute{N}}\exp\left[\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(t)}h_{\alpha i}^{(t)}-w_{ij}\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(t)}\phi(h_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)})-v_{i}\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(t)}x_{\alpha}^{(t-1)}+\tilde{h}_{\alpha i}^{(0)\top}h_{\alpha i}^{(0)}\right]\rangle_{w,v},

and perform the expectation over
W,v
. It is useful to employ the
auxiliary fields

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{j}\phi(h_{\alpha j}^{(t-1)})\phi(h_{\beta j}^{(s-1)})

(15)

\displaystyle C_{x,\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}

\displaystyle=g_{v}\,x_{\alpha}^{(t-1)}x_{\beta}^{(s-1)}

(16)

using auxiliary fields
\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}
and
\tilde{C}_{x,\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}
.
Compute the saddle point values for
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)},C_{x,\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)},\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)},\tilde{C}_{x,\alpha\beta}^{(t,s)}

(in the NNGP limit,
N\rightarrow\infty
). How do the results differ
from the derivations in the lecture notes? What is the result for
the network output covariance
\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle

in the NNGP limit? Assume now that we consider a linear activation
function. What is the result for the network output covariance
\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle

in the NNGP limit and how is it related to the input ? Assume stationary
statistics
\langle x_{\alpha}^{(t)}x_{\beta}^{(s)}\rangle:=\kappa_{2}^{(x)}(t-s)
.
What is the input averaged result for the output covariance
\left\langle\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle_{y}\right\rangle_{x}
?
How does the choice of
g_{w}
influence the behavior of
\left\langle\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle_{y}\right\rangle_{x}

for long times
T\gg 1
?

d)
Fixed points for Neural Network Gaussian
Process Kernel in linear setting and non-linear setting

Suppose that we have a deep linear network with the network architecture

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}^{1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{ij}x_{j\alpha}\,,V_{ij}\sim\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{v}/d)

(17)

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}W_{ij}^{(l)}h_{j\alpha}^{(l-1)}\quad l=2,\dots,L\,,W_{ij}^{(l)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{w}/N)

(18)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}h_{i\alpha}^{(L)}\,,w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{u}/N)

(19)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}\quad\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)

(20)

We want to obtain the corresponding Neural Network Gaussian Process
kernel for this linear network. Instead of resorting to the field
theoretic approach we consider a more “handwaving” calculation,
which allows us to obtain the leading order results in
N
. First
show, that the mean for the network output and the hidden states yields

0
if we average over the network parameters
w,W^{(1)},\ldots,W^{(L)},V
.
Next show how the covariance
\langle h_{i\alpha}^{(l)}h_{j\beta}^{(l)}\rangle_{W^{(l)}}

and
\langle h_{i\alpha}^{(l-1)}h_{j\beta}^{(l-1)}\rangle_{W^{(l-1)},\ldots,W^{(1)},V}

are related to one another. Use that we consider the setting
N\rightarrow\infty
.
Where do you need to exploit this fact? What are the fixed points
for
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(z)}
when we consider the case
L\rightarrow\infty
?
Hint: Consider the cases
g_{w}>1,g_{w}<1
separately. What does
this mean for the inferred network output ? Now we consider the case
where the network layers get a bias terms
b
, which is also standard
practice in machine learning:

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}^{(1)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{d}V_{ij}x_{j\alpha}+b_{i}^{(1)}\,,V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{v}/d),\quad b_{i}^{(1)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{b})

(21)

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{\acute{N}}W_{ij}^{(l)}h_{j\alpha}^{(l-1)}+b_{i}^{(l)}\quad l=2,\ldots,L\,,W_{ij}^{(l)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N),\quad b_{i}^{(l)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{b}^{2})

(22)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{h}}w_{i}h_{i\alpha}^{(L)}+b_{i}^{(\mathrm{out})}\,,w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{u}/N),\quad b_{i}^{(\mathrm{out})}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,g_{b}^{2})

(23)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}\quad\xi_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{\mathcal{N}}(0,\kappa).

(24)

Considering this setting, how does the relation between
\langle h_{i\alpha}^{(l)}h_{j\beta}^{(l)}\rangle_{W^{(l)},\ldots,W^{(1)},V,b^{(1)}...b^{(l)}}

and
\langle h_{i\alpha}^{(l-1)}h_{j\beta}^{(l-1)}\rangle_{W^{(l-1)},\ldots,W^{(1)},V,b^{(1)},\ldots,b^{(l-1)}}

look like? What are the new fixed points for
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(z)}

when we consider the case
L\rightarrow\infty
? Hint: Consider the
cases
g_{w}>1,g_{w}<1
separately. What does this mean for the inferred
network output ? Having seen those results: What would be a reasonable
setting for
g_{w}
if
\sigma_{b}=0
?

As you can imagine, in the case of non-linear networks, the situation
might be a bit more complicated. Consider the non-linear network,
where the only difference is the activation function appearing in

\displaystyle h_{i\alpha}^{(l)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}W_{ij}^{(l)}\phi(h_{j\alpha}^{(l-1)})+b_{i}^{(l)}\quad l=2,\ldots,L,

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{h}}w_{i}\phi(h_{i\alpha}^{(L)})+b_{i}^{(\mathrm{out})}\,,

where we choose the so called rectified linear unit (short ReLU) activation

\phi(x)=\mathrm{ReLU}(x)=\begin{cases}0&x\leq 0\\
x&x>0\end{cases}

(25)

Compute the variance
C_{\alpha\alpha}^{y}
of the NNGP for a network
with ReLu activation. Keep in mind that this means you need to compute
kernels of the form

\langle\phi(h_{\alpha})^{2}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{d}(0,C_{\alpha\alpha})}=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}Ph_{\alpha}\phi^{2}(h_{\alpha})\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi C_{\alpha\alpha}}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2C_{\alpha\alpha}}h_{\alpha}^{2}\right)

(26)

For
L\rightarrow\infty
, what is the value for
g_{w}
which allows
for non-trivial fixpoints ?

Chapter 7
Fokker-Planck equation

To investigate the dynamics of learning in neuronal networks we need
to relate the time-dependent process of adapting the weights to the
distribution of weights that we have studied so far in the setting
of Bayesian inference. To this end we need the theoretical tool that
allows us to derive from an equation of motion, here the one for the
synaptic weights, the evolution of the probability distribution. This
tool is the Fokker-Planck equation.

1
Stochastic differential equations

For simplicity, we here consider the stochastic evolution of a scalar
variable
x(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, before we generalize this result to
the time-dependent stochastic evolution of multiple variables
x(t)\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
.
We therefore first consider the
stochastic differential equation

(SDE)

\displaystyle dx(t)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x)\,dt+g(x)\,dB(t)

(1)

\displaystyle x(0+)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle a,

where
a
is the initial value and
dB
a stochastic increment.
Stochastic differential equations are defined as the limit
h\to 0

of a dynamics on a discrete time lattice of spacing
h
. For discrete
time
t_{l}=lh
,
l=0,\ldots,M
, the solution of the SDE consists
of the discrete set of points
x_{l}=x(t_{l})
. For the discretization
there are mainly two conventions used, the Ito and the Stratonovich
convention
[
69
]
. In case of additive noise (
g(x)=\mathrm{const.}
),
where the stochastic increment in (
1
) does not
depend on the state
x
, the two conventions yield the same continuous-time
limit
[
69
]
. However, as we will see, different discretization
conventions of the drift term lead to different path integral representations,
as we will see later. The Ito convention defines the time discrete
notation of (
1
) to be interpreted as

\displaystyle x_{i}-x_{i-1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f(x_{i-1})\,h+g(x_{i-1})\,b_{i},

(2)

where
b_{i}
is a stochastic increment that follows a probabilistic
law. A common choice for
b_{i}
is a normal distribution
\rho(b_{i})=\mathcal{N}(b_{i}|0,hD)
,
called a
Wiener increment
. Here the parameter
D
controls
the variance of the noise, as above. If the variance of the increment
is proportional to the time step
h
, this amounts to a
\delta
-distribution
in the autocorrelation of the noise
\xi=\frac{dB}{dt}
, because

\langle b_{i}b_{j}\rangle=\delta_{ij}\,Dh
and we see from (
2
)
that the correlation of the noise
\xi_{i}=b_{i}/h
then is
\langle\xi_{i}/h\xi_{j}/h\rangle=\delta_{ij}\,\frac{D}{h}
,
which can be regarded as the discrete analogue of the Dirac distribution,

\displaystyle\langle\xi(t)\xi(s)\rangle

\displaystyle=D\,\delta(t-s).

1
Fokker-Planck equation

We will now derive the Fokker-Planck equation, an equation describing
the time-evolution of the probability density function, closely following
the derivation in
[
70
]
. We note that the process has
the
Markov property
: the evolution of the system at time
point
t_{i}
only depends on the current state
x_{i}
, but not
on the history
x_{j<i}
, the states by which state
x_{i}
was
reached. This means that the knowledge of the state
y
at some earlier
time point
s
is sufficient to know the statistics in the future.
For such processes, one may write the evolution of the probability
distribution as the so-called
Chapman-Kolmogorov

equation
,

\displaystyle p(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,p(x,t;y,s)

(3)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,p(x,t|y,s)\,p(y,s),

where the first line marginalizes the joint probability of state
x

at time
t
and state
y
at time
s
,
p(x,t;y,s)
, over the
state
y
at time
s
. The second line is just the fundamental relation
between conditioned and unconditioned probabilities,
p(x,y)=p(x|y)\,p(y)
.
It is an expression of the conservation of probability: Every state

y
at time
s
can move to any state
x
at a later time
t>s

by the transition probability
p(x,t|y,s)
. So the equation can be
regarded as a linear integral equation for the evolution of the joint
probability distribution for the state at the more advanced time point

t
.

Figure 1:
Illustration of the Chapman-Kolmogorov equation.
The Chapman-Kolmogorov
equation describes the time evolution of the probability density.
The conditional probability (green) allows for the transition between
probabilities of different states and times.

Our goal is now to derive, instead, a differential equation for this
evolution. Instead of providing the conditional probability
p(x,t|y,s)

we may equivalently provide the moment-generating function for the
difference
x-y

\displaystyle Z(j|y,s,\tau)

\displaystyle=\int\,dx\,e^{j(x-y)}\,p(x,s+\tau|y,s),

where we set
t=s+\tau
. Here the triple
(y,s,\tau)
plays the
role of parameters. Differentiating by
j
obviously yields the moments
of
x-y
.

We may evaluate
Z(j|y,t,\tau=h)
for the stochastic differential
equation (
2
). We read off that
x_{i}-x_{i-1}
has
non-vanishing first and second cumulants only, which are

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x_{i}-x_{i-1}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=f(x_{i-1})\,h,

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=g^{2}(x_{i-1})\,D\,h,

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}(x_{i}-x_{i-1})^{n>2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle\equiv 0,

where we used that the right hand side is Gaussian distributed by
definition, cumulants of order higher than two vanish. Evaluating
these expressions at
x_{i-1}=y
we may write
Z
in terms of these
two cumulants

\displaystyle Z(j|y,t,h)

\displaystyle=\exp\big(j\,f(y)\,h+\frac{j^{2}}{2}\,g^{2}(y)\,D\,h\big),

\displaystyle=1+j\,f(y)\,h+\frac{j^{2}}{2}\,g^{2}(y)\,D\,h+\mathcal{O}(h^{2}),

(4)

where we may stop at order
h
here, because we are after an evolution
equation for infinitesimal time steps
h\to 0
, so this truncation
will not be an approximation.

We may hence express the conditional probability distribution as the
inverse Fourier transform

\displaystyle p(x,t+h|y,t)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dj\,e^{-j(x-y)}\,Z(j|y,t,h)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}dj\,\Big(1+j\,f(y)\,h+\frac{j^{2}}{2}\,g^{2}(y)\,D\,h+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})\Big)\,e^{-j(x-y)}.

The factors
j
and
j^{2}
appearing under the integral can also
be written as application of
-\partial_{x}
and
\partial_{x}^{2}

to
e^{-j(x-y)}
. This allows us to write the last line as

\displaystyle\Big(1-\partial_{x}f(y)\,h+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{x}^{2}\,g^{2}(y)\,D\,h+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})\Big)\,\underbrace{\frac{1}{2\pi i}\int dj\,e^{-j(x-y)}}_{\delta(x-y)},

(5)

so that we get from the Chapman-Kolmogorov relation (
3
),
by eliminating the integral over
y
by using
\delta(x-y)
,

\displaystyle p(x,t+h)-p(x,t)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,p(x,t+h|y,t)\,p(y,t)-p(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\partial_{x}f(x)\,h+\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{x}^{2}\,g^{2}(x)\,D\,h+\mathcal{O}(h^{2})\Big)\,p(x,t),

where the term
1
in (
5
) has been canceled
versus the subtraction of the last term
p(x,t)
in the penultimate
line. Note that the differential operators here also act on the functions

f(x)
and
g(x)
; this is because

\displaystyle\int dy\,\big[f(y)\,\partial_{x}\,\delta(x-y)\big]\,p(y,t)

\displaystyle=-\int dy\,f(y)\,p(y,t)\,\partial_{y}\,\delta(x-y)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.b.p.}}}{{=}}\int dy\,\delta(x-y)\,\partial_{y}\big[f(y)\,p(y,t)\big]

\displaystyle=\partial_{x}\big[f(x)\,p(x,t)\big],

where we used that the derivative of the Dirac distribution is defined
such that the derivative acts on the test function (here
f(y)\,p(y,t)
)
multiplying the distribution, so that integration by parts holds with
vanishing boundary terms. In the last step we used that the integral
together with the Dirac
\delta
leads to the evaluation of the function

y\mapsto\partial_{y}\big[f(y)\,p(y,t)\big]
at the point
y=x
.
An analog argument holds for the diffusion term.

We write the obtained
Fokker-Planck equation
in infinitesimal
form (dividing by
h
and taking the limit
h\to 0
) as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}\left(f(x)p(x,t)\right)+\frac{D}{2}\partial_{x}^{2}\left(g^{2}(x)p(x,t)\right)

(6)

We remark that:

•

The Fokker-Planck equation is exact for the considered Langevin equation
with Gaussian white noise.

•

The first term is also called
drift term
, because it arises
from the deterministic drift; the second
diffusion term
,
because it comes from the noise.

•

The expansion in terms or the moments of the transition probability
we performed in (
4
) is the so called
Kramers-Moyal
expansion
. It may also be performed for arbitrary Markov processes.

•

In general, for non-Gaussian increments, arbitrary many moments and
hence derivatives in (
6
) contribute. However,

Pawula’s theorem
states that the Kramers-Moyal expansion
terminates either after the first term (deterministic system), the
second term (as in the Gaussian case), or requires infinitely many
terms; this implies that for general statistics of the noise, the
Fokker-Planck equation becomes an approximation. Still, as frequently
employed SDE integrate many stochastic increments over time, employing
the Fokker-Planck equation may still be a very good approximation
even for non-Gaussian noise.

We may write the Fokker-Planck equation in a more suggestive form
as a
continuity equation
that expresses the conservation
of probability as

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\partial_{x}\,J(x,t),

\displaystyle J(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Big(f(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\partial_{x}\,g^{2}(x)\,D\Big)\,p(x,t),

where the first line has the usual form of a continuity equation,
namely the temporal derivative equals minus the divergence (in one
dimension) of the probability current
J
. Note that here the derivative
in
\partial_{x}\,g^{2}(x)\,D
also acts on
p(x,t)
. The second
equation expresses the current as the sum of the
drift term
,
proportional to the product of the density of each state
x
and
its velocity
f(x)
and the second term is the
diffusion
term
, which describes the probability current due to the stochastic
jumps (it is proportional to
D
, the noise amplitude): it is directed
towards minus the gradient of the density. If we had an
N
-component
vector
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
instead of a scalar, the above formulation generalizes
to

\displaystyle\partial_{t}p(x,t)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i}\partial_{x_{i}}J_{i}(x,t)=-\nabla\cdot J(x,t)

(7)

\displaystyle J_{i}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Big(f_{i}(x)-\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{j}D_{ij}\partial_{x_{j}}g_{i}(x)\,g_{j}(x)\Big)\,p(x,t),

(8)

where
D_{ij}
is the covariance matrix of the noise
\langle\xi_{i}(t)\xi_{j}(s)\rangle=D_{ij}\,\delta(t-s)
.
This can be seen by considering the Kramers-Moyal expansion (
4
),
in which
j_{i}f_{i}h
will appear from the first cumulant and
\frac{1}{2}D_{ij}g_{i}(x)g_{j}(x)h

from the second cumulant.

2
Boltzmann distribution and detailed balance

We are now equipped with the methods to make the link between the
Boltzmann distribution in equilibrium and the time-evolution described
by the Fokker-Planck equation.

For the stochastic differential equation (
1
) with
uncorrelated
additive noise
with
D_{ij}=D\,\delta_{ij}
,
which is independent of the state, so
g=1
, and there the deterministic
force is conservative, which is

\displaystyle f(x)

\displaystyle=-\nabla V(x),

the probability current of the Fokker-Planck equation in the continuum
formulation (
8
) takes the form

\displaystyle J_{i}(x,t)

\displaystyle=\Big(-\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}V(x)-\frac{D}{2}\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{i}}\Big)\,p(x,t).

(9)

We see from the continuity equation that we get a particular stationary
distribution
\partial_{t}p(x,t)\equiv 0
if the probability current
is a constant as a function of
x
, because then the divergence in
(
7
) vanishes at each point in space. In such
a state hence all probability currents are identical. If we assume
that the amplitude of fluctuations of
x
are limited, which is physically
certainly reasonable, there cannot be a probability current at arbitrary
large values of
x
(the argument of the left hand side of (
9
)).
Hence the only physical way how such a stationary distribution may
arise is if the current vanishes for all configurations
x
. This
condition is also called
detailed balance
, because it amount
to saying that the mutual probability fluxes between neighboring points

x
and
x+\delta x
is vanishing at each configuration
x
, so
the fluxes between any such pair of configurations are of same magnitude
but have opposite direction – they are in balance.

We hence need to determine the solution of the differential equation

\displaystyle 0=

\displaystyle\Big(-\nabla V-\frac{D}{2}\nabla\Big)\,p(x).

We see that

\displaystyle p_{0}(x)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\big(-\frac{2}{D}\,V(x)\big)

(10)

is such a stationary solution, which is of Boltzmann form, if we interpret

V(x)
as an energy and
D
as the temperature
k_{B}T
.

Figure 2:
Probability distribution in detailed balance.
Stationary
solution for the probability densities in a double well potential
with different strengths of the diffusion term.

2
Training by Langevin dynamics in relation to Bayesian inference

We are now in the position to relate the Bayesian view on training
neuronal networks to the stochastic dynamics of gradient descent,
following
[
71
,
47
]
. Consider the quadratic
loss function (
2
). Now consider changing the
weights proportional to the gradient with weight decay

\displaystyle dW

\displaystyle=\big(-\gamma W-\nabla_{W}\mathcal{L}\big)\,dt+dB

(11)

\displaystyle=-\nabla_{W}\,\big(\frac{\gamma}{2}\,\|W\|^{2}+\mathcal{L}\big)\,dt+dB,

where
dB
is a Gaussian noise (Wiener increment) with variance
D
,
so
\langle dB_{i}(t)dB_{j}(s)\rangle=D\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(t-s)\,dt
.
The equilibrium distribution of the weights then is of the Boltzmann
form, according to (
10
)

\displaystyle p_{0}(W)

\displaystyle\propto\exp\big(-\frac{\gamma}{D}\,\|W\|^{2}-\frac{2}{D}\,\mathcal{L}(z,y(W))\big).

(12)

We thus obtain a distribution of the weights that is given by a Gaussian
term
e^{-\frac{1}{2}\frac{2\gamma}{D}\,\|W\|^{2}}
and a term that
depends on the training data
e^{-\frac{2}{D}\mathcal{L}}
. The two
parameters
D
and
\gamma
allow us to separately control the variance
of the Gaussian and the importance of the data term. The data term
depends on the outputs
y_{\alpha}
of the network. We wish to understand
the behavior of the network where the weights are drawn from the distribution
(
12
), which implies a joint probability
distribution for
y
and
z
, which is

\displaystyle p(z,y|x)\propto

\displaystyle\int dW\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{2\gamma}{D}\,\|W\|^{2}-\frac{2}{D}\,\mathcal{L}(z,y)\big)\,\delta(y-y(W|x))

(13)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int dW\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{2\gamma}{D}\,\|W\|^{2}-\frac{1}{D}\,\|y-z\|^{2}\big)\,\delta\big(y-y(W|x)\big)

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\Bigg\langle\exp\big(-\frac{1}{D}\,\|y-z\|^{2}\big)\,\delta\big(y-y(W|x)\big)\Bigg\rangle_{W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{D}{2\gamma})}

\displaystyle\propto

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(z|y,D/2)\,\langle p(y|W,X)\rangle_{W_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{D}{2\gamma})},

which is the same as (
3
) if one sets
g_{w}/M=D/(2\gamma)

and
\kappa=D/2
. So the noise
D
here plays the role of the regularization
and the weight decay term in (
11
) controls
the width of the prior distribution of the weights in the Bayesian
approach.

On a conceptual level, the difference between the Bayesian approach
and the training of individual networks is that the posterior distribution
represents ensembles of trained networks. This means that one obtains
an uncertainty estimate for the trained network. One may practically
employ such ensembles by training multiple networks from different
initial conditions or by using the same initialization and stopping
the training process at different points in time after the equilibrium
distribution has been reached. Using multiple such networks one may
then obtain a practical estimate of the uncertainty of the network
output, for example by quantifying the variability of the outputs
of this ensemble.

3
Keypoints

We learned:

Fokker Planck equation

•

Assuming Markovian property we can utilize the Chapman-Kolmogorov
equation to compute path probability distribution
p(x,t)
of SDEs.

•

We can approximate the time evolution of
p(x,t)
yielding the Fokker
Planck equation; which is exact if the SDE noise is Gaussian white
noise.

•

The stationary distribution of the Fokker-Planck equation of an SDE
with Gaussian noise has a Boltzmann form if the deterministic force
is conservative.

•

Training networks using Langevin stochastic gradient descent (LSGD)
yields the same equilibrium distribution we describe analytically
using Bayesian inference. This links training dynamics with Bayesian
inference and allows one to test theoretical results numerically.

4
Exercises

1
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck process: time evolution of moments

For a quadratic potential
V(\mathbf{x})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{x}^{\mathrm{T}}m\mathbf{x}
, the Langevin
dynamics (see
Section
2
) become an Ornstein-Uhlenbeck
process

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\mathbf{x}(t)

\displaystyle=-m\,\mathbf{x}(t)+\boldsymbol{\xi}(t),

(14)

with
m\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
,
\left\langle\xi_{i}(t)\right\rangle=0

and
\left\langle\xi_{i}(t)\xi_{j}(s)\right\rangle=D\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(t-s)
.

Show that

\displaystyle\mathbf{x}(t)=

\displaystyle\exp\left(-m\,t\right)\mathbf{x}(0)\,+\,\int_{0}^{t}dt^{\prime}\,\exp\left(-m\,\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)\right)\boldsymbol{\xi}(t^{\prime})

(15)

solves (
14
).

Using (
15
), derive expressions
for the mean
\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}(t)\right\rangle
and the
covariance at two different points in time
C(t,s)=\left\langle\left(\boldsymbol{x}(t)-\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}(t)\right\rangle\right)\left(\boldsymbol{x}(s)-\left\langle\boldsymbol{x}(s)\right\rangle\right)^{\mathrm{T}}\right\rangle\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}
.

Which differential equations do
C(t+\tau,t)

(\tau>0)
and
C(t,t)

obey? To this end take the derivatives
\frac{\partial}{\partial\tau}C(t+\tau,t)

and
\frac{\partial}{\partial t}C(t,t)
. How would these time evolution
equations change if we had a noise that is non-diagonal in space
D\,\delta_{ij}\to D_{ij}
?

As you have seen in
Chapter
7

the Fokker-Planck equation describes the time evolution of the probability
density
p(\boldsymbol{x},t)
exactly for Gaussian white noise. What
is the equilibrium distribution
p_{0}(\boldsymbol{x})
the dynamics
(
14
) relax to?

2
Connecting Bayesian inference and Gradient descent for linear regression

In this exercise we want to explicitly see the connection between
the Bayesian approach and training by stochastic gradient descent
in the framework of linear regression. Just as one can calculate the
posterior of the outputs (see (
10
)),
we can also ask the question of how the weights are distributed after
conditioning on the training data (i.e. posterior of the weights,
see (
8
) and
Figure
1
).
As for the outputs, we make use of the Bayesian formulation

p(w|X,z)=\frac{p(z|X,w)\,p(w)}{p(z|X)}.

(16)

Here,
p(w)
is the prior of the weights with

w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/M)

and the likelihood is given by

\displaystyle p(z|X,w)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,p(y|X,w)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\delta\left(y-y(W|x)\right)

\displaystyle=\int dy\,\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{D}\delta\left(y_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{T}}w\right)

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|Xw,\kappa),

with
X=\left(x_{1},\dots,x_{D}\right)^{\mathrm{T}}
,
X\in\mathbb{R}^{D\times M}
,

w\in\mathbb{R}^{M}
and the label noise
\kappa
. Show that
p(w|X,z)

follows a Gaussian distribution. State the mean and covariance.

As you have have seen in the lecture, if one chooses
\kappa=\mathcal{D}/2

and
g_{w}/M=\mathcal{D}/(2\gamma)
, this corresponds to the equilibrium distribution
of

dw=\big(-\gamma w-\nabla_{w}\mathcal{L}\big)\,dt+dB

(17)

with
\langle dB_{i}(t)dB_{j}(s)\rangle=\mathcal{D}\,\delta_{ij}\,\delta(t-s)\,dt

and
\mathcal{L}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha=1}^{D}\left(z_{\alpha}-x_{\alpha}^{\mathrm{T}}w\right)^{2}
.
Derive the time evolution equation for the mean of the weights
\frac{d\langle w\rangle}{dt}

and verify that the stationary point is given by the mean of (
16
).
How do you solve such an equation in general? And how could you then
infer the effective time constant for
\left\langle w\right\rangle
? To this end, decompose
the data overlap matrix
X^{\mathrm{T}}X=\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}v_{i}v_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}

into its orthogonal eigenmodes.

Next we give you without proof for the linear stochastic differential
equation

\displaystyle d\tilde{w}(t)

\displaystyle=-A\,\tilde{w}(t)\,dt+dB(t)

(18)

the time evolution for the second cumulant at equal times
C(t)=\langle\tilde{w}(t)\tilde{w}(t)^{\mathrm{T}}\rangle

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}C(t)

\displaystyle=-A\,C(t)-C(t)\,A^{\mathrm{T}}+\mathcal{D}\mathbb{I}.

(19)

Bring the stochastic gradient descent into the form (
18
)
and and verify that the variance of (
16
)
is a fixed point of (
19
).

3
Lagrange multipliers and gradient descent

Recap: Method of Lagrange multipliers

Figure 3:
Maximization of a function
H(p_{1},p_{2})
under the constraint

c(p_{1},p_{2})=0
. The normal vector
\nabla_{p}H
on the level
curves and the normal vector
\nabla_{p}c
on the hypersurface defined
by the constraint
c
must be parallel at the point of maximum,
\nabla_{p}H=\lambda\,\nabla_{p}c
.
This motivates the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda
and leads to the
optimization of
\nabla_{p}(H-\lambda c)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0
.

Obtaining a neural network which solves a specific task, such as labeling
images or regressing time series, corresponds, in the end, to an optimization
task. One seeks to optimize a loss function under the constraint that
the output of the network obeys the defining equations of the network
that implements the mapping from data samples to outputs,
x\mapsto y
.

In general maximizing a function
H
in the variables
p_{1}...p_{N}

with respect to some given constraints
c
is solved by the method
of
Lagrange multipliers
(illustrated in
Figure
3
).
To this end, one writes the constraints in the form
c(p_{1},\ldots,p_{N})=0
.
For any (not necessarily linear) constraint
c(p)=0
, the gradient

\nabla_{p}c(p)
is a vector that at each point is perpendicular
to the
N
-dimensional hypersurface formed by the constraint. Show
that this is indeed the case using the parametrization of a curve
for the constraint with
\varphi:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{N}
so that
c(t)=c(\varphi(t))=0
.
Hint: Use that the multidimensional chain rule and the fact that
\varphi

characterizes a path in the variables
p_{i=1...N}
. Later use the
relation between the velocity field
\partial_{t}\varphi(t)
and
the curve
\varphi(t)
.

Maximizing the function
H(p(x_{1}),\ldots,p(x_{N}))
given the constraints,
we therefore have to consider all curves
\varphi
within the plane
and search for the
t
at which
H(\varphi(t))
is stationary. Use
this statement to show, that the gradients
\nabla_{p}H
and
\nabla_{p}c

are parallel to each other. Using this we know, that the gradients
are related through a scalar
\lambda
, which we call the Lagrange
multiplier:

\displaystyle\nabla_{p}H

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lambda\nabla_{p}c

(20)

\displaystyle\nabla_{p}(H(p)-\lambda c(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

(21)

Several constraints
c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}
can be applied successively,
each reducing the space by one dimension, so that the final condition
is

\displaystyle\nabla_{p}(H(p)-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}c_{i}(p))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0.

Application to derive backprogation through time

We now want to use this general concept to understand the process
of training a discrete time recurrent neural network following
[
72
]
.
Consider the following setup where we assume scalar inputs, hidden
states, weights and labels
x_{\alpha}(t),\,h_{\alpha}(t)\,,w,\hat{y}_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}

and
1\leq\alpha\leq D
for
D
training samples. The setup reads
for

\displaystyle h(0)

\displaystyle=0,

(22)

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}(t+1)

\displaystyle=w\phi(h_{\alpha}(t))+x_{\alpha}(t)\quad t=0,\ldots,T-1,

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}(\hat{y}_{\alpha}-h_{\alpha}(T))^{2},

where we utilize a discrete time recurrent neural network in (
22
)
and
\hat{y}_{\alpha}
denotes the true labels corresponding to the
input sequence
x_{\alpha}(t)
. Formulate the condition for the path

h(t)
for
t=1,\ldots,T
that minimizes
L
under the
DT
constraints
that the equation of motion (
22
)
is fulfilled at each time point
t
and for each training sample

\alpha
, by introducing
DT
Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{\alpha}(t)

and constraints
c
to construct an action
S[h(t:1,\ldots T)]:=L[h]-\lambda\,c[h]
.
Compute the stationary point
\frac{\partial S}{\partial h}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0

with regard to each point at the sequence
h(t:1,\ldots,T)
and with
regard to each training sample
h_{\alpha}(t)
(the
h_{\alpha}(t)

here play the role of the parameters
p
in the abstract formulation
above) to obtain an equation of motion for the Lagrange multiplier.
Think about the interpretation of the equation of motion for
\lambda
