in terms of error backpropagation.

Then determine the gradient with regard to
w
of
S
to derive
the learning rule. Here use that by
\lambda
following the equation
of motion that ensures that
h
is a minimum, inner derivatives
\frac{\partial S}{\partial h}\frac{\partial h}{\partial w}=0
,
because
\frac{\partial S}{\partial h}=0
by construction. Also note
that
\frac{\partial S}{\partial\lambda}=c=0
by construction, because
we choose the constraints such that
c=0
.

Chapter 8
Feature learning

We here want to go beyond the NNGP as a theory of neuronal networks.
The latter has been obtained in the limit where the width
N\to\infty

is taken to infinity, while the number of training points
P
is
kept fixed. This led to the dominance of the rate function
\Gamma(C)\propto N

in determining the most likely value for the kernels
C
. Because

\Gamma
is independent of the labels
z
, as a result the NNGP
kernels depend only on the input data point
x
, but not on the labels

z
. This implies that the structures within the network described
by the kernels are independent of the joint statistics of
(x,z)
,
but rather only depend on the statistics of
x
alone.

Learning the statistical relationship between
(x,z)
, on the other
hand, is often referred to as “feature learning”; originally this
term refers to the fact that trained neuronal networks show the property
to learn “features” of the data, that is, neurons become selective
to certain patterns in the input data
x
that are relevant to the
task. “Relevance” here means that these feature-selective neurons
help the network to represent the statistical relationship between
inputs
x
and desired outputs
z
, for example to assign the right
label. The NNGP, being independent of
z
, hence cannot show such
behavior.

To study feature learning, we therefore need to consider a different
limit, namely sending the number of training points
P
and the width
of the layer
N
to infinity at the same rate, so
P=\alpha\,N\to\infty
,
where
\alpha=\mathcal{O}(1)
stays of order unity.
In the first part, we will recover the works by Li et al.
[
27
]
,
and by Ariosto et al.
[
73
]
, exemplified for single
hidden layer networks. This theory shows that both these works compute
the maximum a posteriori estimate of the length of the readout weights

\|w\|^{2}
. This section reformulates the original works such that
one does not need to introduce imaginary helping fields which do not
have physical meaning. We will then present the extension to deep
linear networks where the overlap between adjacent weight matrices
will appear as an order parameter. Subsequently we will look at another
formulation of feature learning that studies the adaptation of the
Gaussian process kernel itself to the joint statistics of
x
and

z

[
29
,
30
]
. We will here follow
the exposition of the latter work.

1
NNGP is label-agnostic

We have obtained the NNGP approximation from (
13
), rewritten
with help of the auxiliary fields (in the case of a single hidden
layer network) as

\displaystyle\int dy\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}C\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}z-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})-\Gamma(C)\big).

The NNGP limit corresponds to computing the maximum of
\Gamma(C)\propto N
,
while neglecting the term
-\frac{1}{2}y^{\mathrm{T}}(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}y-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})\propto\mathcal{O}(P)
.
This may also be seen as computing the maximum of
C
if one integrates
out
z
in addition to
y
, because one is then left with

\displaystyle\int dz\,\int dy\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}C\,\exp\big(-\Gamma(C)\big).

By comparing to (
13
), integrating out
z
removes the
term that depends on the training labels. This shows that the posterior
distribution of the weights in this case is identical to the prior
distribution of the weights, because the “data-term” term
\propto\|z-y\|^{2}
,
which would change the prior distribution to the posterior, has dropped
out. This is also obvious from the fact that the resulting iteration
(
15
) or (
26
) that yields the most
likely value for the
C
is independent of
z
.

In this sense, the NNGP limit is data agnostic. It does not describe
how the distribution of the weights within the networks change due
to the presence of the training data. To capture this, we will need
to study the effect of “data-term”
\propto\|z-y\|^{2}
on the
posterior distribution of the weights and hence on the posterior distribution
of the network outputs. So the distribution of the weights that influence
the statistics of
C
is the same as their prior distribution. So
where is the learning then in this limit? Which parameters actually
change?

Since the mean and predictive distribution is still given by (
17
)
and since these expressions are the same as those of Bayesian linear
regression
y=w^{\mathrm{T}}x
, only with
C^{(L)}
instead of
C^{(xx)}=\frac{g_{v}}{d}XX^{\mathrm{T}}
,
the only weights that change are these readout weights
w
. This
implies that the NNGP limit describes learning of the last layer of
the network, while all internal weights are essentially static, given
by their prior distribution. The reason is that due to the large
N

and hence the dominance of
\Gamma(C)
, the inner part of the network
is very “stiff”.

2
Field theory of single hidden layer network

We here again first consider the setup studied for the derivation
of the NNGP, a single hidden layer network, described by (
1
)
with Gaussian i.i.d. priors for all weights and we consider
P
tuples
of training data
\mathcal{D}=\{(x_{\alpha},z_{\alpha})\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
.
A difference will be that we consider
P\propto N
, the number of
data samples scaling linear with the number of hidden units.

The i.i.d. distribution of the input weights
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{V}/d)

implies that

\displaystyle h_{\alpha i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)}),

(1)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{P\times P}\ni C^{(xx)}

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{V}}{d}\,X\,X^{\mathrm{T}}.

Assuming an i.i.d. Gaussian regularization noise of variance
\kappa
,
the the central object of interest is the joint distribution of the
readouts
y=\{y_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
and the network outputs

z=\{z_{\alpha}\}_{1\leq\alpha\leq P}
, which follows from standard
manipulations as before (cf. (
3
)) as

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\big\langle\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+W(\tilde{y}|w)\,\big)\big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. }}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})},

(2)

where the cumulant-generating function
W
appears that is a sum
of products of
w_{i}\phi(h_{\alpha i})
, which are independent across

i

\displaystyle W(\tilde{y}|w):=

\displaystyle\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

(3)

3
Kernel scaling approach in linear single hidden networks

Now consider the special case that the activation function
\phi(h)=h

is the identity. Then (
3
) can be computed trivially
as

\displaystyle W(\tilde{y}|w):=

\displaystyle\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{i}w_{i}\,h_{\alpha i}\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

(4)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}\,,

where we used the definition of the cumulant-generating function of
a Gaussian. The sum over
i
appears because of the independence
of the
h_{i}
across
i
. This shows that the readout weights only
appear in the form of the squared norm
\|w\|^{2}\equiv\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}
.
The distribution of the output of the network (
2
)
is hence

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\big\langle\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}C^{(xx)}\tilde{y}\,\|w\|^{2}\big)\big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. }}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})}.

In the limit of large
N
we may assume the norm
\|w\|
to concentrate,
namely to obey a large deviation principle, so we introduce this quantity
as an auxiliary variable, which we name
Q:=\|w\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}
;
also note that, given
\|w\|
, the integral over
\tilde{y}
in (
2
)
simply yields
y|_{\|w\|^{2}}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\|w\|^{2}C^{(xx)})
, so

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int dQ\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,Q\,C^{(xx)})\,p(Q).

(5)

Here the distribution of the squared norm is

\displaystyle p(Q)

\displaystyle=\big\langle\delta[-Q+\|w\|^{2}]\big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})}

(6)

\displaystyle=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{Q}}{2\pi i}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\tilde{Q}\big[-Q+\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}\big]\big)\big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})}

\displaystyle=\int_{-i\infty}^{i\infty}\frac{d\tilde{Q}}{2\pi i}\,\exp\big(-\tilde{Q}Q-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\big[1-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\tilde{Q}\big]\big),

where we performed the
d
Gaussian integrals over the
N
mutually
independent
w_{i}
in the last step; in detail

\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(\tilde{Q}w_{i}^{2}\big]\big\rangle_{w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{W}}{N})}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\,g_{w}/N}}\,\int\,dw\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}\,\Big[\frac{N}{g_{w}}-2\tilde{Q}\Big]\,w^{2}\big)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi g_{w}/N}}\,\sqrt{2\pi\,\Big[\frac{N}{g_{w}}-2\tilde{Q}\Big]^{-1}}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{1}{\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\Big[\frac{N}{g_{w}}-2\tilde{Q}\Big]}}=\Big[1-2\,\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\tilde{Q}\Big]^{-\frac{1}{2}}.

So far all steps are exact.

Eq. (
5
) shows that the auxiliary variable
Q
being
a scalar may only carry fluctuations in the direction of the overall
scaling of the kernel. As we will be seeking a mean-field approximations
within this variable, the only result can be that the scale of the
covariance of the readout
y
will be
QC^{(xx)}
. We will see in
the following that this procedure can be iterated across layers. The
reason is that within the mean-field approximation, namely neglecting
fluctuations of
Q
, preactivations remain independent Gaussian across
neuron indices
i
, which is required for the step (
4
)
to be applied iteratively.

4
Approximation for large
N

One expects that
Q
concentrates, because it is the distribution
of
Q=\|w\|^{2}=\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}^{2}
for large
N
and for i.i.d.

w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}
. Formally, this is shown by the scaled cumulant generating
function of the form
N\,\lambda_{Q}\,(\frac{\tilde{Q}}{N}\,)=-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\big[1-2g_{W}\,\frac{\tilde{Q}}{N}\big]

appearing in (
6
), which implies a mean of order
\langle Q\rangle=\mathcal{O}(1)

and all higher order cumulants of
Q
being suppressed by at least

\mathcal{O}(N^{-1})
. So on exponential scales, one obtains the distribution
of
Q
from the rate function, which is the Legendre transform of
the cumulant-generating function, as we have seen in
Chapter
4

\displaystyle p(Q)

\displaystyle\simeq e^{-\Gamma(Q)}\,,

\displaystyle\Gamma(Q)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{Q}}\tilde{Q}Q-N\,\lambda_{Q}(\frac{\tilde{Q}}{N})

(7)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{Q}}\tilde{Q}Q+\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\big[1-2g_{w}\,\frac{\tilde{Q}}{N}\big]\,,

which in field theory terms is a saddle point approximation in
\tilde{Q}
.
The supremum condition can be computed exactly which yields the condition

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}Q-g_{w}\,\big[1-2g_{w}\frac{\tilde{Q}}{N}\big]^{-1}
,
solved for
1-\frac{2g_{w}}{N}\tilde{Q}=\frac{g_{w}}{Q}
and

\displaystyle\tilde{Q}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\,\big[1-\frac{g_{w}}{Q}\big]

\displaystyle\equiv\Gamma^{\prime}(Q)

(8)

and inserted into (
7
) yields the rate function for

Q

\displaystyle\Gamma(Q)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\,\big[Q-g_{w}\big]-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\,\frac{Q}{g_{w}}.

(9)

So the final expression for the joint probability of
z
and
y

is

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int dQ\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,Q\,C^{(xx)})\,e^{-\Gamma(Q)}.

(10)

1
Recovering the NNGP result

For the previously obtained NNGP result (
16
) we neglected
the data term or, in other words, were only interested in the maximum
of the rate function. Thus we can recover this result by searching
for the most probable
Q
according to the rate function.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\Gamma^{\prime}\left(Q\right)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2g_{w}}-\frac{N}{2Q}

\Rightarrow\,Q=g_{w}\,.

This rescaling by
g_{w}
is exactly what we also would have obtained
with the iteration derived in
Section
2

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\langle h_{\alpha}h_{\beta}\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}

such that

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\overset{\mathrm{NNGP}}{\simeq}\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,g_{w}\,C^{(xx)})\,.

5
Posterior

We will now compute the saddle point in
Q
of (
10
).
One may wonder what is the meaning of this step: By (
5
),
this has the meaning of computing the maximum of the posterior for

Q=\|w\|^{2}
. To see this, first note that we may obtain an action
which only contains
z
if one marginalizes (
10
)
over the network outputs
y
, because this yields

\displaystyle p(z|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\equiv\int dy\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

(11)

\displaystyle=\int dQ\,\exp\big(S(Q|z)\big),

with the action

\displaystyle S(Q|z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}\,z-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)-\Gamma(Q)\,,

(12)

where in the rate function
\Gamma(Q)=\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\,Q-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\,Q+\mathrm{const}

given by (
9
) we may drop all terms that are independent
of
Q
and we here used that the integral over
y
can be considered
as the convolution of the probability distribution
\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)

with the distribution of the readout
y
, giving rise to the addition
of the corresponding variances.

This form agrees to the original work
[
27
]
, their
Eq. A11 after we have inserted the rate function (
9
).
This means that in the saddle point equation for
Q
one ignores
the effect of
y
. Taking into account that the form of (
11
)
is
p(z)=\int PQ\,p(z|Q)\,p(Q)
, computing the
Q
-integral in saddle
point approximation has the meaning that one determines the maximum
a posteriori for
Q
, because

\displaystyle p(Q|z)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(z|Q)\,p(Q)}{p(z)}\,,

whose maximum
Q^{\ast}
only depends on the numerator and is therefore
given by the stationary point of (
12
), because
\ln\,p(z|Q)\,p(Q)=S(Q|z)+\mathrm{const}

has the same stationary point as
p(z|Q)\,p(Q)
. So the result is
the most likely value for the length of the readout vector
Q^{\ast}=\|w\|^{2}
.
In the following we will see how one can compute from here other useful
properties of the predictor.

The saddle point approximation of
Q
in (
11
), obtained
as the maximum of (
12
), is given by

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial S}{\partial Q}=\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}C^{(xx)}\,\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}z-\mathrm{tr}\,C^{(xx)}\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}-\frac{N}{2}\big(\frac{1}{g_{w}}-\frac{1}{Q}\big).

(13)

To obtain the derivative of
\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}=:N^{-1}

by
Q
, we first considered
[N^{-1}\,N]_{\gamma\delta}=\delta_{\gamma\delta}
,
differentiated by
N_{\alpha\beta}
yields
[N^{-1}]_{\gamma\alpha}\,\delta_{\beta\delta}+\sum_{\epsilon}\,\frac{\partial[N^{-1}]_{\gamma\epsilon}}{\partial N_{\alpha\beta}}\,N_{\epsilon\delta}=0
,
multiplied from right with
[N^{-1}]_{\delta\iota}
and summed over

\delta
one has
\frac{\partial[N^{-1}]_{\gamma\iota}}{\partial N_{\alpha\beta}}=-[N^{-1}]_{\gamma\alpha}[N^{-1}]_{\beta\iota}
.
The using the chain rule
\frac{\partial N_{\alpha\beta}}{\partial Q}=C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}
.
Also we used that
\frac{\partial}{\partial N}\,\ln\det(N)=N^{-1}
,
which follows from writing
-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(N)=\ln\,\int Px\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}x^{\mathrm{T}}Nx\big)+\mathrm{const}
.
Differentiating the right hand side by
N_{\alpha\beta}
yields the
second moment
-\frac{1}{2}\langle x_{\alpha}x_{\beta}\rangle\equiv-\frac{1}{2}\,[N^{-1}]_{\alpha\beta}

(also shown in the earlier exercises).

6
Predictor statistics

To obtain predictions beyond the length of the readout
\|w\|^{2}

we may start from (
14
), but generalized such
that instead of variance
\kappa
we insert a general covariance
matrix
K
and we perform an integration over
y

\displaystyle p(z|K,C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\int dy\,\mathcal{N}(z|y,K)\,\big\langle\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\,\big[y_{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})\big]\big\rangle_{w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N}),\hskip 8.19447pth_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d. over }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

(14)

If one sets the matrix
K=\kappa\mathbb{I}
, we again arrive at the same
expression (
14
) as before. For this case, note
that one may use
z
as a source variable to differentiate by to
obtain the mean discrepancy
\langle\Delta\rangle:=z-\langle y\rangle

between target and network output, because of
\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa\mathbb{I})\propto\exp\big(-\|z-y\|^{2}/(2\kappa)\big)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\,\ln p(z|\kappa\mathbb{I},C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\kappa}\,\big\langle z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}\big\rangle.

(15)

We obtain an expression in the mean-field approximation for this observable
as

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\,\ln p(z|\kappa\mathbb{I},C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{mean-field}}}{{\simeq}}\frac{d}{dz_{\alpha}}\,\sup_{Q}S(Q|z,\kappa\mathbb{I})

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\,S(Q^{\ast}|z,\kappa\mathbb{I})+\underbrace{\frac{\partial S}{\partial Q}}_{=0}\,\frac{\partial Q}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\big|_{Q=Q^{\ast}}\,,

where the derivative by
Q
vanishes because
Q^{\ast}
has been
determined by the supremum condition as the stationary point of the
action. The partial derivative by
z_{\alpha}
only acts on
-z^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}\,z/2

in the expression for (
12
), which hence yields

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa\,\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}\,z\,.

(16)

Likewise, the presence of the general matrix
K
allows us to measure
the statistics of the discrepancies
\Delta_{\alpha}:=z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}
,
because, writing the Gaussian
\mathcal{N}(z|y,K)=\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}(z-y)^{\mathrm{T}}K^{-1}(z-y)+\frac{1}{2}\,\ln\,\det\,(K^{-1})\big)

explicitly we observe that derivatives by
\big[K^{-1}\big]_{\alpha\beta}

yield

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}\ln p(z|K,C^{(xx)})\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\langle(z-y)_{\alpha}(z-y)_{\beta}\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\,\kappa\,\delta_{\alpha\beta}\,,

(17)

where we used that
\partial\,\ln\,\det\,(K^{-1})/\partial K^{-1}=K
,
as before. With the same manipulations that led to (
11
)
one has with
C(Q)=Q\,C^{(xx)}
the action

\displaystyle S(Q|z,K)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big(C(Q)+K\big)^{-1}\,z-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\big(C(Q)+K\big)-\Gamma(Q)\,.

So in mean-field approximation for
Q
we get

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}\ln p(z|K,C^{(xx)})\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{mean-field}}}{{\simeq}}\frac{d}{d[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}\,\sup_{Q}S(Q|z,K)\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}S(Q^{\ast}|z,K)\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}+\underbrace{\frac{\partial S}{\partial Q}\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}}_{=0}\,\frac{\partial Q}{\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}\,,

where the inner derivative by
\partial S/\partial Q
again drops
out due to stationarity of the action at
Q^{\ast}
, which is given
by the solution of (
13
). The latter partial derivative
evaluates to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}}S(Q|z,K)\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}

\displaystyle=\Big[-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C+K\big]^{-1}\,KK\,\big[C+K\big]^{-1}\,z+\frac{1}{2}\,K\,(C+K)^{-1}\,K\Big]_{\alpha\beta}\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}

(18)

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\,\Big[-\frac{1}{2}\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}+\frac{1}{2}\,(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}\Big]_{\alpha\beta}\Big|_{K=\kappa\mathbb{I}}\,,

where we used that
\partial K_{\gamma\delta}/\partial[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}=-K_{\gamma\alpha}\,K_{\beta\delta}
,
which follows by symmetry from
\partial K_{\gamma\delta}^{-1}/\partial K_{\alpha\beta}=-K_{\gamma\alpha}^{-1}\,K_{\beta\delta}^{-1}
.

So the second moment of the discrepancies with (
17
)
is

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\,\Big[\,\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}

(19)

\displaystyle+\mathbb{I}/\kappa-(C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}\,\Big]_{\alpha\beta}

and the average training loss is

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}\rangle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\sum_{\alpha=1}^{P}\langle(z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha})^{2}\rangle

(20)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\langle\Delta\Delta^{\mathrm{T}}\rangle.

Note that the computation of the variance by a first derivative in
(
18
) ignores fluctuations of
C
; this
limits the accuracy of this result. Indeed, one finds that in certain
settings, such fluctuation corrections produce sizable corrections
to the predictor statistics (see, e.g.
[
4
,
31
]
).

The expression for the expected discrepancies can be seen in the light
of the bias-variance decomposition, because the first line in (
19
)
is, by (
16
), the outer product of the mean
discrepancies
\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\rangle\langle\Delta_{\beta}\rangle
.
To this end, note that the mean of the network output for a training
point, according to (
17
) is
\langle y\rangle=C\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}z
.
The mean discrepancy therefore becomes

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle=z-\langle y\rangle

(21)

\displaystyle=\big[\mathbb{I}-C\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\big]\,z

\displaystyle=\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}-C\big]\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,z

\displaystyle=\kappa\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,z.

Likewise, the variance of the predictor for a training point is with
(
17
)
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}yy^{\mathrm{T}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=C-C[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}C
,
which can also be written as

\displaystyle\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}yy^{\mathrm{T}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

\displaystyle=C-C\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,C

(22)

\displaystyle=\overbrace{C-\underbrace{[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}}_{\mathbb{I}}\,C}^{=0}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,C

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathbb{I}\,\underbrace{[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]}_{\mathbb{I}}-\kappa^{2}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}

\displaystyle=\kappa\mathbb{I}-\kappa^{2}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}.

So comparing (
21
) and (
22
)
with (
19
) we observe that we get the
bias-variance decomposition (
19
)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\rangle\,\langle\Delta_{\beta}\rangle+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

7
Comparison to numerics

a

b

Figure 1:
Feature learning in a linear single hidden layer network.

Comparison of NNGP (black), Li & Sompolinsky (green) and simulation
(yellow) for mean discrepancies
\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\rangle
.

P=80
samples,
N=100
,
d=200
, Ising task with
p=0.1
, regulator

\kappa=0.01
. Numerical sampling by Langevin dynamics with
1,000,000

training steps,
20,000
samples taken.

8
Deep linear networks

We here reformulate the scaling approach by
[
27
]

in a manner so that one obtains a physical meaning for the introduced
order parameters and that one may formulate the theory by help of
a large deviation approach.

9
Setup deep linear network

We here consider is a network with a
L
hidden layers whose activations
are called
h^{(a)}\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, where
0\leq a\leq L
.

\displaystyle h^{(0)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle V\,x,

(23)

\displaystyle h^{(a)}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle W^{(a)}\,h^{(a-1)}\quad 1\leq a\leq L,

\displaystyle y

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle w^{\mathrm{T}}h^{(L)},

\displaystyle z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle y+\xi,

with Gaussian i.i.d. priors
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{v}/d)

and
W_{ij}^{(a)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
and
readout noise
\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)
.

1
Backward approach

Start with the joint probability of the output and the readout

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\big\{\prod_{a=1}^{L}dh^{(a)}\big\}\,p(y|h^{(L)})\,p(h^{(L)}|h^{(L-1)})\cdots p(h^{(2)}|h^{(1)})p(h^{(1)}|X).

(24)

To follow the backward approach, we first consider the readout conditioned
on the second-last hidden-layer’s activations.

Idea of the approach

We want to know the joint distribution of the
y_{\alpha}
. Consider
the case that we condition on the last hidden activations
h^{(L-1)}
:
The randomness of
y_{\alpha}
has two sources, the readout
w

and the projection from the previous layer
W^{(L)}
. The matrix

h_{\alpha i}^{(L-1)}
is given and fixed. The only appearance of
the index
\alpha
is in
h_{\alpha i}^{(L-1)}
, so the output is
written as

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{ij}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}.

(25)

The first factor plays the role of a projection from the neuron space
to the sample space and is fixed. The two other factors fluctuate.
Since the weights are all drawn i.i.d., we expect that also the product

\sum_{i}W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}
will be i.i.d. and can be approximated
as Gaussian
\eta_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})

but with a variance
Q_{1}
to be determined

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}\,\sqrt{Q_{1}}\,\eta_{j},

(26)

so that the kernel of the network readout would be

\displaystyle\langle y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{g_{w}}{N}Q_{1}\,\sum_{j=1}^{N}h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}h_{\beta j}^{(L-1)}.

Once replaced by a Gaussian, the approach can be iterated to conditioning
on the layer before, because
\eta_{j}
then plays the role of
w_{i}

in the first step. So conditining on the pre-activations of the layer
before, one obtains from (
26
)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}=\sqrt{Q_{1}}\,\sum_{ij}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-2)}W_{ji}^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}\eta_{i},

(27)

which is of the same form as (
25
), only that

w_{i}\to\eta_{i}
and
L\to L-1
. So the same procedure can be
iterated. The need for the formal approach arises, because we need
to know the most likely value for
Q
. So in a way this corresponds
to a variational Gaussian approximation for the quantity
W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}

in the first step and of
W_{ji}^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}\eta_{i}
in the following
step, etc.

Formal approach

To compute the distribution of the readout (
25
)
conditioned on
h^{(L-1)}

\displaystyle p(y|h^{(L-1)})

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)})\big),

we write down its cumulant-generating function

\displaystyle W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)})

\displaystyle=\ln\big\langle\exp\big(\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,\sum_{ij}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}\big)\big\rangle_{W^{(L)},w}.

(28)

Taking the expectation over
W_{ij}^{(L)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)

yields

\displaystyle W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)})=\ln\big\langle\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\sum_{j}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}h_{\beta j}^{(L-1)}\,\|w\|^{2}\big)\big\rangle_{w}.

Taking the disorder average over
w
is the same problem as in the
single hidden layer network (
4
). So one introduces

Q:=\|w\|^{2}
as an auxiliary variable, the length of the readout,
and in the limit of large
N
, one hence obtains

\displaystyle W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)})

\displaystyle\simeq\ln\,\int dQ_{1}\,\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,Q_{1}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\sum_{j}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}h_{\beta j}^{(L-1)}\big)\,e^{-\Gamma(Q_{1})},

(29)

where the rate function is the same as before, given by (
7
).

The latter result may be rewritten with help of a Gaussian
\eta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})

as

\displaystyle W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)})\simeq\ln\,\int dQ_{1}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}}\,\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{j}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-1)}\eta_{j}\big)\big\rangle_{\eta_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})}\,e^{-\Gamma(Q_{1})}.

This step shows that a saddle point equation in
Q_{1}
, fixing the
variance of
\sqrt{Q_{1}}\eta_{j}
, amounts to replacing

\displaystyle\sum_{i}W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{Q_{1}}\,\eta_{j}.

(30)

In particular, the non-Gaussian product of two Gaussian variables
has been replaced by one effective Gaussian variable, as in the conceptual
step (
27
).

Now condition on the pre-activations of one layer before, on
h^{(L-2)}

\displaystyle W_{2}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-2)})

\displaystyle=\ln\,\int dh^{(L-1)}\,\big\langle\exp\big(W_{1}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-1)}\big)\,\delta\big[h^{(L-1)}-W^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}h^{(L-2)}\big]\big\rangle_{W^{(L-1)}}

\displaystyle=\ln\,\int dQ_{1}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}}\,\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{ij}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-2)}W_{ji}^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}\eta_{i}\big)\big\rangle_{\eta_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N}),W^{(L-1)}}\,e^{-\Gamma(Q_{1})},

where we computed the trivial (due to the Dirac
\delta
) integral
over
h^{(L-1)}
.

Comparing the expectation value
\big\langle\exp\big(\ldots\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\sum_{ij}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-2)}W_{ji}^{(L-1)}\eta_{i}\big)\rangle

to the expectation value computed in (
28
), one realizes
that, apart from the factor
\sqrt{Q_{1}}
, the two are identical
if one renames
\eta_{i}\to w_{i}
and
L-1\to L
. The expectation
value over
W^{(L-1)}
hence yields by the same steps as before,
effectively replacing

\displaystyle\sum_{i}W_{ji}^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}\eta_{i}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{Q_{2}}\,\xi_{j}

(31)

in the cumulant-generating function

\displaystyle W_{2}(\tilde{y}|h^{(L-2)})

\displaystyle=\ln\,\int dQ_{1}\,\int dQ_{2}\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sqrt{Q_{1}Q_{2}}\,\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,\sum_{j}\,h_{\alpha j}^{(L-2)}\xi_{j}\big)\big\rangle_{\xi_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{w}}{N})}\,e^{-\Gamma(Q_{1})-\Gamma(Q_{2})}.

We here notice that the meaning of
Q_{2}
is different from the
meaning in the first step: in contrast to the readout layer, where
the order parameter
Q_{1}=\|w\|^{2}
has the physical meaning of
the length of the readout vector, here it has the meaning of the length

\|\eta\|^{2}
, so is also contains the overlap of
\sum_{i}\,W_{ji}^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w_{i}

which is part of the definition of
\eta_{i}
by (
30
),
so its meaning is

\displaystyle Q_{2}

\displaystyle=\|\eta\|^{2}=\frac{1}{Q_{1}}\,\|W^{(L)\mathrm{T}}w\|^{2},

(32)

which contains the projection of the output directions of
W^{(L)}

on the readout vector
w
. In line with (
32
),
the meaning of the order parameter
Q_{3}
is with (
31
)

\displaystyle Q_{3}

\displaystyle=\|\xi\|^{2}=\frac{1}{Q_{2}}\,\|W^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}\eta\|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{Q_{1}Q_{2}}\,\|W^{(L-1)\mathrm{T}}W^{(L)\mathrm{T}}\,w\|^{2}.

Iterating this approach until the first preactivation one has

\displaystyle W_{L}(\tilde{y}|X)

\displaystyle=\ln\,\int d^{L}Q\,\big\langle\exp\big(\sqrt{\prod_{l=1}^{L}Q_{l}}\,\sum_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,\sum_{j}\,x_{\alpha j}\,\eta_{j}\big)\big\rangle_{\eta_{j}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g_{v}}{d})}\,e^{-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Gamma(Q_{l})}

\displaystyle=\ln\,\int d^{L}Q\,\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\prod_{l=1}^{L}Q_{l}\,\sum_{\alpha\beta}\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(xx)}\big)\,e^{-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Gamma(Q_{l})},

where the only difference in the last step is that we take the disorder
average over
V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{v}/d)
.
By induction we also find the meaning of the order parameters as

\displaystyle\prod_{a=1}^{l}Q_{l}

\displaystyle=\|\Big\{\prod_{a=L}^{L-l+2}W^{(a)\mathrm{T}}\Big\}\,w\|^{2}.

(33)

We notice that the result for all
Q_{l}
fixed is a Gaussian distribution
in
y
, so we obtain for (
10
)

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int d^{L}Q\,\mathcal{N}\big(y|0,\prod_{l=1}^{L}Q_{l}\,C^{(xx)}\big)\,e^{-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Gamma(Q_{l})},

\displaystyle\int P^{L}Q\,\exp\big(S(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{L}|z)\big)

\displaystyle:=\int dy\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

(34)

\displaystyle S(Q_{1},\ldots,Q_{L}|z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\big(C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}z-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det\big(C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)-\sum_{l=1}^{L}\Gamma(Q_{l})\Big|_{C=\prod_{l=1}^{L}Q_{l}\,C^{(xx)}}.

2
Saddle point solution

Assuming a symmetric solution is compatible with the result by
[
27
]

(their Eqs. 45 and 46 in the main text and Appendix A statement after
Eq. (A19)). This symmetry is not clear a priori; one might, of course,
in principle find solutions that break this symmetry. One can check
that the symmetric solution is indeed the one with the highest probability,
as shown in (
2
). Assuming
this symmetry

\displaystyle Q^{(a)}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{symmetry}}}{{=}}Q\quad\forall 1\leq a\leq L,

(35)

one obtains
L
identical stationarity conditions (compare text after
their Eq. (A19)) from the saddle point condition from (
34
)

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\frac{\partial S}{\partial Q^{(a)}}\big|_{Q^{(a)}=Q}

(36)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial S}{\partial C}\,\frac{\partial C}{\partial Q^{(a)}}\big|_{Q^{(a)}=Q}-\Gamma^{\prime}(Q)

\displaystyle=Q^{L-1}\Big[\frac{1}{2}\,z^{\mathrm{T}}\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}C^{(xx)}\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}z-\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\,C^{(xx)}\,(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}\Big]\Big|_{C=Q^{L}\,C^{(xx)}}-\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\,\big[1-\frac{g_{w}}{Q}\big],

where we used the equation of state (
8
) for
\Gamma^{\prime}
.
Introducing the short hand

\displaystyle r(Q)

\displaystyle:=-\frac{2Q}{N}\,\frac{\partial S}{\partial C}\,\frac{\partial C}{\partial Q^{(a)}}\big|_{Q^{(a)}=Q}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\,\Big[-z^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}C\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}z+\mathrm{tr}\,C\,(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})^{-1}\Big]\Big|_{C=Q^{L}\,C^{(xx)}}

(
36
) is a self-consistency equation

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=-\frac{N}{2Q}\,r(Q)-\frac{N}{2g_{w}}+\frac{N}{2Q}.

Multiplied by
2Q/N
and rearranged

\displaystyle 1-\frac{Q}{g_{w}}

\displaystyle=r(Q),

(37)

which is identical to Eq. (A46) in
[
27
]
and can be
solved by means of bisectioning.

a

b

Figure 2:
Feature learning in linear
L=4
hidden layer network.

a

Order parameters
\prod_{a=1}^{l}Q_{l}
(
33
) compared
to simulation
\|\prod_{a=L}^{L-l+2}W^{(a)\mathrm{T}}\,w\|^{2}
for

T=50,000
steps. Dashed line is self-consistent solution of (
37
)
for
Q^{\ast}
.
b
Comparison of action
S(Q)
for symmetric
solution (black fat), and symmetry broken states (colors, different
values of the respective other
Q
). Dashed fat: Assumption of
Q=g_{w}

for all but the final layer. Other parameters:
d=200
,
N=100
,

g_{w}=0.7
,
\kappa=0.01
.

10
Extension to the non-linear case

Instead of a linear network, we here want to consider an odd non-linear
activation function
\phi(h)
for the case of a non-linear network

[
73
]
, here applied to the single hidden layer case.
To this end, start from the setup (
1
) and
use (
2
) and (
3
). From step (
4
),
we perform an expansion of the cumulant-generating function

\displaystyle W(\tilde{y}|w)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\tilde{y}_{\alpha}\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}\,\tilde{y}_{\beta}\,\sum_{i}w_{i}^{2}\,+\mathcal{O}\big(\tilde{y}^{4}\big),

(38)

where
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}:=\langle\phi(h_{\alpha})\phi(h_{\beta})\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

and where we used the pairwise independence of the
\phi(h_{\alpha i})

across
i
, so that only diagonal terms appear. This approximation
therefore corresponds to stating that the
\phi_{\alpha}
be jointly
Gaussian distributed; it is not quite the same as stating that
\phi

be the identity. The latter would correspond to taking
C^{(\phi\phi)}=C^{(xx)}

in addition, which is the result for the linear network. So we obtain
the result by
[
73
]
from the action (
12
)
by replacing
C^{(xx)}
by
C^{(\phi\phi)}
, which corresponds to
their action Eq. (33) in
[
73
]
.

1
Recovering the NNGP result

By neglecting the first two terms in (
34
) when searching
for the most probable value of
Q_{l}
, we recover the NNGP limit.

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\overset{!}{=}\Gamma^{\prime}\left(Q_{l}\right)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,Q_{l}=g_{w}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\mathcal{N}\big(y|0,g_{w}^{L}\,C^{(xx)}\big)

This we could have also obtained by using the iterative equation (
26
)

\displaystyle\bar{C}^{(a)}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\langle\phi^{(a-1)}\phi^{(a-1)}\rangle_{h^{(a-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{C}^{(a-1)})}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\langle h^{(a-1)}h^{(a-1)}\rangle_{h^{(a-1)}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,\bar{C}^{(a-1)})}

\displaystyle=g_{w}\,\bar{C}^{(a-1)}\quad\forall\,1\leq a\leq L+1

\Rightarrow\,\bar{C}^{(L)}=g_{w}^{L}\,C^{(xx)}.

11
Kernel adaptation approach

So far we have followed the feature learning approach that results
in a rescaling of the data kernel
C^{(xx)}
by some constant. We
here would like to investigate a complimentary approach, similar to
the one followed by
[
29
]
and by
[
30
]
.
The setup here is again the single hidden layer network as in (
1
).
We use (
12
) as the starting point

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle\simeq\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}C\,\mathcal{N}(y|0,C)\,e^{-\Gamma(C)},

(39)

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{C}}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C-W\,\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)}\big),

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\phi^{\mathrm{T}}\tilde{C}\,\phi\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

If we are interested in the maximum posterior of
C_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle p(C|z)

\displaystyle=\frac{p(z|C)\,p(C)}{p(z)},

(40)

we need to compute the stationary point of the numerator. First note
that we may obtain the statistics of the predictor by using
z
or

\kappa\to K
as sources to differentiate by; one may therefore integrate
over
y
without losing information about its statistics; this implies
that the variance of
y
and the variance
\kappa
of the regularization
simply add up and one has

\displaystyle p(z|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle:=\int p(z,y|C^{(xx)})\,Py

\displaystyle=\int\mathcal{D}C\,\exp\big(S(C|z)\big),

where the action is

\displaystyle S(C|z)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}z-\frac{1}{2}\ln\det(C+\kappa\mathbb{I})-\Gamma(C)+\mathrm{const}.,

(41)

whose stationary point
C^{\ast}
obeys
\partial S/\partial C\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}0

so it fulfills

\displaystyle 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\frac{\partial S}{\partial C_{\alpha\beta}}=\frac{1}{2}\big(\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}\big)_{\alpha\beta}-\frac{1}{2}\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}-

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Gamma(C)}{\partial C_{\alpha\beta}}\quad\forall\alpha,\beta.

(42)

For the rate function holds the equation of state (
14
),
so

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Gamma(C)}{\partial C_{\alpha\beta}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}.

(43)

So (
42
) shows that this fixes the
\arg\sup=\tilde{C}

of the supremum condition in (
10
). By this latter equation
we hence see how the stationary value of
C
not only depends on

p(C)
, but is also shifted due to the presence of the first two
terms (if the first two terms would be absent, we would have
\partial\Gamma(C)/\partial C_{\alpha\beta}=0=\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}
,
which is given by (
15
); this would be the uncorrected
NNGP kernel. We will find that
\tilde{C}\neq 0
produces corrections
to the NNGP kernel.

12
Predictor statistics

The statistics of the predictor follows completely analogous to the
approach of the linear network. Again, the training label
z
may
be used as a source to differentiate by, due to the term
\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa\mathbb{I})\propto\exp\big(-\|z-y\|^{2}/(2\kappa)\big)

in (
2
) as in (
15
), to
obtain the mean of the discrepancy

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\,\ln p(z|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\kappa}\,\big\langle z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}\big\rangle.

Since we compute the integrals over
C
in (
42
) in saddle
point approximation, the action is stationary with regard to
C
,
so

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\,\ln p(z|\kappa\mathbb{I},C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\frac{d}{dz_{\alpha}}\sup_{C}\,S(C|z)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial S}{\partial z_{\alpha}}+\underbrace{\frac{\partial S}{\partial C}}_{=0}\,\frac{\partial C}{\partial z_{\alpha}}\Big|_{C=C^{\ast}}

\displaystyle=-\big[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}z,

which together yields the same expression (
16
)
as before

\displaystyle\langle\Delta\rangle

\displaystyle\equiv z-\langle y\rangle

(44)

\displaystyle=\kappa\,\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}z.

By the same argument, again using that
C^{\ast}
is a stationary
point of the action, we get, completely analogous as (
19
),
by differentiating by
[K]_{\alpha\beta}^{-1}
the matrix of second
moments of the discrepancies

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\,\Big[\,\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}+\mathbb{I}/\kappa-\big[C^{\ast}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big]^{-1}\,\Big]_{\alpha\beta}

(45)

\displaystyle=\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\rangle\,\langle\Delta_{\beta}\rangle+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}y_{\alpha}y_{\beta}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}.

From this result, one obtains an insight for the meaning of the field

\tilde{C}
given by (
42
): We observe that

\displaystyle 2\kappa^{2}\,\tilde{C}

\displaystyle=\langle\Delta\rangle\langle\Delta\rangle^{\mathrm{T}}+\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}yy^{\mathrm{T}}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}-\kappa\mathbb{I}.

So the auxiliary field
2\kappa^{2}\tilde{C}
on the off-diagonal elements
equals to the second moment of the discrepancies; on the diagonals
one needs to subtract the variance of the regulator.

13
Linear single hidden layer network

To get an idea of the meaning of the mean-field equations, we again
consider the case of
\phi(h)=h
the identity function. The rate
function
\Gamma(C)
can then be computed explicitly, because then
(
7
) has the closed form

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,h^{\mathrm{T}}\tilde{C}\,h\big)\Big\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

Writing the Gaussian expectation value over
h
explicitly as

\displaystyle\big\langle\exp\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,h^{\mathrm{T}}\tilde{C}h\big)\big\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(2\pi)^{\frac{P}{2}}\,\det\big(C^{(xx)}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}\,\int\,\prod_{\alpha}dh_{\alpha}\,\exp\big(-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mathrm{T}}\,\big([C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\tilde{C}\big)\,h\big)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\det\big(C^{(xx)}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}\,\det\big([C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\tilde{C}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\det\big(\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,C^{(xx)}\,\tilde{C}\big)^{\frac{1}{2}}}.

Because the latter expression is the normalization condition of a
Gaussian, the argument of the determinant must be symmetric; so are
the matrices
C
and
\tilde{C}
, which implies that they also commute,
because

\displaystyle C\,\tilde{C}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}(C\,\tilde{C})^{\mathrm{T}}

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C^{\mathrm{T}}=\tilde{C}\,C.

(46)

So for
W
we have

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,C^{(xx)}\,\tilde{C}\big).

(47)

The supremum condition in (
10
) then reads as
0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}C-g_{w}\,\big(\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,C^{(xx)}\tilde{C}\big)^{-1}\,C^{(xx)}

(Note: here the order of the factors must be compatible with differentiating

-\partial/\partial\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,\frac{N}{2}\ln\,\det\big([C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\tilde{C}\big)=g_{w}\,\big([C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\tilde{C}\big)^{-1}=g_{w}\,\big[[C^{(xx)}]^{-1}\,\big(\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}C^{(xx)}\tilde{C}\big)\big]^{-1}=g_{w}\,\big(\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}C^{(xx)}\tilde{C}\big){}^{-1}C^{(xx)}
).
Rearranging this as
g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}[C]^{-1}=\mathbb{I}-2\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,C^{(xx)}\tilde{C}

and solving for
C^{(xx)}\tilde{C}=\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\,\big(\mathbb{I}-g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}[C]^{-1}\big)
.
Multiplying from left with
[C^{(xx)}]^{-1}
we get

\displaystyle\tilde{C}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}\,\big([g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-C^{-1}\big).

(48)

So together with (
42
) one has

\displaystyle 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}2\frac{\partial S(C)}{\partial C}=

\displaystyle[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}-[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}-N\,\big([g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-C^{-1}\big),

(49)

which is a quadratic matrix equation in
C^{-1}
. For large
N

and
\kappa=0
this simplifies slightly to

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle N\gg 1}}{{\simeq}}C^{-1}\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,C^{-1}+N\,\big(C^{-1}-[g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}]^{-1}\big).

The term
zz^{\mathrm{T}}
for the case of binary classification is a matrix
with a pair of blocks: diagonal blocks with positive sign, off-diagonal
blocks with negative sign, in the case the the labels of the two classes
are
\pm 1
. The term
[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}=\langle\Delta\rangle\langle\Delta\rangle^{\mathrm{T}}/\kappa^{2}

in (
49
) can be understood as the outer product of
the vectors of mean discrepancies (by comparing to (
44
)).
It shows that the inverse kernel
C^{-1}
gets a correction precisely
into that direction compared to the inverse NNGP kernel
[g_{w}\,C^{(xx)}]^{-1}
,
also also visible in
Figure
3
b,
c.

Even though the corrections in the level of the kernel elements are
small (order
1/N
), a change of
1/N\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}
corresponds to a
change of the action of the kernel on
z
as
1/N\,z\,\|z\|^{2}
,
where the length
\|z\|^{2}=P
, so in the proportional limit
P\propto N
,
this causes an outlier eigenvalue of the kernel matrix of
\mathcal{O}(1)
.
More striking effects of this kernel approach to feature learning
are described in
[
31
]
, demonstrating that the sample complexity
can be changed by these corrections.

a

b

c

d

Figure 3:
Comparison of kernels between theory and simulation. a
Input
kernel
C^{(xx)}
.
b
Kernel
C^{\ast}
of hidden layer
from theory.
c
Scatter plot of input kernel versus hidden
layer kernel (crosses: simulation; dots: theory).
d
Measured
kernel
\frac{g_{w}}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}h_{\alpha i}h_{\beta i}
of
hidden layer from simulation. Other parameters: Single hidden layer
linear network. Regulator
\kappa=0.01
. Ising task with

p=0.1
,
d=200
,
g_{v}=g_{w}=0.5
, number of hidden units
N=100
;

P=80
patterns.

A more stable way of solving this equation is by a gradient ascent
with regard to
C
. To this end we use that
C_{xx}
and
C
as
covariance matrices are both positive semidefinite, so that we compute
instead of the gradient
PS/PC

\displaystyle 2C\,\frac{\partial S(C)}{\partial C}C^{(xx)}

\displaystyle=C\,\Big([C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\,zz^{\mathrm{T}}\,[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}-\frac{1}{2}[C+\kappa\mathbb{I}]^{-1}\Big)\,C^{(xx)}

\displaystyle-N\,\big([g_{w}^{-1}C-C^{(xx)}\big),

which we use as a gradient ascent to obtain a solution shown in
Figure
3
.
The latter equation avoids computing the inverses
C^{-1}
and
C^{(xx)-1}
,
which may not exist; the inverses including the regulator term
\kappa\mathbb{I}

for
\kappa>0
are uncritical.

14
Nonlinear single hidden layer network

To treat the nonlinear case, one may expand the cumulant-generating
function
W
in (
7
) into the first two leading
cumulants to compute
\Gamma(C)
given by (
10
). Since
the cumulants are the coefficients in the Taylor expansion of the
cumulant-generating function, we may approximate the latter by a truncation
of the former. For the specific scaling form of (
7
)

W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})=N\,\lambda\big(\tilde{Q}/N)
a truncation at second order
amounts to neglecting terms of order
N^{-2}
. The cumulant expansion
of
W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})
reads (in Einstein’s summation convention, summing
over repeated indices on the right)

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,g_{w}\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}+\frac{g_{w}^{2}}{2N}\,\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\,C_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}\,\tilde{C}_{\gamma\delta}+\mathcal{O}(N^{-2})

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}

\displaystyle=\big\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\big\rangle_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}\,,

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}

\displaystyle=\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta},\phi_{\gamma}\phi_{\delta}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}_{h\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}\,,

where
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta},\phi_{\gamma}\phi_{\delta}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\,\phi_{\gamma}\phi_{\delta}\rangle-\langle\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}\rangle\,\langle\phi_{\gamma}\phi_{\delta}\rangle

is the second order cumulant of
\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}
. The
supremum condition for
\Gamma
then yields a linear equation in

\tilde{C}

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}C_{\alpha\beta}-g_{w}\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}-\frac{g_{w}^{2}}{N}\,C_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}\,\tilde{C}_{\gamma\delta}\,.

(50)

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{g_{w}^{2}}\,[C^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}]_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{-1}\,\big(C_{\gamma\delta}-g_{w}\,C_{\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi)}\big)

Since the cumulant expansion to second order keeps Gaussian fluctuations
of
\phi_{\alpha}\phi_{\beta}
, at this order the replacement of

p(C)
by
e^{\Gamma(C)}
is an exact operation; this is so because
integrating over
\tilde{C}
in (
8
) and taking the supremum
yields the same expression for a Gaussian, namely in both cases one
obtains (apart from algebraic prefactors)

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\tilde{C}}\,\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}C_{\alpha\beta}-W\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(xx)}\big).

(51)

\displaystyle=\frac{N}{2}\,\big[C_{\alpha\beta}-g_{w}\,C_{\alpha\beta}^{(\phi\phi)}\big]\,[C^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}]_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{-1}\,\big[C_{\gamma\delta}-g_{w}\,C_{\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi)}\big],

where
[C^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}]^{-1}
is the inverse appearing from
solving (
50
). This shows that
C

here follows a quadratic potential which may be thought of as a superposition
of Gaussian kernels, where each kernel appears with the Gaussian measure

e^{-\Gamma(C)}
.

The maximum a posteriori estimate for
C
thus obeys with (
42
)
and with (
50
), where
\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}

is the solution of (
50
), the derivative
of (
51
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Gamma(C)}{\partial C_{\alpha\beta}}=

\displaystyle\tilde{C}_{\alpha\beta}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle(\ref{eq:stat_condition_for_tilC})}}{{=}}\frac{N}{g_{w}^{2}}\,[C^{(\phi\phi,\phi\phi)}]_{\alpha\beta,\gamma\delta}^{-1}\,\big[C_{\gamma\delta}-g_{w}\,C_{\gamma\delta}^{(\phi\phi)}\big].

15
Keypoints

In summary:

Feature learning

•

The NNGP kernel only depends on the statistics of the inputs, but
not on the joint statistics of inputs and training labels.

•

One needs to go beyond the NNGP limit and consider a setting where

P/N\neq 0
to uncover feature learning.

•

There are different approximations to go beyond the NNGP limit in
linear DNNs. Two of them include the kernel scaling approach and the
kernel adaptation approach for large networks.

•

For linear networks, the kernel gets rescaled by a factor
Q
when
moving across layers, much like the NNGP limit where this factor
Q

is the variance of the weight prior
g_{w}
; the difference is that

Q
is different from
g_{w}
due to the training process.

•

One can obtain the predictor statistics, such as the mean of the deviations
from the true training label
z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}
and the MSE
training error, from the conjugate fields
\tilde{C}
in the presented
field theoretic formalism.

16
Appendix: Large deviation principle for Wishart matrices

The presented kernel adaptation approach relies on the approximation
of the distribution
p(C)
for the matrix
C\in\mathbb{R}^{P\times P}
.
We here employed a large deviation approach. In general one needs
to be careful when relying on large deviation results for high-dimensional
random variables. This can be easiest appreciated on the current example
of a single hidden later linear network. The prior distribution of
the matrix

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\,h_{\alpha i}h_{\beta i}

(52)

defined in (
5
), for
\phi(h)=h
is known as
the Wishart distribution
[
74
]
, where
h_{\alpha i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.P. in }i}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(0)})

.

The probability of the matrix is known exactly

\displaystyle-\ln p(C)/N

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\,[C^{(0)}]^{-1}C-\frac{1-\frac{P-1}{N}}{2}\,\ln\det\big(C\big)+\mathrm{const}.

(53)

We may compare this expression to the result obtained from the rate
function, which is obtained from (
10
) with (
47
),
and the supremum condition on
\tilde{C}
which yields (
48
)

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tilde{C}}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\tilde{C}^{\mathrm{T}}C-W\,\big(\tilde{C}|C^{(0)}\big)\,,

\displaystyle W(\tilde{C}|C^{(0)})=

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(\mathbb{I}-\frac{2}{N}\,C^{(0)}\,\tilde{C}\big)\,,

\displaystyle\tilde{C}=

\displaystyle\frac{N}{2}\,\big([C^{(0)}]^{-1}-C^{-1}\big)\,,

which yields the final expression

\displaystyle\Gamma(C)/N

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\,[C^{(0)}]^{-1}\,C-\frac{1}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(C\big)+\mathrm{const}.

(54)

Comparing the expressions (
53
) and (
54
),
we notice that the former differs by the factor
\frac{1-\frac{P-1}{N}}{2}

from the latter, where it is
\frac{1}{2}
. So the two distributions
are close if
P\ll N
.

In the proportional limit
P\propto N
that we investigate for feature
learning, the assumption that
P\ll N
is not necessarily justified.
In a typical setting of a neuronal network, the matrix
C^{(0)}=XX^{\mathrm{T}}

with
X\in\mathbb{R}^{P\times d}
has a rank that is
\mathrm{rk}(C^{(0)})=\min(P,d)
.
This implies for its spectrum that only
\min(P,d)
of its eigenvalues
are non-zero. If we are interested in the limit
P\to\infty
, we
get
\min(P,d)=d
, so we can expand
C^{(0)}
into the set of the

d
eigenvectors
u_{1\leq\mu\leq d}

\displaystyle C^{(0)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu=1}^{d}\,\lambda_{\mu}\,u_{\mu}u_{\mu}^{\mathrm{T}}\,,

where
\lambda_{\mu}
are the corresponding eigenvalues. We may write
the vectors
h
in this basis
\{u_{\alpha}\}
, too,

\displaystyle h_{\alpha i}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu=1}^{d}\sqrt{\lambda_{\mu}}\,z_{\mu,i}\,u_{\alpha\mu},

(55)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{d\times N}\ni z_{\mu,i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,1)\,,

so that
C_{\alpha\beta}^{(0)}\,\delta_{ij}=\langle h_{\alpha i}h_{\beta j}\rangle
,
as it should and the
h
are obviously Gaussian.

The Wishart matrix is defined as (
52
). Expressing
the
h_{\alpha i}
as linear combinations (
55
),
we notice that only a finite number
d\times d
of projectors
\{u_{\mu}u_{\nu}^{\mathrm{T}}\}_{1\leq\mu,\nu\leq d}

appears in its expansion

\displaystyle C_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{d}\sqrt{\lambda_{\mu}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\nu}}\,\sum_{i=1}^{N}z_{\mu,i}z_{\nu,i}\,u_{\alpha\mu}u_{\beta\nu}

\displaystyle=:\sum_{\mu,\nu=1}^{d}\sqrt{\lambda_{\mu}}\sqrt{\lambda_{\nu}}\,\bar{K}_{\mu\nu}\,u_{\alpha\mu}u_{\beta\nu}\,.

(56)

The
\bar{K}_{\mu\nu}:=\sum_{i=1}^{N}z_{\mu,i}z_{\nu,i}
are random
and follow a Wishart distribution themselves, namely (cf. (
53
))

\displaystyle-\ln\,p(\bar{K})/N

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{K}-\frac{1-\frac{d-1}{N}}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(\bar{K}\big)+\mathrm{const}\,.

In the limit
P,N\to\infty
, while
d
stays constant, we may replace
the density with

\displaystyle-\ln\,p(\bar{K})/N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d\ll N}}{{\simeq}}\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{K}-\frac{1}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(\bar{K}\big)+\mathcal{O}(P/N)+\mathrm{const}

The density for the matrix given by (
56
)
written as
C=U\sqrt{\Lambda}\,\bar{K}\,\sqrt{\Lambda}\,U^{\mathrm{T}}
with
diagonal matrix
\sqrt{\Lambda}=\mathrm{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_{1}},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_{P}})

and unitary matrix
U=(u_{1},\ldots,u_{P})
,
U^{\mathrm{T}}U=\mathbf{I}
is then
obtained with
\bar{K}=\sqrt{\Lambda}^{-1}U^{\mathrm{T}}\,C\,U\,\sqrt{\Lambda}^{-1}

and
\mathrm{tr}\,\bar{K}=\mathrm{tr}\,U\,\Lambda^{-1}\,U^{\mathrm{T}}\,C=\mathrm{tr}\,[C^{(0)}]^{-1}\,C

(exploiting the cyclic invariance of the trace) as well as
\det\big(\bar{K}\big)=\det\big(\sqrt{\Lambda}^{-1}U^{\mathrm{T}}\,C\,U\,\sqrt{\Lambda}^{-1}\big)=\det\big([C^{(0)}]^{-1}\big)\,\det\big(C\big)

(exploiting that
\det(AB)=\det(A)\det(B)
as well as
\det(U)=1

and
\det(\Lambda)=\det(C^{(0)}
)) as

\displaystyle-\ln p(C)/N

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d\ll N}}{{\simeq}}\frac{1}{2}\,\mathrm{tr}\,[C^{(0)}]^{-1}\,C-\frac{1}{2}\,\ln\det\,\big(C\big)+\mathrm{const}.\,,

which is the same result as the rate function (
54
).
In the last step we suppressed additional constant terms that come
from the substitution of the random variable
\bar{K}\to C
.

This result shows that, in the proportional limit where
P\propto N\to\infty
,
but the dimension of the data vectors
d
stays constant, the large
deviation approach is justified.

17
Appendix: Expectation values of error functions

One often considers as an activation function a sigmoidal function,
for example the error function
\phi(x)=\mathrm{erf}(x)
. This can also be
written with

\displaystyle g(x)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,e^{-\frac{x^{2}}{2}},

\displaystyle G(x)

\displaystyle:=\int_{-\infty}^{x}g(t)\,dt

as

\displaystyle\phi(x)

\displaystyle=\mathrm{erf}(x)

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\sqrt{\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{x}\,e^{-t^{2}}\,Pt

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle s=\sqrt{2}t}}{{=}}\sqrt{\frac{2}{\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}\,x}\,e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle=2\,\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\,\int_{0}^{\sqrt{2}\,x}\,e^{-\frac{s^{2}}{2}}\,ds

\displaystyle=2\,\big(G(\sqrt{2}\,x)-G(0)\big)

\displaystyle=2\,G(\sqrt{2}\,x)-1.

We need to evaluate expectation values of
\phi
and
\phi^{2}

with regard to Gaussian distributed arguments, such as

\displaystyle\langle\phi(x)\rangle_{x\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu,\sigma^{2})}

\displaystyle=2\,\langle G(y)\rangle_{\mathcal{N}(M,\Sigma)}-1

(57)

\displaystyle=2\,G(\frac{M}{\sqrt{1+\Sigma}})-1

\displaystyle=\phi(\frac{M}{\sqrt{1+\Sigma}}/\sqrt{2})

\displaystyle=\phi(\frac{\mu}{\sqrt{1+2\sigma^{2}}}),

where
M=\sqrt{2}\,\mu
and
\Sigma=2\sigma^{2}
. The last result
follows from
[
75
]
; one may also see this by
writing
\mathrm{erf}
as the expectation of the Heaviside function under
a unit variance Gauss, so that variances
1
and
\Sigma
add up.

Likewise, we need

\displaystyle\langle\phi(x)\phi(y)\rangle_{x,y\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg[\left(\begin{array}[]{c}\mu_{1}\\
\mu_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\sigma_{11}^{2}&\sigma_{12}^{2}\\
\sigma_{21}^{2}&\sigma_{22}^{2}\end{array}\right)\Bigg]}

\displaystyle=\langle(2G(\sqrt{2}\,x)-1)(2G(\sqrt{2}\,y)-1)\rangle

\displaystyle=4\,\langle G(x^{\prime})G(y^{\prime})\rangle-2\langle G(x^{\prime})\rangle-2\langle G(y^{\prime})\rangle+1

\displaystyle=4\,\langle G(x^{\prime})G(y^{\prime})\rangle-2G\big(\frac{M_{1}}{\sqrt{1+\Sigma_{11}}}\big)-2G\big(\frac{M_{2}}{\sqrt{1+\Sigma_{22}}}\big)+1,

\displaystyle=4\,\langle G(x^{\prime})G(y^{\prime})\rangle-\phi(\frac{\mu_{1}}{\sqrt{1+2\sigma_{11}^{2}}})-\phi(\frac{\mu_{2}}{\sqrt{1+2\sigma_{22}^{2}}})-1,

where
x^{\prime},y^{\prime}\sim\mathcal{N}\Bigg[\left(\begin{array}[]{c}M_{1}\\
M_{2}\end{array}\right),\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}\Sigma_{11}&\Sigma_{12}\\
\Sigma_{21}&\Sigma_{22}\end{array}\right)\Bigg]
. We decompose the bivariate Gauss into two uncorrelated parts driven
by a pair of uncorrelated unit variance Gaussian variables
z_{1}

and
z_{2}
, respectively, and a joint Gaussian variable
z
, writing
them as

\displaystyle x^{\prime}

\displaystyle=M_{1}+A\,z_{1}+\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z

\displaystyle y^{\prime}

\displaystyle=M_{2}+B\,z_{2}+\mathrm{sgn(\Sigma_{12})\,}\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z.

This ensures that
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x^{\prime}y^{\prime}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=\Sigma_{12}

and
\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x^{\prime 2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=A^{2}+|\Sigma_{12}|
as well as

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}y^{\prime 2}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}=B^{2}+|\Sigma_{12}|
, so

\displaystyle A

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Sigma_{11}-|\Sigma_{12}|},

\displaystyle B

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\Sigma_{22}-|\Sigma_{12}|}.

Then we may write with (
57
)

\displaystyle\langle G(M_{1}+A\,z_{1}+\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z)\rangle_{z_{1}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}

\displaystyle=G(\frac{M_{1}+\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z}{\sqrt{1+A^{2}}})

\displaystyle\langle G(M_{2}+B\,z_{2}+\mathrm{sgn}(\Sigma_{12})\,\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z)\rangle_{z_{2}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,1)}

\displaystyle=G(\frac{M_{2}+\mathrm{sgn}(\Sigma_{12})\,\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z}{\sqrt{1+B^{2}}})

\displaystyle 4\,\langle G(x^{\prime})G(y^{\prime})\rangle

\displaystyle=4\,\int g(z)\,G(\frac{M_{1}+\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z}{\sqrt{1+A^{2}}})\,G(\frac{M_{2}+\mathrm{sgn}(\Sigma_{12})\,\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}\,z}{\sqrt{1+B^{2}}})\,dz,

so that we may use the idea by
[
75
]
, which
employs the result of
[
76
, 20,010.4]

\displaystyle 4\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(a+b\,z)\,G(c+d\,z)g(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=2\,G(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}})+2\,G(\frac{c}{\sqrt{1+d^{2}}})

(62)

\displaystyle-4\,T(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},\frac{c+cb^{2}-abd}{a\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}})

\displaystyle-4\,T(\frac{c}{\sqrt{1+P^{2}}},\frac{a+ad^{2}-bcd}{c\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}})

\displaystyle-\begin{cases}0&\text{if }ac>0\text{ or if }ac=0\text{ and }a\text{ or }c>0\\
2&\text{if }ac<0\text{ or if }ac=0\text{ and }a\text{ or }c<0\end{cases}

where
a=\frac{M_{1}}{\sqrt{1+A^{2}}}
,
c=\frac{N_{2}}{\sqrt{1+B^{2}}}

,
b=\frac{\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}}{\sqrt{1+A^{2}}}
,
P=\frac{sgn(\Sigma_{12})\,\sqrt{|\Sigma_{12}|}}{\sqrt{1+B^{2}}}
,
and

\displaystyle T(h,y)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\int_{0}^{y}dx\,\frac{e^{-\frac{1}{2}h^{2}(1+x^{2})}}{1+x^{2}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle h=0}}{{=}}\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\arctan\big(y\big).

The case
N_{1}=0\Rightarrow a=0
and
c\neq 0
therefore leads to

\displaystyle 4\,T(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},\frac{c+cb^{2}-abd}{a\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}}\big)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle a\searrow 0}}{{=}}4\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\arctan\big(y\big)\big|_{y\to\frac{c+cb^{2}}{a\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}}}

\displaystyle=4\,\frac{1}{2\pi}\,\begin{cases}\frac{\pi}{2}&a\,c>0\\
-\frac{\pi}{2}&a\,c<0\end{cases},

so

\displaystyle 4\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(b\,z)\,G(c+d\,z)g(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=2\,G(\frac{c}{\sqrt{1+d^{2}}})

(63)

\displaystyle-4\,T(\frac{c}{\sqrt{1+d^{2}}},\frac{-bd}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}}).

and likewise for the other term if
M_{2}=0\Rightarrow c=0
and
M_{1}\neq 0

\displaystyle 4\,\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}G(a+b\,z)\,G(d\,z)g(z)\,dz

\displaystyle=2\,G(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}})

\displaystyle-4\,T(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}},\frac{-bd}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}+d^{2}}}).

Finally, if
N_{1}=N_{2}=0\Rightarrow a=c=0

one has
[
65
]

\displaystyle\langle\phi\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{2}{\pi}\,\arcsin\big(\frac{\Sigma_{12}}{\sqrt{(1+\Sigma_{11})(1+\Sigma_{22}})}\big).

(64)

The terms in the first line are
2G(\frac{a}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}})=\phi(\frac{a/\sqrt{2}}{\sqrt{1+b^{2}}})+1
.

18
Exercises

a) Feature learning in linear networks

a.1) Stochastic gradient descent

In this exercise we want to compare the results from the feature learning
theory with the numerical results obtained with network training.
We hence need to implement a version of network training which corresponds
to the Bayesian approach we have been studying so far. Ultimately
we have a network

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=Vx_{\alpha}\quad V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{v}/d)

(65)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{T}}h_{\alpha}\quad w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)

(66)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}\quad\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)

(67)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}(y_{\alpha}-z_{\alpha})^{2}

(68)

with
x_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},h_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R}^{N},y_{\alpha}\in\mathbb{R},V\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times d},W\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times N}

and squared error loss terms and the training data set
\mathcal{P}=(x_{\alpha},y_{\alpha})
,

\alpha=1,\ldots,P
. We want to sample from the distribution

p(W,V|\mathcal{P})\propto\exp\left(-L(\mathcal{P},W,V)-\frac{N}{2g_{w}}\|w\|^{2}-\frac{d}{2g_{V}}\mathrm{tr}V^{\mathrm{T}}V\right)\quad.

(69)

As we saw during the lecture we can sample from this distribution
by Langevin gradient descent, a specific implementation of stochastic
gradient descent. First consider the simplified example with
w(t)\in\mathbb{R}

dw(t)=-\gamma w(t)dt+dB(t)

(70)

with the Gaussian noise
\langle dB(t)dB(t^{\prime})\rangle=P\delta_{ij}\delta\left(t-t^{\prime}\right)dt
.
Calculate the equilibrium distribution of
w
. Calculate the time
discrete version of the equation using the Ito convention: see (
2
)
in the script. Similarly we want to use the following stochastic
differential equation to sample from
p(W,V|\mathcal{P})
via

\displaystyle d\Theta

\displaystyle=(-\gamma\Theta-\nabla_{\Theta}L)\,dt+dB\quad,\quad\Theta=\{W,V\}

(71)

\displaystyle\langle dB_{i}(t)dB_{j}(s)\rangle

\displaystyle=P\delta_{ij}\delta(t-s)dt\quad.

(72)

How do we need to choose
\gamma
and
P
in our case above? Choose

P
in such a way that we use the same noise distribution for
W,V
.
. Use your results and fill the gaps denoted with TODO in the corresponding
Python program.

a.2) Theoretical results

We also want to compare the numerics to the theory. In particular
we want to compare the length of the trained readout weights
Q=\|w\|^{2}

to the value predicted by the feature learning theory for
Q
. For
this we need to solve

0=\frac{\partial S}{\partial Q}=\frac{1}{2}z^{\mathrm{T}}\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}C^{(xx)}\,\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}z-\mathrm{tr}\,C^{(xx)}\big(QC^{(xx)}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}-\frac{N}{2}\big(\frac{1}{g_{w}}-\frac{1}{Q}\big)

(73)

for
Q
. We find the root of this equation using the so called bisection
method. Follow the instructions in the program in the method “return_Q_Sompolinsky_biscection”.

a.3) Comparison of numerics and theory

We want you to plot the theoretical value of
Q
and numerical values
for
\|w\|^{2}
. In order to do this we need to make sure that the
stochastic differential equations above actually sample from the distribution

p(W,V|\mathcal{P})
. We do this by training the network. We
set a so called burn-in-time (T0) which is the amount of gradient
descent steps we wait, before we start sampling. How would you set
this burn-in-time and how would you see whether the stochastic differential
equations converged to
p(W,V|\mathcal{P})
? After the burn-in-time
we sample a certain number (sample_amount) of weight configurations
from
p(W,V|\mathcal{P})
. However, we wait some amount of time
(delta_T) between the samples. Why do we do this and how would you
set this?

Using the program produce a plot where
Q
is plotted against
N\in[10^{1},\ldots,10^{4}]

. First for
P=10
data points: How do the empirical results match
the theory ? Where do you see deviations from the theory and why?
Produce plots for
P=4,10,20
. If there are deviations, do they depend
on
P
? Produce plots for different values of
g_{w}
. Does this
change the match between theory and empirical results? If so why?

a.3) Notes regarding the program

The program utilizes JAX in order to be efficient and fast. If you
want to debug and e.g. check the numerical values of the parameters
you can turn jax arrays to numpy arrays by numpy.array(…). Some
hints

•

checks parts of the program successively

•

save your theory and data traces and do pretty plots afterwards using
the saved traces

b) Most likely network output

Consider is a network with a single hidden layer defined by (
1
),
but with a different scaling (also called “mean-field scaling”)
in the readout weights
w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N^{2})
.
Follow the derivation in
Section
3
to
obtain

\displaystyle p(z,y|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\big\langle\,\prod_{\alpha=1}^{P}\delta\,\big[y_{\alpha}-\sum_{i=1}^{N}w_{i}\,\phi(h_{\alpha i})\big]\big\rangle_{w_{i},h_{\alpha i}}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{N}(z|y,\kappa)\,\int\mathcal{D}\tilde{y}\,\big\langle\exp\big(-\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y+W(\tilde{y}|C^{(xx)})\big)\big\rangle,

where the cumulant-generating function is

\displaystyle W(\tilde{y}|C^{(xx)})

\displaystyle=N\,\ln\,\Big\langle\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha\beta}\frac{\tilde{y}_{\alpha}}{N}\frac{\tilde{y}_{\beta}}{N}g_{w}\,\phi(h_{\alpha})\phi(h_{\beta})\big)\Big\rangle_{h_{\alpha}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,C^{(xx)})}.

Compute
p(z,y|C^{(xx)})
for large
N
by introducing the rate
function

\displaystyle\Gamma(y)

\displaystyle:=\sup_{\tilde{y}}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}y-W(\tilde{y}|C^{(xx)}).

(74)

Use this approximation to obtain a set of equations for the most likely
network output
y^{\ast}
given the training labels by considering

0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{\alpha}}\,\ln\,p(z,y|C^{(xx)})
.
Show that this condition relates the auxiliary fields
\tilde{y}
and the
discrepancies as

\displaystyle\tilde{y}_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta_{\alpha}}{\kappa}\equiv\frac{z_{\alpha}-y_{\alpha}}{\kappa}.

Obtain a second set of equations for
\tilde{y}
from the supremum condition
in (
74
); show that the result is

\displaystyle 0\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}-g_{w}\,\sum_{\beta}\big[\phi(h_{\alpha})\phi(h_{\beta})\big]_{h}\frac{\tilde{y}_{\beta}}{N},

(75)

where the measure
[\ldots]_{h}
is given by

\displaystyle\big[\ldots\big]_{h}:=

\displaystyle\frac{\int dh\,\ldots\,\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{g_{w}}{N^{2}}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h)\phi(h)\tilde{y}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mathrm{T}}[C^{(xx)}]^{-1}h\big)}{\int dh\,\exp\big(\frac{1}{2}\frac{g_{w}}{N^{2}}\,\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}\phi(h)\phi(h)\tilde{y}-\frac{1}{2}h^{\mathrm{T}}[C^{(xx)}]^{-1}h\big)}.

(76)

Show that the most likely value of the discrepancy is

\displaystyle\Delta_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=\kappa\,\big(\frac{g_{w}}{N}\,\big[\phi(h)\phi(h)^{\mathrm{T}}\big]_{h}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\big)^{-1}\,z.

Show that for the case of a linear activation function
\phi(h)=h

one has

\displaystyle W(\tilde{y}):=

\displaystyle-\frac{N}{2}\,\ln\det\big(\big[[C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-g_{w}\frac{\tilde{y}}{N}\frac{\tilde{y}^{\mathrm{T}}}{N}\big]\big)-\frac{N}{2}\ln\det(C^{(xx)})

and derive the expression corresponding to (
75
)
from it. Show that for the most likely value of the discrepancies
holds

\displaystyle\Big[\,\big\{[g_{w}C^{(xx)}]^{-1}-\frac{\Delta}{\kappa N}\frac{\Delta}{\kappa N}^{\mathrm{T}}\big\}^{-1}+\kappa\,\mathbb{I}\Big]\,\frac{\Delta}{\kappa N}

\displaystyle=z.

Compare this to the result in NNGP (in standard scaling
w_{i}\sim\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)
)

\Delta=\kappa\,(g_{w}C^{(xx)}+\kappa\,\mathbb{I})^{-1}z
written as
(g_{w}C^{(xx)}+\kappa\,\mathbb{I})\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}=z
:
which shows that the rank-one matrix
\propto\Delta\Delta^{\mathrm{T}}
modifies
the inverse of the kernel
g_{w}C^{(xx)}
.

c) Product of two random matrices

In the feature learning approach, we have replaced a product of a
random matrix and a random vector by a random Gaussian vector, see
equation (
26
). We here want to check the
validity of this approximation. Consider the product of two random
matrices, which may resemble the connections between adjacent layers

\displaystyle Y_{ij}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{l}W_{il}V_{lj},

(77)

\displaystyle\mathbb{R}^{N\times N}\ni W_{il},V_{lj}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g}{N}).

Compute the cumulant-generating function of the product (
77
)
and show that to leading order in
N
the result is

\displaystyle Y_{ij}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\frac{g^{2}}{N}).

You may use that
\ln\det(A)=\mathrm{tr}\ln(A)
(which can be shown by moving
into the eigenbasis of
A
), and the expansion
\mathrm{tr}\,\ln\big(\mathbb{I}+B)=\mathrm{tr}\,B+\mathcal{O}(B^{2})
.

d) Numerics: Predictor statistics and kernels

We have already seen that we can utilize the theory in Li & Sompolinksy
for linear networks to obtain the norm of the weight vector after
training. We want to show that we may also obtain results for the
training loss. Check numerically, whether the expressions from the
main text (
20
) hold

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{tr}\left(\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\beta}\rangle\right)

\displaystyle\langle\Delta_{\alpha}\Delta_{\beta}\rangle

\displaystyle=\kappa^{2}\left(\left[C^{*}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\right]^{-1}zz^{\top}\left[C^{*}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\right]^{-1}+\frac{\mathbb{I}}{\kappa}-\left[C^{*}+\kappa\mathbb{I}\right]^{-1}\right)

for a linear network for the form

\displaystyle h_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=Vx_{\alpha}\quad V_{ij}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{v}/d)

(78)

\displaystyle y_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=w^{\mathrm{T}}h_{\alpha}\quad w_{i}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,g_{w}/N)

(79)

\displaystyle z_{\alpha}

\displaystyle=y_{\alpha}+\xi_{\alpha}\quad\xi_{\alpha}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{i.i.d.}}}{{\sim}}\mathcal{N}(0,\kappa)

(80)

\displaystyle L

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\alpha}(y_{\alpha}-z_{\alpha})^{2}

(81)

with
C^{*}=QC^{xx}
. Plot the training loss in the feature learning
approximation and the NNGP approximation as a function of
g_{w}

and compare to numerical results. Choose settings with
P=10
. First
set
g_{w}=1,\kappa=0.01
and plot the training loss as a function
of the amount of training data
N=10^{1},\ldots,10^{4}
. Are your
results consistent with your expectations? Now keep
N=200
,
P=10
,

\kappa=0.01
and scan the loss over the values
g_{w}=0.01,\ldots,0.4
.
Compare the numerical results to the NGGP and the feature learning
results. What is a lower bound for
\mathcal{L}
(aka. the lowest
value of
\mathcal{L}
that you would expect to see?)

We now want to check, whether the network kernel is in fact only scaled
by a factor
Q
, as predicted by the theory of Li and Sompolinksy.
The program already records the empirical kernel as “C_phi_phi”.
Use a scatter plot of the kernel elements of the theoretically predicted
kernel and the empirical kernel to determine, whether the theory predicts
the correct kernel. Hint: Use the autocorrelation of the loss (which
can be computed by the new method ’compute_acf’) to determine, whether
your sampling distance is appropriate.

d) Legendre transform of a Gaussian

Consider the cumulant generating function of a Gaussian

\displaystyle W(j)

\displaystyle=j^{\mathrm{T}}\kappa_{1}+\frac{1}{2}j^{\mathrm{T}}\kappa_{2}j\quad j\in\mathbb{R}^{d}

(82)

\displaystyle j^{\top}\kappa_{1}

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha=1}^{d}j_{\alpha}\kappa_{1\alpha}

(83)

\displaystyle j^{\mathrm{T}}\kappa_{2}j

\displaystyle:=\sum_{\alpha,\beta=1}^{d}j_{\alpha}\kappa_{2\alpha\beta}j_{\beta}

(84)

with a symmetric, invertible, positive definite covariance matrix

\kappa_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d}
and the mean
\kappa_{1}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Show that the effective action

\Gamma(x)=\sup_{j}\left\{j^{\mathrm{T}}x-W(j)\right\}

(85)

is also quadratic. Next show that in general the second derivative
of the cumulant generating function and the second derivative of the
effective action are inverse to each other.

\mathbb{I}=W^{(2)}\Gamma^{(2)}.

To this end, start with
\delta_{ki}=\frac{\partial j_{i}}{\partial j_{k}}

and use the equation of state
j_{i}=\frac{\partial\Gamma}{\partial x_{i}}
.
What is the property of the Legendre transform that you are exploiting
to achieve this? Does this relation hold for general
W,\Gamma
?

Chapter 9
Nomenclature

We here adapt the nomenclature from the book by Kleinert on path integrals

[
64
]
. We denote as
x
our ordinary random variable
or dynamical variable, depending on the system. Further we use

•

p(x)
probability distribution

•

\langle x^{n}\rangle

n
-th moment

•

\mathopen{\hbox{${\langle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\langle}$}}x^{n}\mathclose{\hbox{${\rangle}$}\kern-1.94444pt\hbox{${\rangle}$}}

n
-th cumulant

•

x^{\mathrm{T}}y=\sum_{i}x_{i}y_{i}
scalar product

•

\mathcal{N}(x|a,b)=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi b}}\,\exp\left(-\frac{1}{2}\frac{(x-a)^{2}}{b}\right)

Gaussian distribution

•

S(x)\propto\ln\,p(x)
action

•

-\frac{1}{2}x^{\mathrm{T}}Ax
quadratic action

•

X\in\mathbb{R}^{P\times N}
data matrix

•

y
labels

•

f
network output

•

w
readout weights

•

W^{(a)}
network weights of layer
a

•

h
hidden representation, fields, synaptic input

•

\phi(\circ)
(non-linear) activation function

•

C
kernel, covariance matrix between hidden representations

•

Z(j)=\langle\exp(j^{\mathrm{T}}x)\rangle
moment generating function[al]
(MGF) or partition function

•

\mathcal{W}(j)=\ln\,Z(j)
cumulant generating function[al] (CGF)
or generating function of connected diagrams; (Helmholtz) free energy

•

\Gamma[x^{\ast}]=\sup_{j}\,j^{\mathrm{T}}x^{\ast}-\mathcal{W}[j]
generating
function[al]; Gibbs free energy
